PEBC Qualifying Exam Part I Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Incision and drainage is the main treatment, and antibiotics are usually not needed here): A small, uncomplicated abscess in an otherwise healthy person is treated by incision and drainage. Antibiotics penetrate poorly into an undrained collection and add little when there are no systemic signs or risk factors. Squeezing at home can push bacteria deeper into the tissue.
  2. The correct answer is C (Square root of (160 x 90 / 3600), which equals 2.0 m2): The Mosteller formula is the square root of height in centimetres times weight in kilograms divided by 3600. Here 160 x 90 = 14,400, divided by 3600 is 4.0, and the square root is 2.0 m2. Leaving out the square root gives 4.0 m2, which would double the dose, and weight over height squared is the body mass index, not a surface area.
  3. The correct answer is B (Migraine with aura raises stroke risk, so an estrogen-free method should be recommended): Migraine with aura is a contraindication to estrogen-containing contraceptives because of an increased risk of ischemic stroke. Being a nonsmoker does not remove that risk, and lowering the estrogen dose does not make the method safe. She should be referred for a progestin-only or nonhormonal alternative.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reduce by about 25 percent every two weeks, taking smaller steps near the end): Canadian deprescribing guidance supports a gradual taper, commonly about 25 percent of the dose every two weeks with smaller steps near the end, slowed if withdrawal appears. Rapid reductions or on-off cycling provoke rebound anxiety and withdrawal. Buspirone takes weeks to work and does not prevent benzodiazepine withdrawal.
  5. The correct answer is D (Assign a date no later than the earliest drug expiry and within stability data limits): Repackaged drugs are protected for a shorter period than the original container, so the beyond use date cannot exceed the earliest expiry of any contents. It also must respect available stability data for repackaging, which is often shorter than the expiry. Using a fixed one year date would let some tablets be used after they expire.
  6. The correct answer is A (Decline an early refill, discuss her use with her, and contact the prescriber about the loss): Repeated early requests for a benzodiazepine call for a professional assessment of use, not an automatic refill or refusal. Talking with TL and the prescriber addresses safety and possible misuse while keeping her in care. Cutting her off abruptly can cause withdrawal.
  7. The correct answer is A (Mesalamine 1 g suppository inserted rectally once daily at bedtime, before sleep): Proctitis is treated first with a topical 5-ASA, and a suppository delivers drug directly to the rectal mucosa. Systemic corticosteroids and thiopurines are reserved for moderate to severe disease or failure of 5-ASA. Oral budesonide is not first-line for isolated proctitis, which responds best to topical 5-ASA, and it is not used as maintenance therapy.
  8. The correct answer is C (Confirm why the brand is needed, explain the coverage gap, then dispense as written): A prescriber's no substitution instruction must be respected, so the pharmacist cannot override it to suit the plan. Confirming intent and explaining the cost difference lets NB make an informed decision. Sending her away delays therapy, and altering the form is not permitted.
  9. The correct answer is B (Blend the drug with equal diluent, then keep doubling the mixture with more diluent): Geometric dilution mixes the small amount of potent drug with an equal amount of diluent, then repeatedly adds diluent equal to the mixture already present. This builds uniformity in stages. Adding all the diluent at once leaves pockets of concentrated drug that cause dose variation between capsules.
  10. The correct answer is B (Avoid it because of her clot history, and arrange other treatment plus iron therapy): Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic with systemic effects, and a history of thromboembolism is a contraindication. Estrogen containing contraceptives also raise clot risk, so they are a poor alternative. Her low ferritin shows iron deficiency that needs iron, and an NSAID cannot replace it.
  11. The correct answer is C (Oxybutynin is strongly anticholinergic and may be contributing to the confusion, constipation and fall risk): Oxybutynin blocks muscarinic receptors in the brain, bowel and bladder, so confusion, constipation and falls are the expected harms in a frail older adult. It does cross into the central nervous system, which rules out the idea that it is too poorly absorbed to matter. Food timing does not change these effects, and a higher dose would add to the burden.
Case CHB1

HM is a 78 year old woman weighing 58 kg, height 160 cm, admitted with a urinary tract infection and new atrial fibrillation. Serum creatinine is 118 umol/L and stable; eGFR is reported as 38 mL/min/1.73 m2. Home medications: metformin 1000 mg twice daily, ramipril 5 mg daily, atorvastatin 20 mg daily. Apixaban 5 mg twice daily was started today. Her blood pressure is 128/76 mmHg and she is eating and drinking normally. Allergies: none.

  1. The correct answer is D (About 32 mL/min, which is lower than her reported eGFR of 38): (140 - 78) x 58 x 1.04 / 118 = 31.7, so about 32 mL/min. The female constant of 1.04 is needed, and her small body size makes Cockcroft-Gault lower than the indexed eGFR. Drug labels for most renally cleared agents are based on Cockcroft-Gault, not eGFR.
  2. The correct answer is A (Continue 5 mg twice daily, because she meets only one of the three criteria): She is 78 (not 80 or older), weighs 58 kg (meets the weight criterion) and has creatinine 118 umol/L (below 133), so only one criterion applies and the standard dose is correct. Creatinine clearance alone does not trigger a reduction in atrial fibrillation, and a clearance near 32 mL/min is not a contraindication.
  3. The correct answer is A (Reduce the daily dose, hold it if she becomes acutely unwell, and recheck kidney function): At an eGFR of 30 to 44, metformin is given at a reduced dose with closer renal monitoring, and it is held during acute illness with dehydration or infection risk because lactic acidosis becomes more likely. It is contraindicated only below an eGFR of 30, so full-dose continuation is too much and withdrawal is unnecessary.
  4. The correct answer is C (Permethrin plus oral ivermectin for the resident, with simultaneous treatment of contacts and staff): Crusted scabies carries a very high mite burden and spreads easily in institutions, so topical therapy is usually combined with oral ivermectin and repeated. Contacts and staff with exposure need simultaneous treatment or reinfestation continues. A corticosteroid alone would not kill mites and could worsen the infestation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Keep him there until paramedics arrive, since naloxone wears off before many opioids do): Naloxone lasts roughly 30 to 90 minutes, while fentanyl and especially longer-acting opioids can outlast it, so breathing may slow again after he seems recovered. He needs observation and medical assessment, and a repeat dose may be needed. Giving extra naloxone when he is awake adds no protection and may cause withdrawal.
  6. The correct answer is A (Hold further doses and seek urgent medical assessment for suspected acute pancreatitis today): Persistent severe abdominal pain radiating to the back, especially with vomiting, is the classic presentation of acute pancreatitis, a recognized risk of GLP-1 receptor agonists. The drug is held and she needs urgent evaluation with lipase testing. Treating it like ordinary dyspepsia or continuing injections delays diagnosis of a serious complication.
  7. The correct answer is D (Continue oral aripiprazole 15 mg daily for 14 days while injected drug levels build): Aripiprazole 400 mg monthly needs oral aripiprazole for 14 consecutive days after the first injection, because plasma levels rise slowly. Stopping at once leaves a gap in coverage and risks relapse. Reducing or alternating the tablets has no support in the labelled initiation plan.
  8. The correct answer is A (ISO Class 5, which is cleaner than the surrounding buffer room maintained at ISO Class 7): The primary engineering control, such as a biological safety cabinet, must provide ISO Class 5 air at the point where open sterile products are manipulated. It sits in a buffer area of ISO Class 7, which in turn is entered through an anteroom of ISO Class 8. Matching the cabinet to the room class would leave the critical zone too dirty.
  9. The correct answer is A (Publication bias, because trials with unfavourable results are less likely to be published): Studies with null or negative findings are published less often, so a review limited to published reports overestimates benefit. Searching trial registries and regulatory documents helps reveal the missing trials. The other biases concern how individual studies measure or assign, not which studies reach print.
  10. The correct answer is C (Contact the prescriber because estrogen raises stroke risk with aura, and suggest a progestin-only method): Migraine with aura carries a higher baseline risk of ischemic stroke, and estrogen-containing contraceptives add to it, so they are contraindicated regardless of blood pressure or smoking status. Lowering the estrogen dose does not remove the contraindication. A progestin-only or non-hormonal method is the appropriate alternative to discuss with the prescriber.
  11. The correct answer is C (Stop naproxen and the potassium based salt substitute, then repeat potassium within a few days): Two avoidable contributors are present: an NSAID that lowers renal potassium excretion and a potassium containing salt substitute. Removing them is the logical first step in a patient with a normal ECG and moderate hyperkalemia. Spironolactone would raise potassium further, and dilution by fluids does not address the cause.
  12. The correct answer is D (14.8 mL per hour, giving 1476 units of heparin each hour): The dose is 18 x 82 = 1476 units per hour. The bag holds 25 000 / 250 = 100 units per mL, so 1476 / 100 = 14.76, rounded to 14.8 mL per hour. A rate of 148 mL per hour would be a tenfold overdose from a decimal slip.
  13. The correct answer is C (Reduce the starting dose by about 50% and increase if early cycles are tolerated): DPYD breaks down fluorouracil, the active drug released from capecitabine, so reduced activity raises exposure and the risk of life threatening toxicity. Guidelines recommend about a 50% lower starting dose for intermediate metabolizers, with titration. Fluorouracil is equally affected, and leucovorin does not change DPYD activity.
  14. The correct answer is C (Dispense 50 tablets, which covers every step of the taper over twenty days): Each five day step uses 4, 3, 2 and 1 tablets daily, giving 20, 15, 10 and 5 tablets, for a total of 50. Adding a margin or counting each step as ten tablets does not match the order. The pharmacist should dispense the quantity needed to complete the taper.
  15. The correct answer is B (Verify with the prescriber's office that the second prescription is a replacement, then cancel the first): A second identical prescription may be a replacement or an error, so confirming with the prescriber prevents duplicate supply. If it is a replacement, the first prescription is cancelled so refills exist in one place only. Reporting the patient is out of proportion for a plausible lost prescription.
  16. The correct answer is B (240 mL, calculated as 8 mL per dose, three doses daily, for ten days): A 400 mg dose from 250 mg per 5 mL is 400 divided by 250, times 5, or 8 mL. Three doses daily is 24 mL, and ten days is 240 mL. Using 5 mL per dose, 10 mL per dose or twice daily dosing misreads either the concentration or the frequency.
  17. The correct answer is A (Early nausea is common, so eat smaller meals as the dose rises gradually): Gastrointestinal effects such as nausea are the most frequent reason patients struggle early, and they ease with smaller meals, stopping at fullness, and slow dose escalation. The 0.25 mg starting dose is a tolerability step and not the maintenance dose. Stopping at the first sign of nausea is unnecessary for most patients.
  18. The correct answer is A (Explain that viral infections do not respond to antibiotics and advise symptom relief with follow up): A short illness with runny nose, cough, and low grade fever in a well child is most likely viral, and antibiotics will not help while adding adverse effects and resistance pressure. Counselling on fluids, acetaminophen as needed, and warning signs to watch for is appropriate. Early antibiotics do not prevent pneumonia in viral colds.
  19. The correct answer is D (Both are accountable, the patient should be contacted, and the incident documented and reviewed): Regulated technicians and pharmacists are each accountable for their own practice, and the pharmacist's check does not remove the technician's responsibility for her entry. The patient could take double the intended weekly dose, so prompt contact and correction are needed. The incident should also be documented and reviewed to prevent a repeat.
  20. The correct answer is C (Confirm doses actually given over the past week with the facility, then inform the physician): The facility's medication administration record documents what was actually given, so confirming the real doses over the past week is the most reliable way to resolve three conflicting sources. The patient's recall and the dispensing record each reflect only one view. The physician needs that reconciled history along with the INR to decide on dosing.
  21. The correct answer is C (A confounder, because it is associated with the exposure and independently causes the outcome): A confounder is linked to the exposure, causes the outcome independently, and is not a step between exposure and outcome. Coffee does not cause people to smoke, so smoking is not a mediator. Effect modification would require the coffee effect itself to change across smoking strata.
  22. The correct answer is D (The three partners within the past 60 days should be notified for testing and treatment): Partners of a person with gonorrhea who had contact in the 60 days before diagnosis should be notified, tested and treated. Many infected partners have no symptoms but still transmit the infection. Limiting notification to one partner, or extending it to 6 months, does not match the recommended look-back period.
  23. The correct answer is D (Substitute azithromycin at an appropriate dose, document it, and notify the prescriber of the interaction): Clarithromycin strongly inhibits CYP3A4 and raises simvastatin exposure, increasing the risk of myopathy and rhabdomyolysis. Azithromycin has little CYP3A4 effect and treats the infection, so substituting avoids the interaction without delay. Moving the statin to another time of day does not remove the interaction.
  24. The correct answer is A (not recommended for infants under one year because of the risk of infant botulism): Honey can carry Clostridium botulinum spores, and an infant's immature gut flora allows them to grow and produce toxin, so honey is avoided before age one. Pasteurization does not destroy the spores. Honey is a reasonable remedy for night cough in children older than one year, and for this infant fluids and saline are safer.
  25. The correct answer is C (reduce to enoxaparin 30 mg once daily because her estimated creatinine clearance is about 25 mL/min): Clearance is (140 - 84) x 52 x 1.04 / 120, about 25 mL/min. Below 30 mL/min the prophylactic enoxaparin dose is reduced to 30 mg daily, and the drug is not stopped. Treatment-range dosing would raise bleeding risk.
  26. The correct answer is C (Giving 200 mg now is appropriate, since the total stays under 800 mg daily): Each 200 mg dose is 10 mg/kg, and the gaps of 6.5 hours respect the minimum interval. Three doses total 600 mg, which is 30 mg/kg and below the 40 mg/kg daily limit. A third dose at 19:00 is therefore acceptable.
  27. The correct answer is D (Strong recommendations can follow when benefits clearly outweigh harms and costs despite low certainty): GRADE separates certainty of evidence from strength of recommendation. A strong recommendation can be justified by a favourable balance of benefit, harm, cost and patient values, such as a cheap, low-risk intervention for a serious outcome. Expert consensus is not a higher grade of evidence.
  28. The correct answer is C (The lot number and expiry date of each source container used for each resident's pack): Recalls are issued by lot number, so only a record linking each pack to the lot of every source container lets the pharmacy find which residents received affected tablets. Imprint codes and quantities do not identify a lot. Expiry dates recorded at the same time also support the beyond use date for each card.
  29. The correct answer is B (Continuous hormonal suppression reduces pain by limiting ovulation and cyclic stimulation of lesions): Endometrial lesions respond to the monthly hormone cycle, so suppressing ovulation and cycling with a progestin or combined contraceptive reduces pain. Hormones control symptoms but do not remove lesions or cure the disease. Endometriosis is not caused by infection and fertility does not return faster.
  30. The correct answer is B (Two to three days after starting, about four to five half-lives of the drug): With repeated dosing, a drug reaches about 94 to 97 percent of steady state after four to five half-lives. At 12 hours per half-life that is 48 to 60 hours. One half-life gives only 50 percent of steady state, and ten half-lives is far more than is needed.
  31. The correct answer is A (20 mL, giving 4 mL once daily for five days): The daily dose is 1 x 20 = 20 mg, which at 5 mg per mL is 4 mL. Over five days the total is 20 mL. The other volumes come from errors in the concentration or the number of days.
Case CHB2

RM is a 72 year old man (80 kg) with type 2 diabetes, hypertension and chronic kidney disease. Labs: creatinine 150 umol/L, eGFR 38 mL/min/1.73 m2, urine ACR 12 mg/mmol, potassium 4.9 mmol/L, hemoglobin 105 g/L, ferritin 80 mcg/L, transferrin saturation 15%, with no bleeding. Medications: metformin 1000 mg twice daily, ramipril 10 mg daily, amlodipine 5 mg daily and ibuprofen 400 mg three times daily as needed for knee pain. No known allergies.

  1. The correct answer is D (Stop ibuprofen and suggest acetaminophen, because NSAIDs reduce renal perfusion and raise potassium in CKD): NSAIDs constrict the afferent arteriole, lower eGFR, raise potassium and blunt the effect of ramipril, so they should be stopped in CKD. Acetaminophen is the preferred analgesic. ACE inhibitors are recommended in diabetic kidney disease with albuminuria and are continued with monitoring, and amlodipine needs no renal adjustment.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reduce to a maximum of 1000 mg daily in divided doses and monitor kidney function often): At an eGFR of 30 to 44 the metformin dose is reduced to about half of the maximum, and the drug is contraindicated below 30 because of lactic acidosis risk. His current 2000 mg per day exceeds that limit. Glyburide has active metabolites cleared by the kidney and carries high hypoglycemia risk, so it is a poor substitute.
  3. The correct answer is B (Replete iron first, since TSAT below 20% indicates deficiency that limits ESA response): A transferrin saturation of 15% with ferritin 80 mcg/L shows absolute iron deficiency, which must be corrected before an erythropoiesis stimulating agent can work well. Transfusion is reserved for symptomatic or severe anemia and risks sensitization in transplant candidates. Vitamin D analogues treat mineral bone disease, not anemia.
  4. The correct answer is D (The benefit reflects the least severe component, so no mortality benefit has been shown): Composite endpoints combine events of different importance, and a favourable result can be driven by the most frequent but least serious component. Here hospitalization fell while death did not change, so the trial supports a reduction in hospitalization only. Composites that include hospitalization are acceptable if the components are reported separately.
  5. The correct answer is D (Explain that narcotic prescriptions cannot be refilled and obtain a new prescription from the prescriber): Under the Narcotic Control Regulations a prescription for a narcotic such as hydromorphone cannot be refilled, so repeats written on it have no effect. Each further supply needs a new prescription. Telephone agreement does not create refill authority, and part-fills must be specified by the prescriber with a total quantity and an interval.
  6. The correct answer is B (Arrange prompt prescriber review about a safer agent, without stopping valproic acid abruptly): Valproic acid carries a high risk of major malformations and neurodevelopmental harm, so a switch should be planned before conception with the prescriber. Stopping suddenly risks breakthrough seizures that endanger her and a pregnancy. Folic acid at a high dose is advised in this group, and a low dose does not give full protection.
  7. The correct answer is C (Change to 750 mg every 48 hours for his creatinine clearance of 35 mL/min): Levofloxacin is renally eliminated, and the 750 mg regimen is extended to every 48 hours when creatinine clearance is 20 to 49 mL/min. Waiting until clearance falls below 10 would cause accumulation and risk of QT prolongation, tendon effects and confusion in an older adult. Splitting the dose does not reduce total exposure.
  8. The correct answer is B (Give perioperative stress-dose hydrocortisone, because long-term prednisone blunts the adrenal response to surgery): After long-term steroid use the adrenal glands cannot mount a normal cortisol surge, so patients having major surgery receive stress-dose hydrocortisone, which is tapered back to the usual dose. Holding the dose risks adrenal crisis with hypotension. Fludrocortisone is a mineralocorticoid and does not supply the glucocorticoid effect needed.
  9. The correct answer is A (Contact the prescriber to obtain the issue date and document it on the prescription record): A prescription must show the date it was issued so the pharmacist can judge its validity and any refill authority. The pharmacist should not assume the date, and a partial supply still relies on an incomplete order. Contacting the prescriber and recording the confirmed date, with a note of who gave it, closes the gap.
  10. The correct answer is C (Complete weekend logs (outcome), audit completion (process), and staff time per audit (balancing)): A balanced set pairs an outcome measure (weekend log completeness, the actual problem), a process measure (whether audits are being done) and a balancing measure (staff time), which reveals unintended consequences. Counting audits alone shows activity, not improvement. A rising count of excursions may reflect better detection and does not by itself show improvement.
  11. The correct answer is D (Dissolve the drug in some syrup, then add syrup up to 120 mL total): Q.s. ad means a sufficient quantity to reach the stated final volume, so the finished product must measure 120 mL. Dissolving the powder in a full 120 mL of syrup gives a volume above 120 mL and a weaker concentration. Replacing part of the syrup with water changes the vehicle that was prescribed.
  12. The correct answer is B (Ask how he takes it, how well it works, and whether he has problems): Refill counselling uses a short set of open questions about use, effectiveness and problems, which finds adherence barriers and adverse effects that developed since the first fill. Repeating full new-prescription counselling wastes time. Skipping the discussion misses ongoing issues.
  13. The correct answer is B (A pictorial schedule using icons for the time of day beside each medication): Pictorial schedules with icons for morning, noon, evening and bedtime reduce the reading demand and support recall for patients with limited literacy. Tables, paragraphs and monographs all rely on text that he may not read confidently.
  14. The correct answer is D (5.6 mL by oral syringe twice daily, which delivers 280 mg per dose): The daily dose is 40 x 14 = 560 mg, so each of two doses is 280 mg. The suspension holds 50 mg per mL, so 280 divided by 50 gives 5.6 mL. An oral syringe is preferred over household spoons because it measures accurately.
  15. The correct answer is A (Hold the tadalafil and call the prescriber, since combined use can cause severe hypotension): Tadalafil has a long half-life, so an evening dose still overlaps with a nitrate taken that morning and can cause profound hypotension. Nitrates should be avoided for 48 hours after tadalafil, so separating the drugs by hours does not make daily dosing safe. The prescriber must choose between the two therapies.
  16. The correct answer is C (Correct the worksheet to salicylic acid 5 g and petrolatum 220 g before weighing): The scale factor is 250 divided by 1,000, which is 0.25, so salicylic acid should be 20 x 0.25, or 5 g, not 2.5 g. Matching the total weight does not prove each ingredient was scaled correctly, since the petrolatum was inflated to hide the error. The correct weights are urea 25 g, salicylic acid 5 g and petrolatum 220 g.
  17. The correct answer is D (Wait at least 36 hours after the last ramipril dose before starting sacubitril/valsartan): Neprilysin inhibition combined with ACE inhibition raises bradykinin and increases the risk of angioedema, so a 36 hour washout is required when moving from an ACE inhibitor. Overlap or a short gap exposes her to that risk. Switching from an ARB needs no washout, which is a different situation.
  18. The correct answer is D (30 days of the reaction first being documented within the hospital): Since Vanessa's Law, hospitals must report serious adverse drug reactions and medical device incidents to Health Canada within 30 days of documenting them. The 15 day timeline applies to manufacturers for serious unexpected reactions, which makes it tempting. Annual and discharge based deadlines are not part of the requirement.
  19. The correct answer is C (Past infection can reactivate under rituximab, so monitoring or antiviral prophylaxis is considered): Anti-HBc positivity shows past infection, and rituximab is strongly linked with hepatitis B reactivation even when surface antigen is negative. Monitoring of HBV DNA and liver tests, or antiviral prophylaxis, is therefore considered. Vaccination does not replace this, and risk is not limited to surface antigen positive patients or one drug class.
  20. The correct answer is C (Natural Medicines, which grades interaction, efficacy and safety evidence for herbal and supplement ingredients): Natural Medicines is a specialized database that rates the evidence and interaction severity for herbs and supplements, such as the serotonergic risk of St. John's wort with an SSRI. Product monograph compendia and depression guidelines do not cover supplement interactions in a graded way. A dispensing profile cannot supply interaction evidence.
  21. The correct answer is D (Three 50 mg tablets taken once daily with food): The dose is 2 mg/kg x 75 kg = 150 mg, which equals three 50 mg tablets. Two tablets would give only 1.3 mg/kg, and four or five tablets would exceed the ordered dose at 2.7 mg/kg and 3.3 mg/kg. Taking azathioprine with food may reduce nausea.
  22. The correct answer is D (Restricting enrolment to adults aged 60 to 70, which removes most age variation among participants): Restriction, randomization and matching act at the design stage by limiting or balancing the confounder before data are collected. Stratification, regression and standardization all adjust after the data exist. Restriction does limit how widely the findings can be generalized to other ages.
  23. The correct answer is B (2 July, because the earliest ingredient expiry comes before the end of the stability period): Twenty-eight days after 10 June is 8 July, but a beyond-use date cannot extend past the earliest expiry date of any ingredient, which is 2 July. The label therefore carries 2 July. Using half the study period or a general month default ignores both the stability data and the ingredient limit.
  24. The correct answer is C (Withhold doxycycline, contact the prescriber, and refer her to a travel clinic about deferring travel): Doxycycline is contraindicated in pregnancy because it affects fetal teeth and bones. Pregnant women should avoid travel to malaria risk areas where possible, and any prophylaxis needs specialist travel medicine input. Chloroquine does not cover resistant falciparum in Tanzania, and repellents alone leave her unprotected.
  25. The correct answer is B (Start antiretrovirals promptly, within 72 hours of exposure, and continue them for 28 days): PEP is most effective when started as soon as possible and must begin within 72 hours of exposure, and she is still within that window. The course lasts 28 days and uses a three drug regimen. A source of unknown status is still assessed for risk, and delaying until follow up visits or test results reduces the benefit.
  26. The correct answer is B (The provincial health privacy statute, recognized as substantially similar to PIPEDA for health custodians): Several provinces have health privacy laws that the federal government has deemed substantially similar to PIPEDA, so they apply to health custodians in place of it. The Privacy Act covers federal institutions, and a corporate policy cannot replace statutory duties.
  27. The correct answer is C (Advise her to stop codeine and have the infant assessed urgently for opioid toxicity): Codeine is converted to morphine, and in an ultra-rapid metabolizer the milk morphine level can cause sedation and respiratory depression in the infant. Signs of this need urgent assessment and the drug should be stopped. Pumping and discarding or spacing doses does not remove the risk, and tramadol has similar concerns in breastfeeding.
  28. The correct answer is C (Continue atorvastatin and monitor liver tests, since mild stable elevation is expected in fatty liver): A mild ALT rise below three times the upper limit that is stable and symptom free is common in fatty liver and is not a reason to stop a statin. Statins are safe and beneficial in this population, which carries high cardiovascular risk. Stopping therapy would remove protection without treating the underlying liver disease.
  29. The correct answer is D (Throughout follow-up, treated patients had a 30% lower rate of dying at any moment): A hazard ratio of 0.70 means that at any point in follow-up, treated patients had a 30% lower instantaneous rate of death than controls. It is not a difference in survival time, a proportion of patients saved, or an absolute risk. The interval excludes 1, so the finding is statistically significant.
  30. The correct answer is A (Kindly decline the gifts and personal contact, keep interactions professional, and suggest community social supports): The pharmacist should be kind but clear: declining expensive gifts and personal contact keeps the relationship professional, and pointing to social supports addresses the likely loneliness. Accepting gifts or sharing a private number crosses boundaries, while banning RK and contacting his family overreacts and breaches his privacy.
  31. The correct answer is D (Assess the request using the patient's record and clinical facts, and address the colleague's assumption respectfully): Decisions about opioid therapy should rest on documented clinical information such as the dispensing history, not on stereotypes about race. Anti-Black racism in pain management is well documented, and a pharmacist has a professional duty to address a colleague's biased assumption respectfully as part of equitable, safe practice. Delaying or flagging without evidence harms the patient, and ignoring the comment allows the bias to continue.
  32. The correct answer is D (It shows what the patient really has at home, including unreported nonprescription products): Seeing the actual containers exposes nonprescription products, supplements, expired items and duplicate or discontinued drugs that patients forget to mention. It supplements, but does not replace, the interview and other sources. Labels show what was dispensed, not necessarily how the patient takes the drug.
  33. The correct answer is A (A1c is a surrogate endpoint, and improving it does not guarantee fewer clinical events): A surrogate endpoint is a laboratory or physiological marker meant to stand in for outcomes patients feel or live through. Some glucose-lowering drugs have improved A1c without reducing, or even while increasing, cardiovascular events. Clinical outcome trials are needed to show that the improvement matters.
  34. The correct answer is B (Provide an emergency supply or renewal for continuity of care, then notify the prescriber): Most provinces allow pharmacists to renew or extend a non-narcotic prescription for continuity of care, with documentation and notification of the prescriber. The duration is limited, not unlimited, and the pharmacist is responsible for notifying the prescriber rather than leaving it to the patient.
  35. The correct answer is B (The prescription number and the name, address and telephone number of the dispensing pharmacy): Provincial regulations generally require the prescription number and the identity and contact details of the dispensing pharmacy so the patient or another provider can trace and query the prescription. Costs, personal prescriber details and health card numbers are not standard label requirements and may raise privacy concerns.
  36. The correct answer is A (Apply from the neck down, leave on for 8 to 14 hours, then wash off): Permethrin must cover all skin from the neck down, including under the nails, folds and between the fingers, and stay on long enough to kill mites. Treating only visible lesions misses mites elsewhere. Close contacts should be treated at the same time, and a second application about a week later is commonly advised to kill newly hatched mites.
  37. The correct answer is B (Professional service area, restricted area without public access, pharmacy self-selection shelf, open retail shelf): Schedule I drugs stay in the professional service area, Schedule II drugs in an area without public access, Schedule III drugs in a self-selection area inside the pharmacy with a pharmacist available, and unscheduled products may sit on any retail shelf. Each distractor places a prescription or Schedule II product where the public can reach it, or a Schedule III product outside the pharmacy.
  38. The correct answer is D (Naltrexone blocks opioids and could precipitate withdrawal, so acamprosate is a better choice): Naltrexone is an opioid antagonist, so in a person physically dependent on opioids it blocks analgesia and can precipitate severe withdrawal. Patients usually need to be opioid free for 7 to 10 days first, which is not realistic with his ongoing chronic pain therapy. Acamprosate has no opioid interaction and is a reasonable alternative.
  39. The correct answer is D (Neuroleptic malignant syndrome; stop haloperidol and give supportive care with cooling and fluids): Fever, severe lead-pipe rigidity, altered mental status and a very high creatine kinase developing over days after an antipsychotic increase indicate neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Reduced reflexes and slow onset separate it from serotonin syndrome, which shows clonus and hyperreflexia. Treatment starts with stopping the dopamine antagonist and supportive care.
  40. The correct answer is A (Withhold dispensing, verify the prescriber through the provincial college directory, and contact them directly): A pharmacist must be satisfied that a narcotic prescription is genuine before dispensing. An unreachable number, an out-of-area prescriber and a patient with no record are warning signs, so the prescriber should be verified independently through the college directory and spoken to directly. Partial supply and recording identification do not replace verification, and handing back a suspect prescription leaves the concern unresolved.
  41. The correct answer is D (75 mg daily, half the target dose, with titration every two to four weeks): The target dose is 2.5 x 60 = 150 mg, so 30 to 80% is 45 to 120 mg, and 75 mg (50%) falls inside that window. Intermediate metabolizers have reduced TPMT activity and accumulate toxic thioguanine nucleotides, so dosing is lowered and titrated against blood counts. Full or higher dosing risks severe myelosuppression.
  42. The correct answer is A (Stop oxybutynin and ask whether donepezil is causing the urgency, a possible prescribing cascade): Oxybutynin directly opposes donepezil and worsens cognition, so it should be stopped. Cholinesterase inhibitors can cause urinary urgency, so the new symptom may be a drug effect that was then treated with a second drug. Tolterodine is also anticholinergic, and a higher donepezil dose would add adverse effects without a sound rationale.
  43. The correct answer is B (Delete the post, then report the breach internally so the patient can be notified): A post linking a patient to a medication discloses personal health information, so it must be removed immediately and handled as a privacy breach under the pharmacy's policy, including notifying the patient. Editing the caption leaves the information partly exposed. Seeking the patient's permission after the fact or hoping nobody notices does not meet breach obligations.
Case CHB3

NB is a 31 year old woman, 7 weeks pregnant, with primary hypothyroidism for five years. She takes levothyroxine 100 mcg every morning with breakfast coffee, and swallows a prenatal vitamin containing iron 27 mg and calcium 250 mg at the same time. Her pre-pregnancy TSH was 2.1 mIU/L. Today her TSH is 5.8 mIU/L and her free T4 is low-normal. She has no drug allergies. Her family physician asks the pharmacist for advice.

  1. The correct answer is B (Pregnancy raises thyroxine requirements, so the dose is usually raised about 25 to 30%): Estrogen raises thyroxine-binding globulin and placental metabolism increases hormone turnover, so most women need roughly 25 to 30% more levothyroxine soon after conception. Leaving the dose unchanged or reducing it risks maternal hypothyroidism, which can impair fetal neurodevelopment. Stopping treatment in the first trimester is harmful because the fetus relies on maternal thyroid hormone then.
  2. The correct answer is A (Take levothyroxine alone with water, and separate the prenatal vitamin by four hours): Iron, calcium and coffee all bind or impair gut absorption of levothyroxine, so the tablet works best taken alone with water, with minerals separated by about four hours. Taking both at bedtime still lets them bind in the gut. Stopping the prenatal vitamin removes folic acid and iron she needs during pregnancy.
  3. The correct answer is C (Check TSH about every four weeks until mid-pregnancy, then once near 30 weeks): Requirements change through pregnancy, so TSH is rechecked about every four weeks in the first half and at least once around 30 weeks to keep it in the trimester-specific target. Levothyroxine reaches steady state in six to eight weeks, so waiting six months or until delivery would leave the fetus exposed to under-treatment.
  4. The correct answer is A (Stop linagliptin and seek urgent medical assessment, because acute pancreatitis is a real possibility): DPP-4 inhibitors have been linked to acute pancreatitis, and severe persistent abdominal pain radiating to the back with vomiting is the classic presentation. The drug should be stopped and he needs prompt assessment. Antacids, dose reductions and changes in timing do not address a possibly serious adverse reaction.
  5. The correct answer is B (Refer him to his physician to assess the weight loss before recommending any laxative): New constipation in an older adult with unintentional weight loss is an alarm feature that needs investigation for colorectal cancer or other serious disease. Iron can darken stools and constipate, but it does not explain the weight loss. Treating the symptom first would delay diagnosis.
  6. The correct answer is A (Refer for medical assessment and avoid loperamide, since fever with bloody stool suggests infection): Blood in the stool with fever points to an invasive bacterial infection, where slowing gut motility with loperamide can prolong exposure to the organism and risk complications. He needs examination and possibly stool testing. Probiotics and bismuth do not replace assessment when these red flags are present.
  7. The correct answer is D (Tell the patient promptly, explain what happened, apologize, and describe how the error will be corrected): Patients have a right to know about errors that reach them, even when no harm has resulted. Prompt, honest disclosure with an apology and a description of corrective steps maintains trust and lets the patient watch for effects. Waiting for harm or concealing the error undermines that trust and conflicts with professional standards.
  8. The correct answer is B (Document the duplicate sedative alert and contact the new prescriber with the profile findings): Two sedative-hypnotics at bedtime raise the risk of falls, confusion and respiratory depression in an 80 year old. The pharmacist should share the profile information with the prescriber so a single plan can be agreed. Stopping a drug without prescriber input or refusing without communication does not resolve the problem.
  9. The correct answer is B (Choose broad spectrum coverage, SPF 30 or higher, and a water resistant label claim): Broad spectrum labelling means the product filters both ultraviolet A and B, and SPF 30 or higher with a water resistant claim suits swimming. Even water resistant products need reapplication after towel drying and about every two hours. Very high SPF numbers add little extra protection and encourage longer exposure, and hypoallergenic describes irritation risk, not sun protection.
  10. The correct answer is C (Contact the first prescriber to verify the history, and withhold dispensing until the overlap is clarified): An overlapping narcotic fill raises concern for therapeutic duplication, misuse or diversion, so the pharmacist should verify with the earlier prescriber and decide with that information. Dispensing without clarification ignores the information in the monitoring program. Partial supply does not address the concern, and the college does not approve individual fills.
  11. The correct answer is B (CBD inhibits warfarin metabolism, so contact the prescriber about closer INR monitoring and dose review): CBD inhibits cytochrome P450 enzymes involved in warfarin clearance, which can raise the INR. With a recent product change and a rise from 2.4 to 3.8, the pharmacist should contact the prescriber for monitoring and possible dose adjustment. Doubling the dose, assuming a diet change, or giving a vitamin K injection without assessment would be unsafe or unfounded.
  12. The correct answer is D (Suspect rising clozapine levels from stopped smoking and request a level and dose review): Hydrocarbons in tobacco smoke induce CYP1A2, so stopping smoking can raise clozapine levels by 50 percent or more within days to weeks, causing sedation, hypersalivation, and hypotension. A level and dose reduction are needed. Nicotine itself does not induce CYP1A2, so a patch will not restore the old level.
  13. The correct answer is C (Two tablets at bedtime, then adding tablets during the day if symptoms persist): The doxylamine and pyridoxine combination is started as two tablets at bedtime, which also uses the sedating effect. If symptoms continue, a morning tablet and then a mid-afternoon tablet are added, to a usual maximum of four tablets daily. Eight tablets a day and dosing every four hours greatly exceed the labelled maximum.
  14. The correct answer is A (Return them to the pharmacy take-back program so they are collected for safe destruction): Unused or expired medications should go to a pharmacy take-back program so they are destroyed in a controlled way. Water treatment does not reliably remove drug residues, so flushing harms waterways. Garbage or recycling disposal is a US-style home method that is not the Canadian recommendation.
  15. The correct answer is C (Briefly state the serious risks of warfarin, then ask what he knows and fill the gaps): Warfarin is a high-alert drug and regular acetaminophen use can raise the INR, so a short focused discussion is needed even when the patient hurries. Asking what he knows targets the gaps efficiently. Simply accepting the refusal leaves a serious risk unaddressed, and forcing the session would damage rapport.
  16. The correct answer is C (Limit citalopram to 20 mg daily or switch agents, and correct the low potassium): Citalopram prolongs the QT interval in a dose-dependent way, and Health Canada limits the dose to 20 mg daily in patients over 65. Hypokalemia from furosemide adds arrhythmia risk. Tricyclics also affect conduction, and stopping warfarin is unrelated to the QT problem.
Case CHB4

BN is a 58 year old man with type 2 diabetes for 12 years. Weight 92 kg, eGFR 70 mL/min/1.73 m2, A1c 8.6%. He takes metformin 1000 mg twice daily and insulin glargine U100 30 units at bedtime. Fasting glucose readings this week were 9.6 to 10.9 mmol/L, pre-supper values 6.5 to 7.5 mmol/L, and a 3 am check was 8.9 mmol/L. He has had no hypoglycemia. He injects only into one area of the abdomen, where a firm lump has formed. His prescriber's plan is to increase glargine by 2 units every 3 days until fasting glucose reaches 4 to 7 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is D (His overnight basal coverage is inadequate, so gradual upward titration of glargine is appropriate): A 3 am value of 8.9 mmol/L is not low, which rules out nocturnal hypoglycemia and rebound, and the in-range pre-supper values show daytime control is acceptable. Persistently high fasting readings therefore point to insufficient basal insulin. Titration of the basal dose is the standard next step, with the injection site problem managed alongside it.
  2. The correct answer is D (Rotate to healthy tissue and avoid the lump, since absorption from it is erratic): A firm lump is lipohypertrophy, and insulin injected into it is absorbed unpredictably, which worsens glucose variability. Injections should rotate within and between sites using healthy tissue. Massaging the area or repeating the same spot does not fix absorption and tends to worsen the tissue change, and his dose may need monitoring when he changes sites.
  3. The correct answer is B (34 units, the starting dose plus two 2 unit increases made on days three and six): The plan raises the dose by 2 units every 3 days, so increases occur on day 3 and day 6 before day 7. That gives 30 plus 2 plus 2, or 34 units. A third increase would only be due on day 9, and a single increase would leave him under-titrated.
  4. The correct answer is B (Take no more loperamide today, since she has already reached the 8 mg daily limit): Her total so far is 4 + 2 + 2 = 8 mg, which equals the labelled daily maximum for self-care. Taking more would exceed the limit and raise the risk of cardiac toxicity and ileus. She should focus on fluids and oral rehydration and seek care if diarrhea continues.
  5. The correct answer is A (add ezetimibe 10 mg daily and recheck lipids in about six to 12 weeks): He is on the maximum statin dose and remains above the secondary prevention target, so the guideline step is to add ezetimibe and recheck lipids. Fibrates mainly lower triglycerides, niacin has not improved outcomes on top of statins, and the 2.6 mmol/L figure comes from older targets.
  6. The correct answer is A (Discard the opened vial 28 days after first use, even if insulin remains in it): An insulin vial in use should be discarded 28 days after the first puncture, whether it is kept at room temperature or in the refrigerator. A vial that would last 41 days therefore outlasts its in-use life, so stability rather than volume limits the supply. Unused insulin is wasted after 28 days, and 14 and 42 days do not match the labelling.
  7. The correct answer is B (Home readings, current regimen and adherence, the pharmacist's concern, and the specific action requested): A useful referral is concise and gives the receiving physician what is needed to act: objective data, the current treatment and adherence, the reason for referral and the request. A five year dispensing history is more than the minimum necessary. A written order is outside the pharmacist's role here, and omitting clinical detail makes the referral unusable.
  8. The correct answer is A (Step down to the lowest effective dose or on demand use and monitor symptoms): In patients whose symptoms have resolved and who lack a clear indication such as Barrett esophagus, severe esophagitis or ongoing NSAID gastroprotection, guidelines advise stepping down to the lowest effective dose or on demand use. Abrupt cessation can cause rebound acid symptoms that prompt restarting. Escalating or continuing indefinitely adds exposure without benefit.
  9. The correct answer is A (A long-acting dual bronchodilator, a LAMA with a LABA, plus salbutamol as needed): A symptomatic COPD patient with persistent dyspnea on a short acting reliever needs long-acting bronchodilation, and LAMA/LABA gives better symptom relief than either alone. Inhaled corticosteroids are not first line without frequent exacerbations or eosinophilia. Scheduled short acting agents and theophylline are less effective and less safe.
  10. The correct answer is B (Advise her to stop ibuprofen and seek emergency care now for suspected gastrointestinal bleeding): Black tarry stools with light headedness on standing in a patient taking warfarin, aspirin and an NSAID signal an active upper gastrointestinal bleed with possible hemodynamic compromise, which needs emergency assessment rather than self treatment. Ibuprofen must be stopped. Selling a PPI or delaying care could allow serious blood loss before treatment.
  11. The correct answer is D (Seal the bag, record the delivery, and arrange a counselling call with the pharmacist today): A delivery process should keep the pharmacy accountable: sealed packaging protects privacy, a record shows who received it, and the pharmacist still has to offer counselling, which a phone call can provide. A leaflet or the courier cannot replace the pharmacist, and an unattended bag risks theft or a wrong recipient.
  12. The correct answer is B (Treat latent tuberculosis first and begin the biologic after the prescriber approves the timing): A positive skin test with a normal chest X-ray and no symptoms indicates latent tuberculosis, which can reactivate under tumour necrosis factor inhibition. Treatment of latent infection comes first, with the biologic started on the prescriber's timing. A normal X-ray does not remove that need, and BCG is a live vaccine that is contraindicated.
  13. The correct answer is D (Offer counselling to the patient by phone or through the neighbour and document what occurs): The counselling offer for a new prescription belongs to the patient, so when an agent collects the drug the pharmacist should arrange to reach the patient, for example by phone, and record the outcome. A leaflet or counselling an agent who will not take the drug does not replace the offer.
  14. The correct answer is C (switching omeprazole to pantoprazole, which has less effect on clopidogrel activation through CYP2C19): Clopidogrel is a prodrug activated by CYP2C19, and omeprazole inhibits that enzyme, which can lower antiplatelet effect. Pantoprazole has minimal CYP2C19 inhibition. Spacing the doses does not help because the inhibition persists, and warfarin is not a stroke substitute here.
  15. The correct answer is D (Explain when relief should occur and which symptoms mean he needs a physician): A self-care consultation ends with a clear timeframe for improvement and specific warning signs that should prompt medical care. Dosing acetaminophen every two hours exceeds the usual interval, and taking it daily to prevent headaches is not appropriate and can contribute to medication overuse headache. Waiting for the tablets to run out gives no safety net.
  16. The correct answer is D (Lower the weekly dose by about 10% and recheck in one to two weeks): A persistent INR slightly above range without bleeding is managed by reducing the weekly dose by 5 to 10% and rechecking within one to two weeks. Vitamin K is not needed at this INR and risks overcorrection. Continuing the same dose leaves a persistent elevation uncorrected, and a change of drug is not required for a correctable finding.
  17. The correct answer is C (Refer him for same day medical assessment of possible cellulitis and advise against using topical steroid): Spreading warmth, swelling, fever and chills in a person with diabetes suggest cellulitis, which needs prompt assessment and systemic antibiotics. A topical steroid could mask or worsen infection. Antihistamines and antifungals do not treat bacterial skin infection and delay would risk deeper spread.
  18. The correct answer is A (Kept in the drug group and analyzed with the group to which they were randomized): Intention to treat analysis keeps every patient in the arm assigned at randomization, regardless of adherence. This preserves the balance created by randomization and reflects the effect of a treatment policy in real use. Removing or reassigning dropouts reintroduces the selection that randomization prevented.
  19. The correct answer is A (Mild burning at the site is common early on and usually fades within days): Tacrolimus ointment often causes transient burning or warmth in the early days, which usually settles as the skin heals. Occlusion raises absorption and is not advised. Burning alone is not an allergy sign, and twice daily use is the labelled schedule, with no skin thinning like that of potent steroids.
  20. The correct answer is B (Reduce to about 42 drops per minute for this 20 drop per mL tubing): 1500 mL over 12 hours is 125 mL per hour, or 2.08 mL per minute. Multiplying by 20 drops per mL gives 41.7, rounded to 42 drops per minute. The 125 figure applies only to 60 drop tubing, and at 20 drops per mL it would empty the bag in about 4 hours.
  21. The correct answer is B (Each mmol of calcium provides 2 mEq, so 2.5 mmol equals 5 mEq): The mEq value equals the mmol value multiplied by the valence, and calcium has a valence of 2. So 2.5 mmol is 5 mEq. Treating calcium like sodium or potassium, where the two units match one to one, would halve the stated amount.
Case CHB5

SN is a 36 year old woman who has filled sertraline 100 mg daily and an oral contraceptive at the pharmacy for three years. She has signed no release authorizing disclosure to anyone. This week the pharmacy receives three contacts: her husband phones to ask whether she collected her prescriptions, a police officer visits asking for her dispensing history for an unrelated inquiry, and SN asks about restricting who can view her record.

  1. The correct answer is D (Decline to confirm or share any information and offer to pass a message to SN): Confirming that a person is a patient or has collected a prescription discloses health information, and SN has not consented. Offering to pass on a message respects her confidentiality while still helping the husband, and quizzing him about her drugs does not establish authority.
  2. The correct answer is D (Decline without a warrant, production order or other legal authority, and document the request): Police have no automatic right to a patient's record, and disclosure needs patient consent, a warrant or court order, or a statutory authority such as an immediate risk to safety. Giving part of the record or waiting for closing time does not create the missing authority.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain the lock-box request, record it, and describe how it may limit other providers' access): Most provincial health privacy laws let a patient restrict access to her information, often called a lock-box, and the pharmacist should explain the request and its consequences for care. Deleting a dispensing entry would falsify the legal record, and a patient does not need a physician to make the request.
  4. The correct answer is B (The sample was drawn too early, but symptoms and low potassium suggest toxicity, so hold digoxin): Digoxin needs about six hours or more to distribute into tissue, so a two hour sample overestimates the true concentration. Still, visual symptoms, nausea, kidney disease and hypokalemia all point to likely toxicity, which hypokalemia worsens, so digoxin should be held and a proper level obtained. Treating the symptoms and continuing the drug is unsafe.
  5. The correct answer is B (Hold the dose, assess her safety, and contact the prescriber before deciding on dosing): Sedation from other central nervous system depressants plus methadone can cause respiratory depression. The dose should be held, the patient assessed, and the prescriber contacted so that dosing is planned safely. Giving a full or half dose adds opioid to an already sedated patient, and a ride home does not make that safe.
Case CHB6

TM is a 24 year old woman with asthma diagnosed two years ago. She uses salbutamol 100 mcg per puff about five times a week and wakes with night cough twice a week. She takes no controller medicine. Her FEV1 is 82% of predicted with reversibility. She is not pregnant, has no known allergies and does not smoke. She takes a combined oral contraceptive and asks how to get better control.

  1. The correct answer is A (Low dose budesonide-formoterol taken as needed for symptoms, replacing salbutamol as her reliever): TM has symptoms more than twice weekly and night waking, so she is not controlled on salbutamol alone. Current guidance favours low dose ICS-formoterol used as needed, which treats symptoms and the underlying inflammation together. A long-acting beta agonist alone lacks an anti-inflammatory component, and montelukast is not a preferred first line agent.
  2. The correct answer is A (Inhale quickly and deeply, hold the breath briefly, then rinse the mouth with water): A dry powder inhaler relies on the patient's own fast, deep breath to disperse the powder, so slow inhalation delivers little drug. Breath holding helps deposition, and rinsing then spitting out water limits oral thrush from the corticosteroid. Exhaling into the device moistens and clumps the powder, and a spacer is not used with these devices.
  3. The correct answer is C (Discuss a cautious step down in dose with the prescriber, since control has been sustained): Asthma that has been well controlled for about three months qualifies for a cautious step down to the lowest dose that maintains control. This lowers drug exposure and cost while preserving protection. Escalating a controlled patient is unnecessary, and abrupt withdrawal of all therapy risks relapse.
  4. The correct answer is A (Stop work, redo hand hygiene, put on new sterile gloves, then disinfect supplies and resume): Gloves that touch a non-sterile surface are contaminated and cannot be made sterile again by spraying alcohol. The pharmacist should have the technician stop, repeat hand hygiene and put on new sterile gloves, then disinfect supplies before resuming. Continuing with the same gloves risks introducing microorganisms into the bag, and wiping the handle does not fix that.
  5. The correct answer is D (A patient identifier such as initials, the reporter's contact details, the suspected product and the reaction): A valid report needs an identifiable patient (initials, age, sex or another code, not a name), an identifiable reporter, a suspected product and a described reaction. Causality assessment, a confirmed diagnosis and prescriber agreement improve a report but are not required. Patient names and health card numbers are not needed.
  6. The correct answer is C (Replace it now and advise storing the device at room temperature, away from heat and light): Epinephrine degrades with heat and light, and pink or brown discolouration shows the solution has deteriorated and may not work in an emergency. The device should be replaced at once rather than at expiry. Storage should be at room temperature, since refrigeration or a hot or freezing vehicle can both damage the product.
  7. The correct answer is B (Rifampin lowers hormone levels, so a non-hormonal method such as a copper IUD is needed): Rifampin induces CYP3A4 and lowers estrogen and progestin levels, so hormonal contraceptives become unreliable during treatment and for about a month after the final dose. A copper IUD or another non-hormonal method avoids the interaction. Taking the pill with food or adding condoms for a short period does not cover the whole induction period.
  8. The correct answer is A (The complaints committee, which can dismiss the matter, resolve it or refer it to discipline): Colleges generally screen complaints through a complaints committee after investigation, with the power to dismiss, require remediation or refer serious allegations to the discipline committee for a hearing. The discipline committee hears referred allegations rather than every complaint, and the association is not the regulator.
  9. The correct answer is A (Recount and review records for error, then report any unexplained loss to Health Canada): The pharmacist first rules out a counting or recording error, because a loss that remains unexplained must be reported to Health Canada as required for narcotics. Quietly adjusting the inventory hides a possible diversion. Waiting for a confession or for another delivery delays a reporting duty that does not depend on identifying who is responsible.
  10. The correct answer is C (Notify the prescriber of the interaction, and request monitoring or an alternative antifungal): Fluconazole inhibits CYP3A4 and CYP2C19, which raises methadone levels, and both drugs can prolong the QT interval. With a baseline QTc of 450 ms, the combination calls for prescriber involvement, monitoring for sedation and arrhythmia, or a different antifungal. Separating administration times does not change enzyme inhibition, and the pharmacist should not change methadone doses alone.
  11. The correct answer is B (an SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 agonist with proven cardiovascular benefit): Diabetes Canada recommends adding an SGLT2 inhibitor or GLP-1 agonist with demonstrated cardiovascular benefit in patients with established atherosclerotic disease whose A1c is above target on metformin, because the cardiovascular benefit extends beyond A1c lowering. Sulfonylureas and thiazolidinediones lack that outcome evidence.
  12. The correct answer is D (Justice, because patients with equal clinical need are entitled to fair access to available stock): Justice requires that patients with similar clinical needs receive care fairly rather than being ranked by ability to pay. Fidelity and autonomy do not address how scarce stock is allocated, and the inhaler is not itself a source of direct harm. The proposal treats equal need unequally on a non-clinical basis.
  13. The correct answer is D (Decline the early fill, document the request, and discuss the lost-supply pattern with the prescriber): Early refills of controlled stimulants for lost supply, repeated requests, and mention of others paying for the drug are diversion warning signs. The pharmacist should decline, record the encounter, and share the pattern with the prescriber, who can decide on follow-up. Reporting to police without evidence or giving partial supplies bypasses that assessment.
  14. The correct answer is A (Verify identity, match the labels to the bag contents, and note refrigeration for the insulin pens): Final release should confirm identity, match drug, strength and directions to the labels, and give storage instructions such as keeping the pens refrigerated. Reading a list aloud shifts the check to the patient, and checking only some items or waiting for questions lets errors and storage problems go unnoticed.
  15. The correct answer is A (A power imbalance exists, so YO should decline while DK remains a current patient): A pharmacist holds more knowledge and access to personal health information than a patient, which creates a power imbalance. A current patient's consent does not remove that imbalance, and documenting the attraction or telling a manager does not make a romantic relationship appropriate. Boundary standards cover emotional and romantic involvement, not only sexual contact.
  16. The correct answer is C (Keep the door closed, monitor temperature, and move stock to validated coolers if limits are reached): A closed refrigerator holds its temperature for several hours, so the door should stay shut while a continuous thermometer is watched. When the 2 to 8 C range is about to be exceeded, stock should be transferred to validated coolers with conditioned packs. Freezing damages insulin and vaccines, and frequent door opening shortens holding time.
  17. The correct answer is D (Offer a qualified interpreter, explain why, and let the daughter stay as support): Family members may omit, soften or change information, and an untrained interpreter can compromise accuracy and the patient's own voice, which is a safety concern with insulin. Offering a qualified interpreter while welcoming the daughter as support respects both. Written English instructions or gestures alone do not ensure he understands injection technique and hypoglycemia.
  18. The correct answer is A (Benztropine 1 to 2 mg intramuscularly, then oral anticholinergic cover for several days): Neck twisting, jaw clenching and upward eye deviation within hours of a high potency antipsychotic injection describe acute dystonia, which responds to an anticholinergic such as benztropine or diphenhydramine. Oral cover is needed because the antipsychotic outlasts a single dose. Dantrolene treats malignant hyperthermia, and a repeat antipsychotic dose would worsen dystonia.
  19. The correct answer is B (Avoid the plaster, see a podiatrist, and test bath water with an elbow or thermometer): Salicylic acid plasters can injure insensate skin and cause ulcers, and home cutting carries similar risk, so a podiatrist should treat the corn. Neuropathy makes hand testing of water temperature unreliable, so an elbow or thermometer is safer. Daily soaking dries and macerates the skin and does not help.
  20. The correct answer is B (Avoid bupropion because of seizure risk, and offer varenicline or combination nicotine replacement): Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold and is contraindicated in patients with a seizure disorder. Varenicline and nicotine replacement are acceptable in stable cardiovascular disease, and the risk of continued smoking exceeds the risk of nicotine replacement. Lamotrigine does not neutralize the seizure risk, and reducing the dose does not remove it.
  21. The correct answer is D (Report the loss to Health Canada within ten days of discovery, so by June 13): The Narcotic Control Regulations require a pharmacist to report a loss or theft of narcotics to Health Canada within ten days of discovering it. Ten days from June 3 is June 13, and notifying police or the college does not replace the federal report.
  22. The correct answer is B (More body fat and slower oxidation prolong the half-life of diazepam and its active metabolite): Diazepam is lipophilic, so a larger fat compartment in older adults enlarges its volume of distribution and lengthens its half-life. Phase I oxidation also slows with age, and the active metabolite desmethyldiazepam accumulates. Renal elimination of unchanged drug is minimal, and hepatic blood flow falls rather than rises with age.
  23. The correct answer is B (98 tablets, counting 14 whole tablets of furosemide that will be split into halves): Ramipril needs 28 tablets and metformin needs 2 x 28, or 56 tablets. Half a tablet of furosemide daily for 28 days is 14 whole tablets, which are split before packing. The total is 28 + 56 + 14, or 98 tablets.
  24. The correct answer is A (A 72 year old man taking aspirin 81 mg with a prior bleeding ulcer): A prior ulcer complication is the strongest predictor of NSAID related upper gastrointestinal bleeding, and age over 65 and concurrent low dose aspirin add further risk. Combining these makes a PPI clearly indicated. The other patients have no major gastrointestinal risk factors, and hypertension or diabetes alone does not justify routine gastroprotection.
  25. The correct answer is B (It highlights differing letters so that similar drug names are less likely to be confused): Tall man lettering capitalizes the letters that differ between look-alike, sound-alike names, helping the eye and brain notice the difference when selecting or verifying a drug. It does not signal dose range, generic status or monitoring needs. It reduces risk but does not replace independent checks, which remain necessary.
  26. The correct answer is C (Repeat HIV testing and review adherence every three months, with kidney function checked periodically): PrEP users need an HIV test about every three months, because continuing PrEP with undiagnosed infection risks drug resistance. Adherence and sexually transmitted infection screening are reviewed at the same visits, and creatinine is checked periodically since tenofovir can affect the kidneys. Viral load monitoring and stopping on an undetectable result apply to treatment, not prevention.
  27. The correct answer is B (Stop warfarin five days before, check the INR the day prior, and skip heparin bridging): In atrial fibrillation without prior stroke or a mechanical valve, trials showed that heparin bridging increases bleeding without lowering thromboembolism. Warfarin is stopped about five days before so the INR is near normal on the day, and the INR is checked beforehand. A procedure with high bleeding risk makes continuing warfarin inappropriate.
  28. The correct answer is C (Health Canada advises against these products for children under six years of age): Health Canada states that over-the-counter cough and cold products should not be used in children under six because of the risk of side effects and lack of proven benefit. Measuring accurately or shortening the course does not change that advice. Saline drops, fluids and rest are safer options.
  29. The correct answer is B (240 days, because the target of 9600 mg divided by 40 mg daily gives 240): The cumulative target is 120 mg/kg x 80 kg = 9600 mg. The daily dose is 0.5 mg/kg x 80 kg = 40 mg, so 9600 / 40 = 240 days. The other results divide by a wrong daily dose.
  30. The correct answer is D (Wait until 28 days after the MMR, so he can be vaccinated in 19 days): Live parenteral vaccines not given on the same day should be separated by at least 28 days to avoid immune interference. That means waiting 19 more days from day 9. Shorter gaps risk a weaker response, while six months is unnecessary.
  31. The correct answer is D (Add ezetimibe 10 mg daily and recheck lipids in four to twelve weeks): When LDL remains above target on a maximally tolerated statin, the Canadian Cardiovascular Society recommends adding ezetimibe first because it is inexpensive, well tolerated and lowers LDL by roughly 15 to 20%. A PCSK9 inhibitor is reserved for patients who stay above target despite both agents. Fenofibrate and niacin do not reduce cardiovascular events when added to a statin.
  32. The correct answer is C (refer him to a physician promptly for assessment rather than selling an over-the-counter cough product): A cough lasting more than three weeks with unintentional weight loss in a long-term smoker is a red flag for lung cancer, tuberculosis or other serious disease. Symptom treatment would delay diagnosis. Referral for a clinical assessment, usually including chest imaging, is the appropriate action.
  33. The correct answer is B (Revise the sign so it states only the claims Health Canada authorized on the licence): Health Canada authorizes the claims for a natural health product, and advertising that goes beyond the licence is misleading and breaches both federal law and professional standards. A small print disclaimer does not cure a claim the product is not authorized to make. The pharmacist-manager remains accountable for marketing within the pharmacy, and making the same claims verbally would be equally improper.
  34. The correct answer is D (Record it in the program, since near misses reveal system weaknesses before patients are harmed): A near miss is an error caught before reaching the patient and is one of the richest sources of learning, so it should be recorded in the reporting program. Reporting is meant to find system causes, not to blame an individual or wait for a pattern of harm. The look-alike drug names metoprolol and metformin are a known source of confusion worth tracking.
  35. The correct answer is A (Cefazolin can be given, since its side chain is unlike those of amoxicillin and ampicillin): Cross reactivity between penicillins and cephalosporins depends mainly on shared R1 side chains rather than the beta-lactam ring. Cefazolin has a unique side chain that does not match amoxicillin, so the risk is very low and it remains the preferred surgical prophylaxis. Halving the dose adds no safety and compromises prophylaxis.
  36. The correct answer is C (Recognize the dose as off-label, review supporting primary evidence, and document the rationale clearly): The product monograph defines the Health Canada approved use, so a higher dose is off-label even if a tertiary source lists it. Off-label dosing is lawful and common in refractory epilepsy when supported by evidence and agreed with the prescriber. The pharmacist should verify the source data and document the reasoning and monitoring plan.
  37. The correct answer is C (5.5 mL, which provides about 176 mg and is close to 15 mg/kg): A 15 mg/kg dose for 12 kg is 180 mg. At 32 mg per mL that is 5.6 mL, which rounds to 5.5 mL. The other volumes correspond to 10, 20 or 30 mg/kg and would under or overdose her.
  38. The correct answer is D (Clarify the reaction details and any past cephalexin use with VW before deciding to dispense): Cross-reactivity between penicillin and cephalexin is possible, especially with similar side chains, and VW's history is uncertain. Collecting detail about the reaction and prior exposure allows an informed decision, which may include contacting the prescriber. Blindly dispensing or labelling a whole class as an allergy are both unsupported.
  39. The correct answer is A (Verify it with the prescriber through independently confirmed contact details before dispensing anything): Altered quantities and an unverifiable contact number are classic signs of a forged narcotic prescription. The pharmacist should confirm it using the prescriber's number from a trusted source such as the college register, because the number printed on the form may be false. Dispensing a partial supply or accepting a voicemail still releases a narcotic on an unverified order.
  40. The correct answer is A (Contact the prescriber today, because this count calls for a clozapine management review): A fall of this size in the neutrophil count, to below 1.5 x 10^9/L in a patient without benign ethnic neutropenia, needs a same-day clozapine review and closer repeat counts. Waiting for the next monthly visit risks progression to agranulocytosis. Abrupt discontinuation risks rebound psychosis, and olanzapine is not a safe equal swap.
  41. The correct answer is D (Hold the lithium and arrange urgent medical assessment, with repeat level and kidney tests): A level of 1.9 mmol/L with neurological signs indicates moderate lithium toxicity, made worse by dehydration. The drug must be held and the patient assessed urgently for fluids, monitoring and possible dialysis. Thiazide diuretics reduce lithium excretion and would worsen toxicity.
  42. The correct answer is C (A narcotic prescription cannot be refilled, so each further supply needs a new prescription): Under the Narcotic Control Regulations a prescription for a narcotic such as oxycodone cannot be refilled, whatever the prescriber writes. A prescriber who wants further supply must issue a new prescription, or instructions to dispense in portions. Phone confirmation or an agreement does not create refill authority.
  43. The correct answer is D (Take it with food, since nausea usually fades within one to two weeks): Nausea is the most common early effect of duloxetine and usually settles within one to two weeks, and taking the capsule with food helps. Stopping is unnecessary for a mild, expected effect. Increasing the dose early would likely worsen nausea, and food does not meaningfully impair absorption.
  44. The correct answer is C (It shows Health Canada reviewed the product and authorized it for sale in Canada): A Natural Product Number or homeopathic DIN-HM means Health Canada assessed the product for safety, efficacy under its claims and quality before licensing it. It does not guarantee a cure and it is not a lot number or a provincial listing. Authorization depends on the evidence level of the claim.
  45. The correct answer is B (Low albumin leaves more phenytoin unbound, so the free level exceeds what the total shows): Phenytoin is highly protein bound, and only the unbound fraction is active. With low albumin the unbound fraction rises, so a total level in the usual range can hide a toxic free concentration, which fits her ataxia and nystagmus. The usual response is to measure a free level or correct the total for albumin.
  46. The correct answer is A (State the objection briefly without judgment, avoid imposing beliefs, and help LS reach another provider): A pharmacist may decline to provide a service but should not impose personal beliefs or judge the patient. A brief, respectful statement and help reaching another provider protects both the pharmacist's conscience and the patient's access. Giving a long moral explanation or hiding the objection by implying other reasons is not acceptable.
  47. The correct answer is B (a high-intensity statin and a single antiplatelet such as low-dose aspirin or clopidogrel): Symptomatic PAD is a coronary-equivalent risk state, so a high-intensity statin and one antiplatelet are the foundation of care. Cilostazol and pentoxifylline may help walking distance but do not reduce cardiovascular events, and adding warfarin raises bleeding without clear benefit.
  48. The correct answer is C (Metformin, ramipril and empagliflozin, restarting after 24 to 48 hours of normal eating and drinking): Sick day guidance holds drugs that raise the risk of kidney injury, lactic acidosis or ketoacidosis during dehydration: metformin, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics, NSAIDs, sulfonylureas and SGLT2 inhibitors. Basal insulin is continued because stopping it risks ketosis. Statins and amlodipine are not on the list, so holding them is not needed.
