  49. The correct answer is D (84 mg, calculated as the target concentration multiplied by the total volume of distribution): Loading dose equals target concentration times volume of distribution. The total volume is 0.6 L/kg x 70 kg = 42 L, so 2 mg/L x 42 L = 84 mg. Using weight alone ignores how widely the drug distributes, dividing weight by the volume term is not a valid dose calculation, and halving the volume understates the dose.
  50. The correct answer is A (Mix 27 g of the 10% ointment with 63 g of white petrolatum until uniform): A 3% w/w strength in 90 g needs 2.7 g of salicylic acid. The 10% ointment contains 0.1 g per gram, so 27 g supplies 2.7 g, and the remaining 63 g is the diluent base. Using 30 g overshoots to about 3.3%, and using 2.7 g of the stock treats the active amount as the amount of ointment.
  51. The correct answer is C (Three hours late, and then she should use condoms for the next two days): Norethindrone progestin-only pills depend on a stable effect on cervical mucus, so the window is only 3 hours. A pill taken more than 3 hours late is treated as missed, and backup is advised for 48 hours. Combined pills have a much more forgiving window, which is why the two rules are often confused.
  52. The correct answer is A (A levonorgestrel 52 mg intrauterine system, which lowers menstrual blood loss over several months): The levonorgestrel intrauterine system thins the endometrium, cutting menstrual blood loss substantially and often causing light bleeding or amenorrhea. A copper IUD usually increases bleeding and cramping. The implant causes unpredictable bleeding, and a daily pill does not meet her request for no daily attention.
  53. The correct answer is A (suggest azithromycin instead, because clarithromycin markedly raises simvastatin exposure and myopathy risk): Clarithromycin is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, and simvastatin should not be combined with it because of rhabdomyolysis risk. Azithromycin has little CYP3A4 effect. Separating the doses does not stop enzyme inhibition, and coenzyme Q10 does not prevent the interaction.
  54. The correct answer is B (Take ciprofloxacin at least 2 hours before, or 6 hours after, the supplement): Calcium and iron chelate ciprofloxacin in the gut and can cut absorption sharply. Separating the doses by at least 2 hours before or 6 hours after the supplement keeps the antibiotic available. Taking them together or only 30 minutes apart does not avoid the binding, and stopping the supplement afterward does nothing for the doses already taken.
  55. The correct answer is B (Disclose the honorarium and sponsorship to attendees and present balanced evidence including alternative anticoagulants): Disclosing the honorarium and sponsorship lets the audience weigh the content, and balanced evidence including alternatives protects independence. Letting the sponsor approve content or presenting only the sponsor's data allows commercial influence over education. Hiding the payment removes transparency even when the facts are accurate.
  56. The correct answer is C (1 mg nightly, which is one 1 mg tablet taken at bedtime): A 25% reduction of the 2 mg starting dose is 0.5 mg, so she takes 1.5 mg in weeks 1 and 2 and 1 mg in weeks 3 and 4. Each step is 0.5 mg because it is based on the starting dose, not the current dose. Whole tablet combinations are used so she does not need to split tablets.
  57. The correct answer is C (Stop glyburide and substitute a DPP-4 inhibitor, which has a low hypoglycemia risk): His A1c is below the relaxed target suited to an older, frail adult, and he is having hypoglycemia, so the priority is to remove the drug causing harm. Glyburide has active metabolites that accumulate with reduced renal function, and other sulfonylureas carry similar risk. A DPP-4 inhibitor lowers glucose with little hypoglycemia, and adding more drugs to chase a tighter target would be the wrong direction.
  58. The correct answer is D (Start a daily low dose inhaled corticosteroid, with salbutamol kept for rescue use): Frequent morning cough and reliever use more than twice weekly show inadequate control in a school age child. A daily low dose inhaled corticosteroid is the preferred first controller. Long-acting beta agonists should not be used alone because they lack anti-inflammatory action, and monthly oral steroids carry unnecessary systemic effects.
  59. The correct answer is D (The patient judges whether care felt safe, and providers reflect on power imbalances and biases): Cultural safety, as described in Canadian health care, shifts the focus from the provider's knowledge to the patient's experience, and it requires providers to recognize power imbalances, colonial history and their own biases. Learning lists of traits promotes stereotypes, identical treatment ignores individual needs, and a single course cannot certify anyone because cultural safety is an ongoing practice.
  60. The correct answer is C (About 50 patients like KL need treatment over five years to prevent one event): The relative risk reduction is 20%, so the absolute risk reduction is 10% times 0.20, which is 2%. The number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.02, or 50. Using the relative risk reduction directly, or the baseline risk itself, ignores the patient's own starting risk.
  61. The correct answer is C (Move to a quiet area, face him directly and speak clearly at a normal pace): Reducing background noise, facing the patient so he can lip read and speaking clearly at a normal pace gives the best chance of being understood. Shouting and exaggerated lip movements distort speech, and bypassing the patient removes his role in his own care.
  62. The correct answer is B (Non-inferiority was met, and superiority is also supported because the upper limit is below zero): A lower pressure is better, so negative differences favour the new drug. The upper confidence limit of minus 2.0 is below the margin and also below zero, so the new drug is non-inferior and statistically better. A large difference in the favourable direction does not breach a non-inferiority margin.
  63. The correct answer is C (Attrition was 17% versus 9%, an imbalance that may bias results and needs explaining): Dividing withdrawals by 500 gives 17% and 9%. An eight point difference between arms suggests that dropout is related to treatment, for example through adverse effects, which can bias the comparison. Intention to treat does not repair missing outcome data on patients who left.
  64. The correct answer is C (Reduce the digoxin dose by 30 to 50% and monitor serum levels and heart rate): Amiodarone inhibits P-glycoprotein and renal clearance of digoxin, commonly raising levels by about 70 to 100%. Empirically lowering the dose by 30 to 50% with monitoring prevents toxicity. Raising the dose would worsen exposure, and the combination is used in practice with dose reduction.
  65. The correct answer is D (Suggest raising the antihistamine to four times the usual daily dose, with prescriber oversight): Guidelines for chronic urticaria advise updosing a non-sedating antihistamine up to four times the licensed dose when standard dosing fails, before considering add-on biologic therapy. Long steroid courses cause harm, and sedating antihistamines or a leukotriene antagonist alone are less supported.
  66. The correct answer is A (Refer for a copper intrauterine device, since enzyme inducers weaken hormonal emergency contraception): Carbamazepine induces CYP3A4, which speeds the breakdown of both levonorgestrel and ulipristal and can reduce their effect. A copper device is not affected by enzyme inducers and remains the most effective method at 60 hours. Saying that no method works is incorrect, because the copper device remains reliable.
  67. The correct answer is D (Confirm the response was useful and learn the outcome or any change in patient care): Follow-up closes the loop by checking that the answer met the need and by learning whether it changed care, which also improves future responses. Re-running searches, billing and sending reference lists may happen in some settings but are not the purpose of this step.
  68. The correct answer is D (At least every six months, and also after relocation or major repair of the cabinet): NAPRA sterile compounding standards require primary engineering controls to be certified by a qualified person at least every six months, and again after being moved or repaired. Annual certification is a common assumption but is less frequent than the standard demands. Surface sampling supports scheduled certification and does not replace it.
  69. The correct answer is B (Confirms the patient takes the prescribed dose, reducing diversion and risk during early stabilization): Witnessed ingestion lets the pharmacist see the dose taken, which improves safety while the dose is titrated and lowers the chance of diversion. It does not change absorption or kinetics, taste cannot verify a dose, and the pharmacy still records each dose it observes.
  70. The correct answer is A (A dose of 400 mg IV, infused over 30 minutes and repeated every 24 hours): The calculation is 5 mg/kg x 80 kg = 400 mg given once daily. The 240 mg and 8 hour pattern reflects traditional dosing at 3 mg/kg, and 560 mg would be 7 mg/kg. The 80 mg figure is only 1 mg/kg and would be subtherapeutic.
  71. The correct answer is C (Steroids do not maintain remission, so a steroid-sparing agent such as a biologic is needed): Systemic corticosteroids induce remission but do not maintain it, and prolonged use causes bone loss, cataracts, diabetes and infection risk. Steroid dependence is an indication to add a thiopurine, methotrexate or a biologic. Inhaled corticosteroids have no role in treating Crohn disease of the bowel.
  72. The correct answer is B (Confirm her therapy in the provincial profile and dispense a limited emergency supply): Continuity of care provisions let pharmacists provide a limited supply of an established therapy to prevent interruption, ideally after verifying the history through the provincial profile or the home pharmacy. A short supply bridges her until normal access is restored. Withholding chronic therapy risks harm, while a large supply goes beyond emergency need.
  73. The correct answer is C (A topical azole antifungal such as clotrimazole 1% at each diaper change, under barrier cream): Satellite pustules and fold involvement after antibiotics point to candidal diaper dermatitis, which needs a topical azole plus a barrier product. Hydrocortisone is not recommended in the diaper area of infants because occlusion increases absorption and may worsen candidiasis. More zinc oxide only treats irritant rash.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Verify the strength, the once weekly frequency and the quantity against the original prescription): High-alert drugs need the pharmacist to independently compare strength, frequency and quantity with the original prescription, because weekly methotrexate dosing errors have caused fatal toxicity when the drug was taken daily. Relying on another person's initials or checking only demographics cannot detect a wrong frequency. Matching the drug name alone also misses strength and directions errors.
  2. The correct answer is D (ANOVA compares all three means at once, limiting the inflated chance of false positives): Running three separate t-tests gives three chances for a false positive, which inflates the overall type I error rate above 5%. A one-way ANOVA tests for any difference among the three means in a single test. The outcome is continuous, ANOVA assumes approximately normal data, and it does not adjust for baseline differences.
  3. The correct answer is B (Take a verbal prescription from the prescriber, document it, and fill a suitable quantity): A prescription for a narcotic cannot be transferred between pharmacies. Federal rules allow a pharmacist to accept a verbal narcotic prescription directly from the prescriber and record it. Supplying on the patient's word would not meet the legal requirement for a valid prescription.
  4. The correct answer is A (Diltiazem, because it controls heart rate without the bronchospasm risk of beta-blockers): Beta-blockers are first-line for thyrotoxic symptoms but can provoke bronchospasm in severe persistent asthma with a recent exacerbation, so a rate-limiting calcium channel blocker such as diltiazem is the accepted alternative. Cardioselectivity is relative and is lost at higher doses. Digoxin is less effective in thyrotoxicosis because of faster clearance and altered sensitivity.
  5. The correct answer is A (Stop methotrexate and seek urgent medical assessment with blood counts for suspected toxicity): Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole reduces methotrexate clearance and adds antifolate effect, which can rapidly cause pancytopenia, mouth ulcers, fever and rash. This needs methotrexate stopped and urgent blood counts. Folic acid, a topical steroid or an antibiotic switch cannot treat marrow toxicity already present.
  6. The correct answer is B (Inactivated vaccines are safe, but live vaccines should be avoided once immunosuppression begins): Inactivated vaccines do not replicate and can be given to patients on immunosuppressants, although responses may be lower. Live vaccines such as varicella and yellow fever risk disseminated infection once infliximab and a thiopurine are started, so any needed live vaccine should be given beforehand, or avoided. Withholding inactivated vaccines leaves him unprotected without any benefit.
  7. The correct answer is D (Call the prescriber about choosing another antibiotic, since both interactions carry serious clinical risk): Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole markedly raises INR with warfarin and adds to hyperkalemia risk with an ACE inhibitor in an older adult whose potassium is already high. Both are significant, so an alternative antibiotic should be requested promptly. Holding warfarin or waiting unnecessarily creates new risks.
  8. The correct answer is D (The result needs confirmatory testing, since most positives at this prevalence are false positives): At a prevalence of 0.1%, about 100 of every 100,000 people are infected, giving about 100 true positives, while the 98% specificity produces nearly 2,000 false positives. Most positives are therefore false and the positive predictive value is under 5%. Confirmatory testing is needed before a diagnosis is accepted.
  9. The correct answer is B (CrCl is about 29 mL/min, so a reduction to 30 mg daily should be discussed): CrCl = (140 - 79) x 58 x 1.04 / 125 = about 29 mL/min. Below 30 mL/min, enoxaparin accumulates, and the prophylaxis dose is usually reduced to 30 mg once daily. Raising the dose would increase bleeding risk, and the other CrCl values come from calculation errors.
  10. The correct answer is A (He misses the usual criteria of BMI 30, or BMI 27 with a comorbidity): Anti-obesity drugs are indicated for a BMI of 30 kg/m2 or higher, or 27 kg/m2 or higher with at least one weight-related comorbidity such as hypertension or dyslipidemia. His BMI is 28 with no comorbidity, so he falls outside the labelled criteria. Overweight alone does not justify prescription therapy.
  11. The correct answer is B (Offer naloxone and counselling on combined risks, then contact the prescriber about the early refill): High dose opioids combined with a benzodiazepine and alcohol multiply the risk of respiratory depression and overdose, and a lost supply with low mood also raises concern. Naloxone and counselling reduce immediate harm, and the prescriber decides about the early refill. Simply dispensing or refusing and abandoning him addresses neither safety nor continuity of care.
  12. The correct answer is A (Tell the prescriber a second first-line trial is required, and discuss alternatives or an exception request): Limited Use criteria are clinical conditions of coverage, so the pharmacist communicates them to the prescriber, who can try the second agent or request an exception. Billing under another DIN is misrepresentation of the product dispensed. Simply charging cash ignores a covered route.
  13. The correct answer is A (Senna 8.6 mg, two tablets at bedtime, with polyethylene glycol added if needed): Opioids slow gut motility, so a stimulant laxative such as senna, with an osmotic agent if needed, is the usual first-line approach. Bulk-forming fibre needs adequate fluid and motility and often worsens opioid constipation. Docusate has little evidence of benefit, and there is no need to lower the opioid dose first.
  14. The correct answer is B (Reduce about 10 percent of the daily dose every two to four weeks): After long term use, a slow taper of about 10 percent of the daily dose every two to four weeks lowers the risk of withdrawal, pain flare and relapse, and can be paused if needed. Rapid tapers or abrupt discontinuation cause intense withdrawal and can lead to patients seeking illicit opioids.
  15. The correct answer is B (Register with the Nova Scotia College of Pharmacists, because each province licenses pharmacists separately): Pharmacy is regulated provincially, so a licence from one province does not authorize practice in another, even though mobility agreements speed up registration. Health Canada does not issue pharmacist licences, and no transition period allows practice before registration is granted.
  16. The correct answer is A (Suggest venlafaxine instead, because paroxetine inhibits CYP2D6 and lowers the activation of tamoxifen): Tamoxifen needs CYP2D6 to form its active metabolite endoxifen, and paroxetine is a strong inhibitor that may reduce its benefit. Venlafaxine has little CYP2D6 effect and reduces hot flashes. Fluoxetine also inhibits CYP2D6, and systemic estrogen is generally avoided after breast cancer.
  17. The correct answer is C (Bismuth quadruple therapy: PPI, bismuth, metronidazole and tetracycline given for 14 days): Recent macrolide exposure raises the chance of clarithromycin resistance, so a clarithromycin regimen is avoided. Bismuth quadruple therapy for 14 days is the preferred first-line regimen in this situation. Acid suppression alone heals the ulcer but leaves the infection and the risk of relapse, and fluoroquinolone regimens are reserved for rescue after failure.
  18. The correct answer is C (This is probably croup, a viral illness that usually settles with supportive care at home): A barking cough, hoarseness and stridor that appears with crying fit mild croup, which is viral and usually resolves with supportive care and, where prescribed, a single dose of dexamethasone. Epiglottitis would present with a toxic child who drools and sits upright. Bronchiolitis is not helped by routine bronchodilators.
  19. The correct answer is B (About twelve hours after the last dose, once steady state is reached after five days): Lithium has a half-life of about 18 to 24 hours, so steady state takes about five days. Trough levels drawn 12 hours after the last dose are used for comparison with the reference range of 0.6 to 1.0 mmol/L for maintenance. Peak or early samples give misleadingly high or unstable values.
  20. The correct answer is B (2.67, calculated as 40 times 80 divided by the product of 60 and 20): The odds ratio is the odds of exposure in cases divided by the odds in controls: 40 over 60 is 0.667 and 20 over 80 is 0.25, giving 2.67. The cross-product 40 x 80 divided by 60 x 20 gives the same value. Dividing the two proportions gives 2.0, which is not an odds ratio.
  21. The correct answer is C (Clonazepam, because benzodiazepines are listed as targeted substances and carry special prescription and record rules): Benzodiazepines such as clonazepam, lorazepam and diazepam are the core group of targeted substances and carry the special prescription and record keeping rules. Beta-blockers, SSRIs and proton pump inhibitors are not listed under these regulations.
  22. The correct answer is A (At least two years from the date each transaction was recorded in the pharmacy): The regulations require records for targeted substances to be kept for a minimum of two years. Provinces and colleges may require longer retention of prescription records, but the federal minimum is two years. The other periods do not match the federal rule.
  23. The correct answer is C (About 3.1 g daily, which exceeds her limit, so reduce the intake now): Two 325 mg tablets four times daily gives 2,600 mg, and the bedtime 500 mg tablet brings the total to 3,100 mg, well above the 2,000 mg limit. Stopping the bedtime tablet alone would still leave 2,600 mg. She needs to cut back the cold product and find a lower acetaminophen plan.
  24. The correct answer is A (The checklist improved safety but added a workload cost, so adapt it before wider adoption): The Study stage compares results with the prediction and looks at both outcome and balancing measures. Missed allergy documentation fell by more than half, which is a meaningful safety gain, but the longer admission time and overtime are an unintended cost. The Act stage should therefore adapt the process, for example by simplifying steps, rather than adopting or abandoning it unchanged.
  25. The correct answer is B (8 units: 6 units for the meal plus 2 units for correction): The meal dose is 60 g divided by 10 g per unit, or 6 units. Her glucose is 4 mmol/L above target, and at 2 mmol/L per unit the correction is 2 units. The total is 8 units, and correction is added whenever glucose is above target, not only above 14 mmol/L.
  26. The correct answer is A (Seek urgent emergency assessment, stop ramipril, and document ACE inhibitor angioedema in his record): Angioedema without urticaria that does not respond to antihistamines points to bradykinin-mediated ACE inhibitor angioedema, which can threaten the airway and can occur even after years of use. The drug must be stopped and urgent assessment arranged. Another ACE inhibitor carries the same class risk.
  27. The correct answer is A (Contact the prescribers to recommend cutting the azathioprine dose to about one quarter to one third): Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, which blocks the breakdown of the active thiopurine metabolite and can cause severe myelosuppression. The usual approach is to reduce azathioprine to roughly 25 to 33% of the dose and monitor blood counts closely. Separating doses does not prevent the enzyme inhibition, and a one-year delay in monitoring is unsafe.
  28. The correct answer is D (Start a phosphate binder with meals and counsel on limiting dietary phosphate from processed foods): Hyperphosphatemia is the priority and is managed with dietary phosphate limitation and a binder taken with meals so it traps phosphate in the gut. His PTH is within an acceptable range for stage 4 CKD and calcium is normal, so a vitamin D analogue is not needed and could worsen phosphate. Diuretics do not meaningfully clear phosphate.
  29. The correct answer is D (Wait until about 14:00 for acetaminophen, and stop the duplicate cold syrup product): The cold syrup contains acetaminophen, so the last dose was at 10:00 and the minimum interval of four hours means the next dose is not due until 14:00. The daily total is not the issue, but the spacing is. Combination cold products are also not recommended under six years of age and duplicate the active ingredient.
  30. The correct answer is B (Docosanol 10% cream started at once and applied five times daily until the lesion heals): Topical antivirals work best when started during the tingling stage, and docosanol 10% cream applied five times daily can modestly shorten healing time. Hydrocortisone does not stop viral replication, and zinc rinses are not supported for this use. Lysine has weak evidence and has not been shown to treat an active episode.
  31. The correct answer is A (Recount, reconcile all records and receipts for an error, and report a loss if unexplained): A discrepancy must be investigated through the records before any conclusion, and a shortage that stays unexplained is a loss reportable to Health Canada. Silently matching the book to the shelf hides a possible diversion. Police involvement alone skips the federal report and the pharmacy's own reconciliation.
  32. The correct answer is A (About 1 additional patient in 200 treated for six months would develop the elevation): An NNH of 200 means that, on average, one additional patient in 200 treated for the trial period would develop the adverse effect. It is tied to the six-month time frame and is not a number of months or a monthly rate. The harm is uncommon but real, so monitoring is still sensible.
  33. The correct answer is C (Remove the efficacy claim and photograph, leaving only the drug name, price and quantity): Federal drug regulations limit public advertising of a prescription drug to its name, price and quantity, so therapeutic claims and promotional images are not allowed. A disclaimer, a trial citation or a side effect list still leaves the promotional material in place. Only removing the claim and photograph brings the poster into compliance.
  34. The correct answer is D (Converted rapidly to morphine, which can reach the infant through breast milk and cause sedation): CYP2D6 converts codeine to morphine, and ultrarapid metabolizers make unusually large amounts, which pass into milk and have caused serious infant respiratory depression and death. Health Canada advises avoiding codeine in nursing mothers for this reason. Slow conversion describes poor metabolizers, who get less analgesia instead.
  35. The correct answer is D (Bismuth harmlessly darkens stools and the tongue, so he should continue the course): Bismuth subsalicylate reacts with sulfur in the gut and saliva to form bismuth sulfide, which darkens stools and sometimes the tongue without harm. Bleeding would usually bring pain, weakness, dizziness or vomiting, and the stool would be tarry and sticky. Stopping early risks treatment failure and antibiotic resistance.
  36. The correct answer is C (continue low molecular weight heparin, since oral anticoagulants cause more bleeding with unresected gastrointestinal tumours): Trials of direct oral anticoagulants in cancer showed more major gastrointestinal bleeding in patients with unresected gastric or gastroesophageal tumours, so injectable heparin is safer here. Warfarin is less effective than heparin in cancer, and withholding treatment leaves a symptomatic clot untreated.
  37. The correct answer is C (Give one puff into the spacer, then have the child take 5 to 6 normal breaths): A young child cannot hold a breath on command, so the mask is sealed on the face and the child breathes normally for 5 to 6 breaths after each single puff. Multiple puffs together reduce delivery, and a loose mask lets drug escape.
  38. The correct answer is A (Check the INR within three to five days of starting the antibiotic): Antibiotics can reduce vitamin K producing gut flora and the infection itself can alter warfarin response, so the INR can rise within days. An early check at three to five days catches this while the course is still running. Empirical dose cuts risk subtherapeutic anticoagulation, and waiting until the course ends misses the period of greatest risk.
  39. The correct answer is A (Quarantine the vaccines in the fridge, label them do not use, and call public health): Vaccines exposed to an excursion should be isolated at 2 to 8 degrees and not used until the manufacturer or public health reviews the temperature and time data. Some products tolerate a short excursion and others do not, so the decision belongs with the people holding stability data. Discarding costly stock without that advice may be unnecessary.
  40. The correct answer is D (The directions give about twice the prescribed dose and must be corrected to 4.8 mL): The prescribed daily dose is 40 x 18 = 720 mg, or 240 mg per dose, which equals 4.8 mL of the 250 mg/5 mL suspension. The labelled 10 mL gives 500 mg per dose, roughly double the intended amount. At 4.8 mL three times daily for 10 days the course needs 144 mL, so one 150 mL bottle is adequate once the directions are fixed.
  41. The correct answer is A (Counsel her directly, then add large print labels, tactile markers and simpler dosing): The patient remains the person who takes the medicines, so she should receive counselling directly and adaptations such as large print, tactile cues and fewer dosing times. Relying on a neighbour risks errors and privacy loss, and written sheets are useless to her.
Case CHB7

SK is a 63 year old man discharged after a non ST elevation myocardial infarction treated with a drug-eluting stent in the left anterior descending artery. Weight 82 kg, eGFR 74 mL/min. He has hypertension, type 2 diabetes and reflux, with no prior stroke or bleeding. Home drugs: ramipril 10 mg daily, metformin 1000 mg twice daily, omeprazole 20 mg daily. New discharge drugs: acetylsalicylic acid 81 mg daily, clopidogrel 75 mg daily, atorvastatin 80 mg daily, metoprolol 50 mg twice daily.

  1. The correct answer is B (Replace omeprazole with pantoprazole, which has a lesser effect on clopidogrel activation): Omeprazole and esomeprazole inhibit CYP2C19, the enzyme that converts clopidogrel to its active form, so antiplatelet effect can fall. Pantoprazole has minimal CYP2C19 inhibition and still protects the stomach during dual antiplatelet therapy. Separating the doses does not help because the inhibition persists, and atorvastatin and metoprolol do not reduce clopidogrel activation.
  2. The correct answer is C (About twelve months, followed by long-term acetylsalicylic acid alone if no bleeding occurs): After an acute coronary syndrome, guidelines advise twelve months of dual antiplatelet therapy with a P2Y12 inhibitor and acetylsalicylic acid, then continued acetylsalicylic acid alone. Shorter courses are considered only when bleeding risk is high, which SK does not show. Extending to three years is not the default for a patient without very high ischemic risk.
  3. The correct answer is C (Postpone the elective repair until dual therapy is complete, and continue acetylsalicylic acid meanwhile): Stopping a P2Y12 inhibitor in the first weeks to months after a drug-eluting stent for an acute coronary syndrome carries a high risk of stent thrombosis, so a purely elective operation should wait until the course is finished. Acetylsalicylic acid is continued whenever possible. Bridging with enoxaparin does not protect a stent.
  4. The correct answer is A (Use a qualified interpreter or language line, then confirm his understanding with teach-back): Counselling on a high-alert drug needs accurate and confidential communication, which a trained interpreter or language line provides and a child cannot reliably give. Teach-back checks that the bleeding signs and diet points were actually understood. A leaflet in English alone does not meet his need, and delaying dispensing leaves him without therapy.
  5. The correct answer is B (Same day assessment for oral antiviral therapy, and ask the prescriber whether to delay etanercept): Herpes zoster in a patient on a biologic and methotrexate needs prompt oral antiviral therapy, ideally within 72 hours of rash onset, and the prescriber decides whether to hold the next etanercept dose. Symptomatic care alone delays treatment. A live vaccine is contraindicated during an outbreak, and a steroid cream does not treat viral infection.
  6. The correct answer is D (Start when bleeding begins and take it regularly for two to three days): NSAIDs work by lowering prostaglandin production, so they are most effective when started at the onset of bleeding or pain and taken on a regular schedule for the first two to three days. Waiting until pain is severe lets prostaglandin levels peak. Taking it with food reduces stomach irritation and does not reduce benefit.
  7. The correct answer is A (Contact the prescriber, since she meets two dose reduction criteria for 2.5 mg twice daily): Apixaban is reduced to 2.5 mg twice daily when a patient with atrial fibrillation has at least two of: age 80 or older, weight 60 kg or less, serum creatinine 133 umol/L or higher. She meets two (52 kg and 140 umol/L), so the prescribed dose is too high. Dabigatran depends more on renal clearance, and splitting tablets does not replace a corrected prescription.
Case CHB8

ZM is a 71 year old woman in a long term care home. She weighs 58 kg, her serum creatinine is 150 umol/L and her potassium is 5.3 mmol/L. She had a rash after trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole in the past. Her medications are ramipril 5 mg daily, spironolactone 25 mg daily and acetaminophen as needed. A covering physician has faxed a prescription for trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole double strength twice daily for seven days for a suspected urinary tract infection.

  1. The correct answer is D (The documented rash after the same sulfonamide antibiotic that has been prescribed): A documented hypersensitivity reaction to the same drug is the clearest barrier to giving it. The raised potassium with ramipril and spironolactone, and her reduced kidney function, add real risk that calls for monitoring or a different agent, but they are relative concerns rather than the strongest reason. The seven day course length is not a problem in itself.
  2. The correct answer is A (I am calling about ZM, who has an order for this antibiotic despite a sulfonamide rash): The Situation states who is calling, the patient and the immediate problem in one or two sentences. Her medication history is Background, the judgement about risk is Assessment, and the proposed alternative is the Recommendation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Hold the order, suggest a non-sulfonamide alternative and document the conversation): A documented reaction outweighs a vague recollection of tolerance, so the pharmacist should not dispense and instead offers a safe alternative while keeping the exchange professional and recorded. Patient consent does not remove the pharmacist's responsibility, and blaming the physician to staff damages collaboration.
  4. The correct answer is D (Ibuprofen can block ASA's antiplatelet effect, so take ASA at least 30 minutes before ibuprofen): Ibuprofen occupies the same cyclooxygenase site as ASA and can prevent ASA from irreversibly acetylating platelet COX-1, which reduces cardioprotection. Taking immediate release ASA about 30 minutes ahead of ibuprofen lets it bind ahead of ibuprofen. Enteric coating does not solve the problem and may worsen it.
  5. The correct answer is C (Do not fill it, document the call, and cancel the faxed prescription after confirming with prescriber): Duplicate supply of an antibiotic to a child wastes drug and risks double dosing. The pharmacist confirms with the prescriber, documents the communication and cancels the duplicate. Pharmacies are expected to communicate to prevent duplicate fills.
  6. The correct answer is A (stopping the decongestant spray and starting an intranasal corticosteroid to manage the congestion): Topical decongestants used beyond about three to five days cause rebound mucosal swelling, called rhinitis medicamentosa. The treatment is to stop the spray, and an intranasal corticosteroid helps settle the swelling while the mucosa recovers. Another topical decongestant keeps the cycle going, and an antihistamine does not reverse this vascular rebound.
  7. The correct answer is B (toward the ground until a visible cloud appears, then repeat the step three more times): A new soft mist inhaler must be primed when first used so the spray is reliable: actuate toward the ground until a cloud is seen, then repeat three more times. A single puff or a single repeat is too little priming for a new cartridge. Waiting a day has no purpose, and priming after three days of non-use is a separate, shorter instruction for a device already in use.
  8. The correct answer is D (Provide supplies without judgement or conditions, offer a sharps container, and share service information): Harm reduction meets people where they are, so supplies are provided without conditions, moral judgement, or required treatment participation. Unconditional access reduces sharing of equipment and infections such as HIV and hepatitis C, and a trusting relationship makes later referrals more likely. Strict one for one exchange and mandatory disclosure reduce access and trust.
  9. The correct answer is A (Non-Insured Health Benefits program administered by Indigenous Services Canada for eligible First Nations and Inuit): NIHB, run by Indigenous Services Canada, covers listed prescription drugs, dental care and other benefits for eligible registered First Nations and recognized Inuit people. Veterans Affairs Canada and the Interim Federal Health Program serve other populations, namely veterans, and refugees and claimants. The Canada Health Act does not itself cover outpatient prescription drugs, so the provincial route is not the answer here.
  10. The correct answer is D (The Canadian product monograph, available through the Compendium of Pharmaceutical Specialties or e-CPS): The product monograph is the Health Canada approved document describing indications, contraindications, strengths and dosage forms. The Compendium of Pharmaceutical Specialties makes these monographs accessible in Canada. Tertiary databases and reviews summarize other information and do not replace approved labelling.
  11. The correct answer is A (Draw 5.25 mL, because the 2% vial contains 20 mg of lidocaine per mL): The dose is 1.5 x 70 = 105 mg. A 2% solution holds 2 g per 100 mL, which is 20 mg per mL, so 105 / 20 = 5.25 mL. Drawing 10.5 mL from the 2% vial would deliver 210 mg, double the ordered dose and a serious risk of lidocaine toxicity.
  12. The correct answer is B (No more than 100 mg further, which brings the 24 hour total to 300 mg): He has taken 200 mg so far, and 300 mg minus 200 mg leaves 100 mg available within the 24 hour window. Every dose counts toward the daily total, so the full amount cannot be restarted. Two 100 mg doses fall short of the monograph maximum, and no halving rule applies.
  13. The correct answer is B (limit simvastatin to 20 mg daily with amlodipine, or choose atorvastatin or rosuvastatin instead): Amlodipine raises simvastatin exposure modestly through CYP3A4, and the simvastatin dose is capped at 20 mg daily with it. Atorvastatin and rosuvastatin are practical alternatives that avoid the cap. The 10 mg cap belongs to other interacting drugs, not amlodipine.
  14. The correct answer is C (Start a bisphosphonate with calcium and vitamin D, because his fracture means high risk): A hip fragility fracture places him in the high-risk category regardless of bone density, and glucocorticoid exposure of three months or more adds to that risk. Pharmacotherapy with a bisphosphonate plus calcium and vitamin D is indicated. Raloxifene is approved only for postmenopausal women, so it does not apply to him.
  15. The correct answer is B (Keep bisoprolol at the same dose, increase the diuretic, and delay titration until euvolemic): A mild fluid increase early after starting a beta-blocker is usually managed with more diuretic while the beta-blocker is kept at its current dose. Titration should wait until he is euvolemic. Stopping the drug removes a mortality-lowering therapy unnecessarily, and increasing it during congestion risks worsening decompensation.
  16. The correct answer is C (A product identification number (PIN) assigned for billing of items without a DIN): A DIN is assigned by Health Canada to drug products. For items such as supplies or services that do not have one, plans accept a product identification number for claims. A natural product number applies to natural health products, and lot numbers or barcodes are not claim identifiers.
  17. The correct answer is C (small differences in blood concentration can cause serious toxicity or loss of therapeutic effect): Critical dose drugs have a narrow gap between a dose that works and a dose that harms, so even a modest difference in exposure matters clinically. Tighter limits narrow the allowed difference between generic and brand. The tighter standard is not due to batch size, special fillers or erratic absorption.
  18. The correct answer is D (Inform the surgical team and advise stopping ginkgo, garlic and St John's wort as directed): Ginkgo and garlic may increase bleeding and St John's wort alters drug metabolism, so these are usually stopped one to two weeks before surgery as directed by the surgical team. Beta-blockers are normally continued perioperatively and stopping them abruptly can cause rebound tachycardia. All products should be disclosed.
  19. The correct answer is C (used once daily throughout the pollen season, with benefit building over several days): Intranasal corticosteroids suppress the inflammatory response and reach full effect over days, so regular daily use during exposure works better than as-needed use. They do relieve congestion on their own, and the labelled dose is not raised on bad days. Expecting relief within minutes leads to disappointment and poor adherence.
  20. The correct answer is B (Metoclopramide blocks central dopamine receptors and can worsen his motor symptoms, so ondansetron is preferred): Metoclopramide crosses the blood-brain barrier and antagonizes dopamine D2 receptors, which counteracts levodopa and can sharply worsen rigidity and bradykinesia. It has no meaningful effect on carbidopa. Ondansetron controls nausea without dopamine blockade, so it is the better antiemetic here.
  21. The correct answer is B (Fluconazole 150 mg once weekly for six months, then reassess for recurrence): Recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis, defined as four or more episodes in a year, is treated with an induction course followed by weekly fluconazole 150 mg for six months. Daily dosing adds exposure without extra benefit, and treating each recurrence as it comes does not prevent further episodes. Recurrence is common after maintenance stops, so patients are reassessed.
  22. The correct answer is C (Publication bias is likely, so the pooled estimate probably overstates the supplement's true benefit): An asymmetric funnel with missing small negative studies is the classic sign of publication or reporting bias. Because the missing studies would have pulled the estimate toward no effect, the pooled benefit is probably inflated. Changing the statistical model does not recover studies that were never published.
  23. The correct answer is C (Fifty days, since each injection uses 28 units plus 2 priming units from 1500 units): Each day uses 28 units for the dose plus 2 units to prime, which is 30 units. Five pens hold 1500 units, and 1500 divided by 30 is 50 days, which also matches 10 days per 300 unit pen. Ignoring priming gives 53 days, subtracting it gives 57, and 140 days wrongly multiplies the 28 day in-use period by five pens.
  24. The correct answer is A (The positive predictive value falls as prevalence falls, so many positives will be false): Predictive values depend on prevalence even when sensitivity and specificity are fixed. In a low prevalence population, false positives from the many healthy people outnumber true positives, so the positive predictive value falls. Specificity is a property of the test and does not change with prevalence.
  25. The correct answer is A (Thirty tablets, calculated as two tablets three times daily across five days): A 1000 mg dose is two 500 mg tablets, taken three times daily, which is six tablets a day. Over five days this is 30 tablets. Using one tablet per dose or four doses per day does not match the order.
  26. The correct answer is A (Give a correction by pen, change the infusion set, and recheck in two hours): A persistent high despite a pump bolus with raised ketones suggests an occluded or failed infusion set, so insulin should be delivered by pen and the set changed. Reusing the same pump delays treatment. Stopping insulin would worsen ketone production, and a ketone level of 1.2 mmol/L in a well patient can be managed at home with close rechecking.
  27. The correct answer is C (Sedative hypnotics impair balance and alertness, raising fall risk, so tapering should be discussed): Zopiclone and other hypnotics cause next-day impairment of balance, reaction time, and cognition, and older adults are especially sensitive, so fall and hip fracture risk rises. Tolerance to these effects is incomplete. Increasing the dose would worsen the risk, and a gradual taper should be considered.
  28. The correct answer is D (a near miss that is still recorded and analysed for contributing factors): A near miss is an error caught before it reaches the patient. Documenting and analysing it shows where the system is weak, such as shelf layout or label printing, and that learning prevents later errors that could cause harm. It does not require patient disclosure or a college report.
  29. The correct answer is A (Sit, spray once under the tongue, and call 911 if the pain lasts five minutes): Heart and Stroke Foundation guidance is to stop and sit, use one dose of nitroglycerin, and call 911 if the pain is not relieved five minutes after that first dose, since it may be a heart attack. Lying flat can worsen hypotension and waiting longer delays emergency care. Further doses can be taken while waiting for the ambulance.
Case CHB9

PV is a 49 year old man who smokes 25 cigarettes daily and lights his first cigarette within 10 minutes of waking. A patch-only quit attempt last year lasted three weeks. He had one generalized seizure two years ago during alcohol withdrawal and has since stopped drinking. He takes ramipril 10 mg daily for hypertension. Blood pressure today is 132/82 mmHg, creatinine clearance is 85 mL/min, and he reports no mental health history. He is motivated to quit within the next month.

  1. The correct answer is C (bupropion, because it lowers the seizure threshold and he had a seizure during alcohol withdrawal): Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold and is contraindicated in people with a seizure disorder, so his past seizure rules it out. Varenicline has no relevant interaction with ramipril and needs no renal adjustment at a clearance of 85 mL/min. Nicotine replacement products are not linked to seizures at usual doses.
  2. The correct answer is C (0.5 mg daily for three days, then 0.5 mg twice daily for four days): Varenicline is titrated to limit nausea: 0.5 mg once daily for days one to three, 0.5 mg twice daily for days four to seven, then 1 mg twice daily. The quit date falls around day eight, when the full dose is reached. Starting at full dose raises the rate of nausea, and the other schedules delay or skip steps of the standard titration.
  3. The correct answer is A (Take it after eating with a full glass of water, and report agitation or mood changes): Taking varenicline with food and a full glass of water reduces nausea, and patients should report agitation, depressed mood or unusual behaviour so that the prescriber can reassess. Food does not meaningfully reduce absorption. Nausea is common and usually settles, and the quit date is set within the first weeks while still taking the drug.
  4. The correct answer is A (hold it during vomiting, diarrhea or poor intake, because of dehydration and ketoacidosis risk): SGLT2 inhibitors cause osmotic diuresis and can lead to euglycemic ketoacidosis, so sick day guidance is to hold the drug during dehydrating illness and restart when eating and drinking normally. Doubling doses or cutting fluids would worsen the risks.
  5. The correct answer is D (Check the INR within three to five days and reduce the warfarin dose if it rises): Fluconazole inhibits CYP2C9, which clears the more potent warfarin enantiomer, so the INR often rises within days and bleeding risk increases. Early INR checks allow a dose reduction before the INR becomes supratherapeutic. Increasing the dose would worsen bleeding, and replacing warfarin with aspirin gives inadequate stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation.
  6. The correct answer is B (The plan needs the prescriber to submit a request showing he meets coverage criteria before paying): A special authorization drug is covered only after the prescriber requests approval and documents that the plan's clinical criteria are met. The rejection does not mean the drug is unapproved or unsafe. A deductible would produce a different rejection message.
  7. The correct answer is B (Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms, usually starting two to eight weeks in): Fever, rash, facial edema, eosinophilia and organ involvement such as liver injury, with a delay of several weeks, characterize DRESS. Aromatic anticonvulsants like carbamazepine are classic culprits. Urticaria and fixed drug eruptions have different timing and lack systemic organ involvement.
  8. The correct answer is C (referring him to the prescriber for assessment and antifungal treatment of probable oral thrush): White, wipe-off patches with hoarseness after an inhaled corticosteroid suggest oral candidiasis, which needs antifungal therapy from a prescriber or a pharmacist with that authority. Rinsing and spitting lowers the risk of recurrence. Stopping the controller risks an asthma exacerbation, an oral corticosteroid carries far more systemic effects, and alcohol rinses irritate the mucosa without treating the infection.
  9. The correct answer is A (inhibits CYP3A4 and raises simvastatin levels, increasing the risk of muscle toxicity and myopathy): Clarithromycin is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and simvastatin depends on that enzyme for clearance. Higher exposure raises the risk of muscle toxicity, including rhabdomyolysis. The other mechanisms describe effects clarithromycin does not have.
  10. The correct answer is D (Crossover dilutes the group differences, so a per-protocol analysis should also support the conclusion drawn): When participants cross over, the groups become more alike, which pulls the intention-to-treat estimate toward no difference and can make a worse drug look non-inferior. Non-inferiority conclusions are therefore expected to hold in both intention-to-treat and per-protocol analyses. Intention-to-treat analysis keeps all randomized participants, so it does not exclude dropouts.
  11. The correct answer is B (Decline to vaccinate her and refer her to an allergist for assessment): A history of anaphylaxis after a previous dose of the same vaccine is a contraindication to routine administration in a community pharmacy. She needs individualized assessment by an allergist or public health, where any further dosing can be done with full resuscitation support. Antihistamine pretreatment does not prevent anaphylaxis, and splitting the dose has no supporting evidence.
  12. The correct answer is D (Yes, because the upper confidence limit of 0.35 is below the prespecified margin of 0.4): Non-inferiority is judged by comparing the worst plausible difference, the upper limit here, with the margin. Since 0.35 is smaller than 0.4, a loss of efficacy larger than the margin is excluded. Including zero in the interval is expected in a non-inferiority result and does not argue against it.
  13. The correct answer is A (Location, which describes where the symptom is felt and whether it radiates): Asking where the pain is and whether it spreads explores the location of the symptom. Characteristics concern what the pain feels like, onset concerns timing, and aggravating factors concern what makes it worse. Separating these elements helps the pharmacist avoid skipping a part of the assessment.
  14. The correct answer is C (Continue levodopa on schedule with sips of water, since stopping can cause severe rigidity and fever): Levodopa has a short half-life, and missed doses can precipitate marked rigidity, aspiration risk and a neuroleptic malignant-like syndrome called parkinsonism-hyperpyrexia. Doses should be given on time with sips of water, or by nasogastric tube if needed, in agreement with the anesthesia team. Amantadine cannot replace levodopa, and skipping later doses leaves him unprotected.
  15. The correct answer is D (Reduce to 2.5 mg twice daily, since she meets two of three dose reduction criteria): The apixaban dose is lowered to 2.5 mg twice daily when a patient has at least two of three features: age 80 or older, weight 60 kg or less, and serum creatinine 133 umol/L or higher. She is 84 and weighs 56 kg, so two criteria are met even though her creatinine is lower. Age over 80 does not rule out apixaban, so switching to warfarin is unnecessary.
  16. The correct answer is D (A long-acting dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker such as amlodipine, which does not slow conduction): Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as diltiazem and verapamil slow sinus and AV node conduction, so adding them to a beta-blocker in a patient with a heart rate of 52 risks severe bradycardia or heart block. Amlodipine relieves angina by vasodilation without slowing conduction. Increasing metoprolol further is also unsuitable at his heart rate.
  17. The correct answer is B (Stop hydrochlorothiazide and monitor sodium closely, avoiding rapid correction above 10 mmol/L in 24 hours): Thiazide induced hyponatremia is common in older women and the offending drug should be stopped. Overly rapid correction risks osmotic demyelination, so a limit of about 10 mmol/L per 24 hours applies. Loop diuretics can also lower sodium, and desmopressin would worsen water retention.
  18. The correct answer is A (Hold isoniazid and contact her prescriber urgently for assessment of her liver): Isoniazid can cause hepatitis, and symptoms with an ALT of more than three times the upper limit of normal call for stopping the drug and prompt medical review. Pyridoxine prevents neuropathy, not liver injury. Rifampin is also hepatotoxic, so it is not a safe immediate substitute.
  19. The correct answer is C (Date, drug and dose, whether witnessed or carried, and the pharmacist's initials for each dose): Each dose should be traceable: the date, drug and dose, how it was given and who was responsible. This supports safe dosing and missed-dose decisions. Recording only missed or changed doses, or weekly totals, leaves gaps that cannot be reconstructed if a safety question arises.
  20. The correct answer is D (A non-sedating second-generation antihistamine such as cetirizine, taken daily on a regular schedule): Regular dosing of a non-sedating second-generation antihistamine is first-line for chronic spontaneous urticaria. First-generation agents add sedation and anticholinergic effects without better control. Systemic steroids are reserved for short rescue use, and topical steroids do not reach the mediators driving the wheals.
  21. The correct answer is B (Ask IR what she has read and what concerns her most about starting atorvastatin): The first Ask invites the patient to share what she knows and what worries her, which shows what information is actually needed and respects her autonomy. The Tell step then addresses her specific concern, and the final Ask checks understanding. Starting with a rebuttal or a form can make the patient defensive.
  22. The correct answer is D (The pooled result is not robust, so confidence in the claimed benefit should be lowered): A sensitivity analysis tests whether one study drives the conclusion. When removal erases statistical significance, the finding depends on a single small trial, which lowers certainty. Large effects from small trials are often overestimates, so they deserve scepticism rather than extra trust.
  23. The correct answer is B (Check public coverage and generic options, then ask the prescriber which drugs are essential): Cost is a barrier to access, so the pharmacist first looks for coverage and lower cost products and involves the prescriber to prioritize drugs that matter most clinically. Choosing drugs by price alone or telling him to skip a mental health drug ignores his clinical needs. Carrying a large balance may be possible later but does not address the underlying access problem.
  24. The correct answer is D (Systemic estrogen works best, and a progestogen is added to protect the uterine lining): Systemic estrogen is the most effective treatment for vasomotor symptoms. In a woman with a uterus, unopposed estrogen raises the risk of endometrial hyperplasia and cancer, so a progestogen is added. Non-hormonal drugs help less than estrogen, and black cohosh lacks strong evidence.
  25. The correct answer is B (Review omeprazole need, modestly raise levothyroxine, and repeat TSH in six to eight weeks): Levothyroxine absorption depends partly on gastric acidity, so a proton pump inhibitor can raise the dose needed. The sensible approach is to confirm the PPI is still indicated, adjust the dose, and recheck TSH once steady state is reached. Taking the tablet with food reduces absorption further, and PPIs are not contraindicated.
  26. The correct answer is D (A pharmacist must be readily available to assist, and no prescription is required): Schedule III products may be sold without a prescription from a self-selection area inside the pharmacy, but a pharmacist must be readily available to help patients choose. Counselling at every sale is the Schedule II standard. Sale outside a pharmacy is allowed only for unscheduled products.
  27. The correct answer is C (Start about 9 to 13 mg daily in divided doses, with breakthrough doses available): Ninety milligrams of morphine divided by 10 and multiplied by 2 gives 18 mg of hydromorphone. Reducing this by 25 to 50% for incomplete cross-tolerance, and more cautiously in an older man with renal impairment, gives about 9 to 13.5 mg daily. Matching milligram counts would be a fivefold overdose.
  28. The correct answer is A (Taper gradually, since adrenal suppression is likely after weeks at this dose): Glucocorticoids suppress the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, and suppression is likely after about three weeks of supraphysiologic doses. A gradual taper allows the adrenal glands time to resume cortisol production and lets the physician watch for disease flares. Abrupt withdrawal can cause adrenal insufficiency with weakness, hypotension and nausea.
  29. The correct answer is C (Raise the concern with the owner, citing the duty to put patient interests ahead of rebates): The manager's first duty is to patients, so the first step is to raise the concern directly with the owner and explain that rebates should not drive substitution decisions. Following the instruction with disclosure on request leaves patients with a costlier choice. Resigning or going to the college comes only if the owner does not change course.
  30. The correct answer is A (Fourteen days, with the PPI taken twice daily throughout the full course): Canadian consensus guidance recommends 14 days of PPI based triple therapy because eradication rates are higher than with 7 or 10 days. The PPI is taken twice daily for the whole course to keep gastric pH high enough for the antibiotics to work. Stopping the PPI early or extending the course does not improve eradication.
  31. The correct answer is D (About 67 percent, suggesting a coverage gap that warrants an adherence conversation): Each 30 day fill covers 30 days, so two fills cover 60 of the 90 days, which is 67 percent. A proportion of days covered below 80 percent is generally taken as inadequate adherence. The April fill falls outside the period and cannot be counted. The gap should prompt a nonjudgmental discussion about barriers.
  32. The correct answer is C (An unpaired two sample t-test, because the outcome is continuous in two independent groups): The outcome is a continuous variable measured in two separate groups and is approximately normal, so an unpaired two sample t-test compares the means. A chi-square test needs categorical data, a paired t-test needs the same patients measured twice, and a rank-based test is unnecessary for normal data.
  33. The correct answer is A (At-risk behaviour: coach the pharmacist and address workload pressures that made skipping the check routine): In just culture, at-risk behaviour is a choice where the risk is not recognized or is believed to be justified, often because shortcuts have become normal. The response is coaching plus removal of the system pressures that encourage the drift. Human error calls for consoling, and recklessness (conscious disregard of a known substantial risk) calls for discipline. The independent check on methotrexate stays necessary because the drug is high-alert.
  34. The correct answer is A (8 mL, because 4 g is 4000 mg and the stock holds 500 mg/mL): A 50% w/v solution contains 50 g per 100 mL, which is 500 mg per mL. A 4 g dose is 4000 mg, and 4000 divided by 500 gives 8 mL. The wrong volumes come from misreading the strength as 50 mg per mL or as 5000 or 1000 mg per mL.
  35. The correct answer is B (Place vaccines on the middle shelves with space around the boxes, away from the door): Vaccines belong in the central part of the refrigerator with air space around them, where the temperature is most stable. Door shelves swing in temperature each time the door opens, and areas next to the cooling element or vent can freeze product. Crisper drawers are not designed for vaccine storage.
  36. The correct answer is C (Refer her for diagnosis, because a first episode during pregnancy should be confirmed): Self diagnosis of vaginal symptoms is unreliable, and in pregnancy a missed bacterial vaginosis or trichomoniasis infection can affect pregnancy outcomes, so a first episode needs clinical confirmation before treatment. Oral fluconazole is avoided in pregnancy. If candidiasis is confirmed, a seven day topical azole course is the usual treatment.
  37. The correct answer is C (Report fever, sore throat or mouth ulcers promptly for urgent white cell blood work): Agranulocytosis is rare but life-threatening and typically presents as fever, sore throat or mouth ulcers, usually within the first three months. Patients must stop the drug and have an urgent complete blood count with differential. A rash can occur but is not the most serious early danger, and weight gain does not indicate low white cells.
  38. The correct answer is D (Separate dolutegravir from the vitamin by two hours before or six hours after): Iron and calcium bind integrase inhibitors in the gut and reduce dolutegravir absorption. Dolutegravir should be taken two hours before or six hours after these minerals, and taking both together with a meal is another accepted approach. Tenofovir alafenamide does not prevent this chelation, and stopping a needed supplement is unnecessary.
  39. The correct answer is B (Avoid naproxen if possible; if required, add a proton pump inhibitor and monitor for bleeding): Age over 65, corticosteroid use and an SSRI each raise the risk of upper gastrointestinal bleeding, and together they raise it substantially. An NSAID is best avoided, or combined with a proton pump inhibitor. Enteric coating does not prevent systemic mucosal harm, H2 antagonists do not reliably prevent ulcers, and switching to ibuprofen still leaves meaningful risk.
Case CHB10

PD is a 76 year old woman admitted to a medical ward 6 days ago with community acquired pneumonia, treated with intravenous ceftriaxone. She also takes pantoprazole 40 mg daily for reflux. Since yesterday she has had 6 watery stools daily with lower abdominal cramps. Her temperature is 38.1 C, white blood cell count 11 x 10^9/L, and serum creatinine 85 umol/L (baseline 80). A stool C. difficile toxin test is positive. This is her first episode.

  1. The correct answer is A (Oral vancomycin 125 mg four times daily for 10 days, and review the ceftriaxone therapy): Her white count is below 15 x 10^9/L and creatinine is near baseline, so this is a non-severe episode, and oral vancomycin for 10 days is a recommended regimen. Metronidazole is no longer preferred. Intravenous vancomycin does not reach the colon lumen, and antimotility drugs risk worsening toxin retention.
  2. The correct answer is C (A total of 200 mL, which is 5 mL per dose for 40 doses): Each 125 mg dose at 25 mg/mL is 5 mL. Four doses daily for 10 days is 40 doses, so 40 x 5 mL gives 200 mL. The other totals come from halving the dose volume or miscounting the number of doses.
  3. The correct answer is D (Contact precautions with gloves and gown, and soap and water hand washing after patient care): C. difficile spreads by spores on hands and surfaces, so gloves, gowns and a dedicated environment are needed. Alcohol-based rub does not kill spores, so hands should be washed with soap and water after care. Droplet and airborne precautions address respiratory spread, which is not the route here.
  4. The correct answer is D (Contact the prescriber to suggest beclomethasone, an inhaled steroid less dependent on CYP3A4 metabolism): Cobicistat strongly inhibits CYP3A4, which raises systemic fluticasone levels and can cause Cushing syndrome and adrenal suppression even with inhaled use. Beclomethasone and ciclesonide are less affected, so changing the steroid is the safest fix. Halving the dose does not remove the interaction, and changing a controlled antiretroviral regimen for an inhaler is disproportionate.
  5. The correct answer is B (Start amoxicillin-clavulanate and give a tetanus booster, as her last was over 5 years ago): Hand puncture wounds from bites carry a high infection risk, and amoxicillin-clavulanate covers Pasteurella, streptococci, staphylococci and anaerobes. A dirty wound needs tetanus toxoid when the last dose was more than 5 years ago. Cephalexin lacks reliable Pasteurella and anaerobic activity, and topical therapy cannot treat a deep puncture.
  6. The correct answer is B (Ask DL what he understands about his illness and what he has been told): SPIKES stands for Setting, Perception, Invitation, Knowledge, Emotions and Strategy. After the setting, the Perception step asks what the patient already understands so information can be matched to it. Detailed findings, premature reassurance or leaving the patient alone skip the assessment of his understanding and emotions.
  7. The correct answer is D (Obtain gestational age, allergies and culture results, then weigh the evidence for safer alternatives): The recommendation depends on patient details such as gestational age, allergies and culture results, so these are gathered before any answer is given. Evidence of conflicting quality is then appraised, and usually safer alternatives exist for urinary infections in pregnancy. Choosing one source blindly, sending the patient to hospital or waiting for the manufacturer does not serve the patient.
  8. The correct answer is A (Water-based liquids have high water activity that supports microbial growth, unlike an anhydrous ointment): Water activity measures the water available to microorganisms. Aqueous suspensions have high water activity and can grow bacteria and fungi, so their dating is short, while an anhydrous ointment has very low water activity and resists growth. Light exposure and the child's age do not explain the difference in dating.
  9. The correct answer is B (Report the loss to Health Canada's Office of Controlled Substances using the prescribed form): An unexplained narcotic shortage is a loss that must be reported to the Office of Controlled Substances, Health Canada, under the Narcotic Control Regulations. Provincial college reporting may also apply but does not replace it. Adjusting inventory or an internal report alone does not meet the federal duty.
  10. The correct answer is D (13 mL per hour, because the concentration of the bag is 100 units per mL): The bag contains 25,000 units in 250 mL, which is 100 units per mL. An order of 1,300 units per hour therefore needs 1,300 divided by 100, or 13 mL per hour. The other rates come from misplacing the decimal in the concentration or from a fixed ten hour assumption that has no link to the order.
  11. The correct answer is C (Dispense only the original quantity, since narcotic prescriptions cannot be refilled, and request a new one): Under the Narcotic Control Regulations a prescription for a narcotic such as hydromorphone cannot be refilled, so each fill needs a new prescription. Recording refills as for a non-narcotic, or dispensing the entire quantity at once, would breach the regulations and create a diversion risk.
  12. The correct answer is D (Condition the ice packs, separate them from vaccines with insulating material, and add a thermometer): Frozen packs must be conditioned until they are slightly thawed, and separated from vaccine with insulating material, so the product does not freeze on contact. Placing a thermometer inside the cooler lets the team confirm the 2 to 8 degree range throughout the trip. Skipping the cold packs would allow the vaccine to warm well above range.
  13. The correct answer is A (Insertion up to the following Saturday at 11 p.m. is acceptable, within seven days): A copper intrauterine device can be inserted for emergency contraception within seven days of unprotected intercourse, which here runs to the next Saturday at 11 p.m. The 72 hour limit applies to levonorgestrel, not to the device. Waiting for the expected period is not the rule, and a 24 hour limit has no basis.
  14. The correct answer is C (Use a workflow where a colleague promptly provides the service and patients are informed): A workable arrangement lets the objecting pharmacist step aside while a colleague provides the service promptly, with patients informed how to access it. Structuring shifts to dodge requests or posting a notice that the pharmacy does not offer the service limits access. Conscience rights are not waived by employment, so forcing the service is also wrong.
  15. The correct answer is A (Start entecavir or tenofovir before chemotherapy and continue for at least 12 months afterward): Isolated anti-HBc positivity means past exposure with covert virus that rituximab can reactivate, sometimes causing fulminant hepatitis. Prophylactic entecavir or tenofovir is started before treatment and continued for at least 12 months after it ends, because reactivation can occur late. Vaccination does not protect an already exposed person, and stopping when chemotherapy ends leaves a high risk window.
  16. The correct answer is D (Recorded the recommendation, prescriber's decision, reason given, date, time and prescriber name): A note should show the recommendation, the prescriber's response and the reasoning, along with the date, time and who was spoken to. Declined recommendations are still part of the care record and protect both the patient and the pharmacist. Vague or judgmental entries do not show what was actually discussed.
  17. The correct answer is D (The cream base can weaken latex, so use a backup method during treatment): The oil based vehicle in clindamycin vaginal cream can degrade latex condoms and diaphragms, so another contraceptive method is needed during treatment and for a few days afterward. Changing the timing of insertion does not remove the effect on the latex. The cream is not water based, and latex is the material at risk.
  18. The correct answer is B (12 bottles, since lead time demand is 6 bottles and safety stock is 6): Daily use is 14 / 7 = 2 bottles, so demand during a 3 day lead time is 6 bottles. Adding the 6 bottles of safety stock gives a reorder point of 12 bottles. Using a full week of demand or leaving out the lead time demand gives a wrong reorder point.
  19. The correct answer is A (Self-assessed learning needs, a plan, and completed activities with reflection on how practice changed): Competence programs are built on reflective practice: identifying learning needs, planning, learning, and recording what changed in practice. Hours alone, an annual written examination or peer testimonials do not show that the learning was relevant to FD's own work.
  20. The correct answer is D ($13.60, which is 20 percent of the combined drug cost and dispensing fee of $68.00): The claim total is $60.00 + $8.00 = $68.00. The patient pays 20 percent, 0.20 x 68 = $13.60, and the plan pays $54.40. Excluding the fee, or adding it in full, does not match a plan that covers a percentage of the whole claim.
  21. The correct answer is A (rosuvastatin, because it is minimally metabolized by CYP3A4, so grapefruit has little effect): Grapefruit juice inhibits intestinal CYP3A4 and markedly increases exposure to simvastatin and lovastatin. Rosuvastatin and pravastatin are not significantly metabolized by CYP3A4. Higher doses of an affected statin would raise, not lower, toxicity risk.
  22. The correct answer is A (Eat a consistent amount of vitamin K containing foods each week rather than avoiding them): Warfarin works by interfering with vitamin K recycling, so large swings in vitamin K intake change the INR. A steady intake lets the dose be set around his usual diet. Cutting out vegetables does not make warfarin safer, and a vitamin K supplement is used only in specific situations under prescriber direction.
  23. The correct answer is B (Contact the nurse practitioner for a new prescription before dispensing any more lorazepam): Lorazepam is a benzodiazepine, a targeted substance regulated under the Benzodiazepines and Other Targeted Substances Regulations. A pharmacist may refill it only when the prescriber has authorized refills on the prescription. This one states none, so a new prescription is needed, and a part-fill or transfer cannot create supply that was never authorized.
  24. The correct answer is A (Use the professional telephone interpreter and confirm understanding by teach-back with her): Trained interpreters give accurate, confidential translation and let the pharmacist verify understanding through teach-back. Children should not interpret complex or sensitive medical information. Written translation alone cannot check technique with an injectable.
Case CHB11

HW is a 72 year old woman in a rural community who has taken lorazepam 1 mg at bedtime for insomnia for six years. Her other medications are amlodipine 5 mg daily and sertraline 50 mg daily. Her prescription was written for 30 tablets with five refills at 30 day intervals. The original fill and three refills have been dispensed. She had a fall at home last month.

  1. The correct answer is C (Call the prescriber, since refills must follow the interval written on the prescription): A refill of a targeted substance follows the number and interval the prescriber specified, so supplying early requires the prescriber's authorization. The regulations do not set a vacation override or a limit of three refills for older adults. Calling the prescriber lets the pharmacist arrange travel supply properly.
  2. The correct answer is C (Sixty tablets, representing the final two refills of thirty tablets each from the authorization): The prescription authorized 30 tablets plus five refills, which is six fills or 180 tablets. The original fill and three refills used four fills, or 120 tablets, so two fills of 30 tablets remain. The other answers miscount the refills already used.
  3. The correct answer is A (Recommend the prescriber consider a gradual taper and tell HW not to stop suddenly): Long term benzodiazepine use in an older adult with a fall justifies deprescribing, but stopping abruptly after six years risks withdrawal, rebound insomnia and seizures, so a gradual taper is advised. Diphenhydramine is a poor substitute in older adults because of anticholinergic effects. Continuing unchanged ignores the fall risk.
  4. The correct answer is D (Accept the small token, which carries no expectation of preferential treatment, and keep care unchanged): A small, unsolicited thank-you from a patient is generally acceptable when it carries no expectation of special treatment and does not influence care. Tying a gift to future business or offering discounts in return would create a conflict. Gifts are not automatically reportable misconduct.
  5. The correct answer is D (Differences between statin users and nonusers may explain the result, so confounding remains possible): Without randomization, people who take statins may differ from those who do not in age, weight and health behaviours, so confounding can persist even after adjustment. A prospective cohort does establish that exposure came first and can estimate incidence and relative risk directly.
  6. The correct answer is C (About 1.6 (10% versus 6.3%); losses were unequal, so the ratio may be biased): Exposed participants followed were 850, so risk was 85 of 850, or 10%. Unexposed followed were 950, so risk was 60 of 950, or 6.3%, giving a ratio near 1.6. Losing 15% of one group but 5% of the other suggests differential attrition, which can distort the comparison.
  7. The correct answer is D (Contact the transplant team urgently to replace mycophenolate while continuing the other immunosuppressants): Mycophenolate is teratogenic and linked to pregnancy loss and malformations, so it must be replaced, often by azathioprine, under transplant team guidance. Stopping all immunosuppression risks graft loss and harms the pregnancy. Waiting or halving the dose still exposes the embryo during organogenesis.
  8. The correct answer is D (Change ramipril to an ARB such as candesartan, which rarely causes a cough): ACE inhibitor cough comes from bradykinin accumulation and is a class effect, so changing to another ACE inhibitor does not solve it. An ARB gives similar cardiovascular protection without bradykinin buildup. Because he has no angioedema, the switch can be made directly without a four week gap.
  9. The correct answer is A (Serious imprecision, such as wide confidence intervals that include both benefit and harm): GRADE lowers certainty for risk of bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision and publication bias. Wide intervals spanning benefit and harm are a clear case of imprecision. Placebo comparators, industry funding and journal indexing are not themselves GRADE domains, although funding may prompt a closer look at bias.
  10. The correct answer is C (Idarucizumab, a specific antibody fragment that binds dabigatran and reverses its anticoagulant effect rapidly): Idarucizumab is the specific reversal agent for dabigatran and is used for life-threatening bleeding such as this unstable gastrointestinal bleed. Vitamin K reverses warfarin and protamine reverses heparin, so neither works here. Plasma does not reliably reverse dabigatran, whose effect is not a clotting factor deficiency.
  11. The correct answer is C (54 patients, because 1,620 divided by 30 tablets per fill equals 54): The 30 day fill at one tablet daily uses 30 tablets, so 1,620 / 30 = 54 fills. Each 90 tablet bottle supplies three such fills, not one, which is why counting bottles or whole bottles per patient understates the number of patients served.
  12. The correct answer is B (Switch to oral cephalexin at an adequate dose and set a clear stop date): A patient who is improving, afebrile, eating, and able to absorb drugs is a candidate for an intravenous to oral switch, which shortens hospital stay and avoids line complications. Cephalexin has similar activity against the usual skin pathogens, and a defined stop date prevents unnecessary extension. Stopping early risks relapse, and adding another agent is unneeded.
  13.
