The correct answer is B (6 percentage points, the placebo group risk minus the drug group risk): Absolute risk reduction is the placebo risk minus the treated risk: 30 of 200 is 15% and 18 of 200 is 9%, giving 6 percentage points. Dividing that difference by the placebo risk gives the relative reduction of 40%, and dividing the two risks gives a relative risk of 0.6. A raw count difference ignores the group denominators.
  14. The correct answer is D (Supply only 24 hours of syringes, since the sterility limit overrides the longer chemical stability): A beyond-use date for a sterile preparation is the shorter of the sterility-based limit for how and where it was compounded and the drug's chemical stability. Published stability of 14 days cannot extend a 24 hour limit set by the compounding environment. The pharmacist can prepare syringes on a daily schedule to meet the physician's needs.
  15. The correct answer is B (Add one tablet in the morning and one in the mid-afternoon, for four tablets daily): The monograph schedule builds from two tablets at bedtime to a maximum of four tablets a day: two at bedtime, one in the morning and one in the mid-afternoon. Doses above four tablets daily exceed the labelled maximum. Taking all tablets at bedtime does not cover daytime symptoms.
  16. The correct answer is B (Continue atorvastatin, since ALT under three times normal is acceptable, and recheck it later): Mild transaminase elevations below three times the upper limit of normal are common with statins and usually settle without any change in treatment. Statins are stopped or the dose is reviewed only when ALT stays above three times normal or symptoms of liver injury appear. Stopping or switching now would remove a proven benefit for a clinically minor finding.
  17. The correct answer is D (Test four weeks after antibiotics, with the PPI held for two weeks beforehand): Antibiotics and PPIs both suppress H. pylori and cause false negative breath tests. Testing is delayed at least four weeks after antibiotics and the PPI is held for two weeks beforehand. Earlier testing or a short PPI hold can miss persisting infection and wrongly suggest cure.
  18. The correct answer is C (12 mL, because 300 mg divided by 25 mg per mL gives 12 mL): The suspension contains 125 mg in 5 mL, which is 25 mg per mL. A 300 mg dose therefore needs 300 divided by 25, or 12 mL. The suspension should be shaken well before measuring because the drug settles.
  19. The correct answer is D (300 mg per day, given in two or three divided doses): Cockcroft-Gault gives (140 minus 70) x 80 x 1.23 / 150, about 46 mL/min. Pregabalin dosing for a clearance of 30 to 60 mL/min is 75 to 300 mg per day in two or three divided doses. A ceiling of 600 mg per day applies only at 60 mL/min or more, and 150 mg per day fits the lower 15 to 30 mL/min band.
  20. The correct answer is D (Swallow capsules whole and keep them in the original bottle, which protects them from moisture): Opening a dabigatran capsule raises absorption by roughly 75 percent and increases bleeding risk, so capsules are swallowed whole. They are sensitive to moisture and stay in the original bottle (discard 4 months after first opening) or original blister package. Refrigeration is not needed.
  21. The correct answer is C (Attend an emergency department now, since injectable hydrocortisone may be needed for adrenal crisis): A patient on long-term glucocorticoid has suppressed adrenal function, and vomiting prevents absorption of her dose just when illness raises cortisol needs. Low blood pressure and dizziness suggest impending adrenal crisis, which needs injectable hydrocortisone and fluids. Missing doses or waiting at home risks collapse.
  22. The correct answer is D (The public limit is 30 g dried equivalent, and sharing is allowed only with other adults): The Cannabis Act allows adults to possess up to 30 g of dried cannabis or its equivalent in public and to share up to that amount with other adults. DM's 50 g exceeds the public limit, although sharing 10 g with an adult is permitted. A purchase receipt does not lift the limit.
  23. The correct answer is C (Advertising must be verifiable and avoid unsubstantiated claims of superiority over other pharmacies): Colleges expect pharmacy advertising to be accurate and verifiable, and claims that one pharmacy is the only caring or safest option cannot be proven and disparage competitors. There is no Health Canada pre-approval step for a pharmacy's service advertising, and pharmacies may describe their services and pharmacists' qualifications truthfully. Radio is not restricted to manufacturers.
  24. The correct answer is C (Stay at 5 mg for four to six weeks before the prescriber raises the dose): Donepezil is started at 5 mg daily and increased to 10 mg only after 4 to 6 weeks at the lower dose, which improves tolerability and lessens nausea and bradycardia. Doubling early raises adverse effects without proven benefit. A six month wait is longer than needed.
  25. The correct answer is B (Hold the prescriptions until the patient authorizes the release by phone or in writing): Knowing a name and birth date does not show consent, and mental health drugs are sensitive. The pharmacist should confirm directly with the patient that this person may collect them. Releasing either drug without consent breaches privacy law, while an abrupt refusal with no route to authorize misses a simple way to help.
  26. The correct answer is D (Fold each used patch sticky sides together, store it sealed, and return it to the pharmacy): Used patches still contain enough fentanyl to harm a child, so they should be folded sticky sides together, kept out of reach and returned to the pharmacy for proper destruction. Cutting patches releases drug and increases exposure. Garbage and flushing both leave drug in places where children or the environment are exposed.
  27. The correct answer is B (Advise her to stop tramadol and go to the emergency department for assessment today): Agitation, tremor, diarrhea, sweating and myoclonus soon after adding tramadol to a high dose SNRI suggest serotonin syndrome, which needs urgent in-person assessment. The new serotonergic drug should be stopped, and the prescriber informed. Treating single symptoms at home or only lowering venlafaxine leaves the interaction active and delays care.
  28. The correct answer is B (About 150 morphine milligram equivalents per day, which exceeds the 90 threshold): Controlled release oxycodone totals 80 mg per day and the average immediate release use adds 20 mg, giving 100 mg of oxycodone. Multiplying by 1.5 gives 150 morphine milligram equivalents per day. The figure of 100 omits the conversion factor, 120 omits the immediate release doses, and 165 uses maximum rather than average use.
  29. The correct answer is C (Dispense enough to cover the trip after checking the plan's early refill and vacation rules): Vacation supply provisions exist for situations like this, so the pharmacist checks the plan's conditions and dispenses accordingly. A fixed refusal ignores the exception, and billing an oversized supply without checking the rules risks a claim reversal. Cash payment is a poor idea when coverage may apply.
  30. The correct answer is A (Taper amitriptyline gradually and reassess pain, considering a topical or renally adjusted alternative): Amitriptyline is strongly anticholinergic and causes orthostatic hypotension, which fits her confusion, dry mouth, constipation and fall, so it is potentially inappropriate in older adults. A gradual taper avoids withdrawal effects. Increasing the dose or adding an opioid would raise fall and delirium risk further.
  31. The correct answer is C (Suggest ticagrelor instead, since her age and weight raise bleeding risk with prasugrel): Prasugrel is not recommended for patients aged 75 or older and carries higher bleeding risk below 60 kg, and she meets both criteria. Ticagrelor is a suitable alternative with a 180 mg loading dose. Having no previous stroke only removes one contraindication, and a reduced loading dose is not a studied approach.
  32. The correct answer is D (A chi-square test, because both variables are categorical and the groups are independent): Infection yes or no is a categorical outcome and the two catheter groups are independent, so a chi-square test (or a Fisher exact test for small counts) compares the proportions. A t-test compares means of continuous data, ANOVA compares means across three or more groups, and a paired test requires matched observations.
  33. The correct answer is C (Lot number, quantity quarantined, date of removal and pharmacist signature, retained on file): A recall record should identify the lot, the quantity removed, when it was removed and who took responsibility. Relying on the wholesaler's records or a verbal confirmation leaves the pharmacy unable to prove its own action. Patient lists matter for consumer-level recalls but do not document the stock handled.
  34. The correct answer is B (Hold the prescription, complete the clinical check and counselling, then review how the gap occurred): Any new anticoagulant needs a pharmacist's clinical assessment and counselling before it leaves the pharmacy, and these cannot be delegated to an assistant or postponed. The technical check does not replace them. Holding the prescription protects the patient, and the later review should find why the workflow allowed a release without the pharmacist.
  35. The correct answer is B (Intervene to assess the warfarin interaction risk, since unscheduled products can still need pharmacist advice): Unscheduled status removes the conditions of sale but not the pharmacist's professional duty to identify drug therapy problems. Topical methyl salicylate can raise the INR in patients on warfarin, so the pharmacist should assess and advise now rather than defer the discussion. Topical salicylates are not Schedule I products.
  36. The correct answer is C (Flank pain with a temperature of 38.6 C and vomiting since last night): Fever, flank pain and vomiting suggest upper urinary tract infection or pyelonephritis, which is a complicated infection needing medical assessment. Frequency, urgency and suprapubic discomfort are typical of uncomplicated cystitis. A remote prior episode does not exclude prescribing.
  37. The correct answer is C (Avoid it, because fluid retention from pioglitazone can worsen his existing heart failure): Thiazolidinediones cause sodium and water retention and are contraindicated in patients with heart failure. A higher diuretic dose or a lower starting dose does not make the drug safe in this setting. The neutral cardiac effect claim is incorrect because the fluid effect can precipitate decompensation.
  38. The correct answer is D (Add a dated, signed amendment explaining the correction and keep the original entry visible): Pharmacy records must be accurate, and corrections are made by a dated, attributable amendment that leaves the original visible so the history can be audited. Deleting or overwriting an entry destroys that trail. Leaving a known error uncorrected is also improper, and the claim discrepancy can then be reconciled with the payer.
  39. The correct answer is C (Call 911, give one spray, and repeat in two to three minutes without a response): An unresponsive person with slow or absent breathing needs emergency services called immediately, together with a naloxone dose into one nostril. If there is no response after two to three minutes, another dose is given. Delaying the call or waiting for a color change wastes time, and stacking all doses at once is not the recommended technique.
  40. The correct answer is A (It can be added, since resting sinus rate stays 77 beats/min or higher despite maximal beta-blocker): Ivabradine slows the sinus node and is added in sinus rhythm when the resting heart rate stays at 77 beats/min or higher despite a maximally tolerated beta-blocker and an ejection fraction of 35% or less. Her rate of 78 beats/min qualifies. It is an add-on, not a replacement for the beta-blocker. It is ineffective in atrial fibrillation and has no potassium-retaining effect.
  41. The correct answer is C (Replace it from an unaffected lot, retrieve the recalled bottle, and review symptoms to report): Subpotent levothyroxine is a Type II concern, so the supply should be swapped promptly without any gap in therapy and she should know which symptoms of under-replacement to report. Stopping the drug creates a harmful interruption. An emergency visit is out of proportion to the risk, and continuing the lot leaves her exposed.
  42. The correct answer is C (Withhold dispensing until the prescriber or a covering physician directly confirms the intended purpose): The Criminal Code protection for pharmacists applies once the pharmacist has been informed of the purpose, and a secondhand report from a family member is not reliable verification of such a serious order. A missing indication is a gap to clarify with the prescriber, not by itself grounds for a college report. Splitting the order does not remove the need for confirmation, so the pharmacist should pursue direct confirmation promptly.
  43. The correct answer is A (A store-wide sale on non-prescription front-store products that is open to all customers equally): Gifts, points or fee discounts connected to filling or transferring prescriptions can steer patients' choice of pharmacy, which is why most colleges prohibit them. A sale on non-prescription goods open to every customer is not tied to prescription services. Limiting redemption to non-prescription items does not remove the link between the reward and prescriptions.
  44. The correct answer is A (Low potency products are preferred on the face and skin folds because absorption is higher): Skin on the face and in skin folds is thin and absorbs more steroid, so low potency products lower the risk of atrophy and systemic effects. Potent agents are reserved for thick skin and short courses. Ointment bases are not avoided in dry flares, and one potency does not suit every site.
  45. The correct answer is C (Swallow it whole 30 to 60 minutes before her first meal each day): PPIs inhibit only actively secreting proton pumps, and pumps are most active after a prolonged fast, so the dose is taken 30 to 60 minutes before the first meal. Taking it with food or at bedtime reduces effect. On demand use after symptoms start is too slow, because PPIs need a day or more to reach full effect.
  46. The correct answer is D (Five days, since short courses are as effective and lower adverse effects and resistance pressure): For uncomplicated cystitis, a five day course of nitrofurantoin achieves cure rates similar to longer courses with fewer adverse effects and less selection for resistance. The three day regimen applies to trimethoprim sulfamethoxazole, not nitrofurantoin. Pharmacists support stewardship by recommending the shortest effective duration to the prescriber.
  47. The correct answer is A (Shred or securely destroy paper with patient information, and train all staff on the procedure): Safeguards must be proportionate to the sensitivity of the information, and health data on paper must be destroyed so it cannot be reconstructed. Tearing, relocating a bin, or storing paper unlocked all leave readable personal information accessible.
  48. The correct answer is B (Memantine blocks NMDA glutamate receptors and may add benefit in moderate to severe disease): Memantine is an NMDA receptor antagonist that reduces glutamate-related excitotoxicity and is indicated for moderate to severe Alzheimer disease, where it can be combined with a cholinesterase inhibitor. It does not inhibit acetylcholinesterase, restore neurons, or act on dopamine. Benefit is modest and symptomatic.
  49. The correct answer is C (Disclose the error to the patient promptly, explain what happened, and outline prevention steps): Canadian patient safety guidance on disclosure calls for telling the patient about any incident that reaches them, whether or not harm occurred, and the patient is owed an honest account. Waiting for harm or for a question undermines trust. Disclosure is documented and paired with a plan to prevent recurrence.
  50. The correct answer is C (Switch to propylthiouracil for the first trimester, then consider returning to methimazole afterward): Methimazole taken in early organogenesis is linked to a pattern of birth defects, so propylthiouracil is preferred in the first trimester. Because of its liver toxicity, many clinicians return to methimazole afterward. Stopping treatment leaves maternal thyrotoxicosis uncontrolled, and radioactive iodine is contraindicated in pregnancy.
Case CHB12

PF is a 47 year old man newly diagnosed with active pulmonary tuberculosis. He lives in a shelter and drinks about six beers daily. He starts isoniazid 300 mg, rifampin 600 mg, pyrazinamide 1,750 mg and ethambutol 1,200 mg once daily. He also takes warfarin 5 mg daily for a prior pulmonary embolism with a recent INR of 2.4. Baseline ALT is 48 U/L (upper limit 40) and HIV testing is negative.

  1. The correct answer is B (Check the INR frequently, expecting to raise the warfarin dose as rifampin induces metabolism): Rifampin is a strong inducer of CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 and lowers warfarin effect, often needing a doubling of the dose. The INR should be checked frequently, starting within a few days, and again when rifampin ends because the dose must come back down. Assuming the earlier stable INR will persist ignores the induction, and aspirin does not replace warfarin for thromboembolic prevention.
  2. The correct answer is C (Recheck ALT every two to four weeks and stop drugs if it exceeds 5x normal): Heavy alcohol use and a mildly raised baseline ALT increase the chance of drug induced liver injury, so liver enzymes are followed closely rather than left unchecked. A baseline ALT below three times the upper limit does not prevent treatment, because untreated tuberculosis is more dangerous. Drugs are stopped if ALT passes five times the upper limit, or three times with symptoms.
  3. The correct answer is A (Take pyridoxine with isoniazid for neuropathy risk, and report visual changes from ethambutol promptly): Isoniazid can cause peripheral neuropathy by depleting pyridoxine, and heavy alcohol use raises the risk, so pyridoxine is added. Ethambutol can cause optic neuritis, so any blurred vision or colour change should be reported at once. Orange secretions come from rifampin, not isoniazid, and pyridoxine does not reduce isoniazid activity.
  4. The correct answer is B (The relative reduction is 50%, but the absolute reduction is only 0.2 percentage points): Event rates of 0.4% and 0.2% give a 50% relative reduction but an absolute reduction of only 0.2 percentage points, or about 1 fewer event per 500 treated. Headlines that quote only the relative figure make small baseline risks sound large. Calling the figure an absolute reduction, or claiming half of patients benefit, misstates the data.
  5. The correct answer is D (100, because the absolute increase in major bleeding is 1 percentage point per year): The absolute risk increase is 3.0% minus 2.0%, or 1 percentage point. The number needed to harm is 1 divided by 0.01, which is 100 over one year. Dividing by a single group's event rate, quoting the count difference, or taking a ratio of rates does not give an NNH.
  6. The correct answer is A (Reduce the calculated dose by 25 to 50% because cross-tolerance is incomplete): Equianalgesic tables are approximations, and tolerance to one opioid does not transfer completely to another. Reducing the calculated dose by 25 to 50% and supplying breakthrough doses guards against overdose while pain is reassessed. Starting at 10% would leave her in severe pain and in withdrawal.
  7. The correct answer is D (Draw 0.5 mL from that vial, because 1 mL would give 10 000 units): The 10 000 units/mL vial gives 5000 units in 0.5 mL, so 1 mL would be a double dose and raise the bleeding risk. Picking the 5000 units/mL vial would let the nurse draw the full 1 mL, but the safest advice is to match the volume to the strength of the vial actually in hand.
  8. The correct answer is B (Contact the prescriber promptly, because dopamine agonists can cause impulse control disorders needing dose reduction): Dopamine agonists such as pramipexole are well known to cause impulse control disorders, including gambling, and the risk rises with dose. The prescriber should reduce or taper the drug under supervision. Abrupt discontinuation can cause dopamine agonist withdrawal and worsening motor function, and food timing does not change the behavioural effect.
  9. The correct answer is A (Diltiazem depresses contractility and can worsen heart failure, so a beta-blocker or digoxin is preferred): Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as diltiazem and verapamil depress contractility and are avoided in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction. A cautiously titrated beta-blocker or digoxin is a more suitable rate control strategy. Diltiazem does not raise potassium and does lower ventricular rate in atrial fibrillation.
  10. The correct answer is B (Identify affected patients, consult prescribers about equivalent alternatives, and counsel on the new device technique): Planning ahead lets the pharmacist find the affected patients, agree on an equivalent alternative with each prescriber, and teach the new device before a gap in therapy occurs. Waiting, posting a notice, or switching drugs without prescriber involvement risks missed doses, wrong device technique and unsupported therapy changes.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Dispense the product that the formulary lists as interchangeable with the one FT has taken): For modified-release products, the release system affects the concentration-time profile, so interchangeability is established product by product and recorded on the formulary listing. Using the listed product keeps FT's exposure consistent. Matching the milligram strength alone does not show equivalence.
  2. The correct answer is C (Product, lot number, expiry, dose, site, date, and pharmacist, with registry reporting as the province requires): Complete immunization records include product, lot number, expiry, dose, route and site, date, and the administering pharmacist, so that adverse events and recalls can be traced to individual patients. Most provinces also require the pharmacist to report the dose to the provincial immunization registry or the patient's physician. Purchasing files do not link lots to patients.
  3. The correct answer is B (Courier the drugs, then counsel by video with a device demonstration before first use): Remote counselling with a live demonstration meets the duty to confirm understanding of new devices when travel is impractical, and delivery maintains continuity of therapy. Printed sheets alone omit the demonstration, which matters most for insulin pens and inhalers. Withholding one drug or requiring a long winter trip creates avoidable risk and barriers.
  4. The correct answer is D (It cannot exceed the earliest expiry date of any ingredient, which here is 30 September): A beyond-use date for a compounded preparation cannot be later than the earliest expiry date among its components, and with no stability data for the finished paste that earliest date also bounds any default period. The later dates do not matter because the shortest-dated ingredient governs. A default of one year does not apply when an ingredient expires sooner.
  5. The correct answer is D (contact both prescribers with the pulse finding and request one agreed dose before filling): A pulse of 48 per minute after a dose increase is a safety signal, and two prescribers are giving opposing instructions. Filling either prescription without clarification risks bradycardia or loss of rate control. Direct communication with both prescribers resolves the conflict, and the pharmacist then documents the agreed plan. Specialist status does not override a safety based change.
  6. The correct answer is C (Use fosfomycin 3 g as a single dose, since her clearance is about 21 mL/min): Clearance is (140 - 82) x 52 x 1.04 / 150 = about 21 mL/min. Nitrofurantoin does not reach effective urinary concentrations at this level of renal function, so halving the dose does not rescue it. Fosfomycin as a single dose does not depend on renal function the same way, whereas trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole would need a reduction and carries potassium and creatinine concerns.
  7. The correct answer is D (Keep the box, because the longer provincial ten year retention period still applies to them): A federal minimum sets only the lowest retention period, and the pharmacy must also meet any longer provincial requirement. With ten years required, records from seven years ago must still be kept, whether or not they involve narcotics. Records belong to the pharmacy's retention duty and cannot be handed to prescribers.
  8. The correct answer is D (Advise her to remove the lenses and arrange urgent eye assessment for possible keratitis): Pain, photophobia and blurred vision in a lens wearer who slept in her lenses suggest corneal infection or ulcer, which can threaten sight within days. She needs urgent examination by an eye care provider. Lubricants, antihistamines and decongestants would delay diagnosis.
  9. The correct answer is C (A negative result, because a negative likelihood ratio of 0.05 sharply lowers the probability): A negative likelihood ratio of 0.05 lowers the odds of disease about twentyfold, which can rule out pulmonary embolism when the pretest probability is low. A positive likelihood ratio of 1.8 changes probability very little and cannot exclude anything. Likelihood ratios are specifically used to update disease probability.
  10. The correct answer is C (36 mg once daily in the morning, the table equivalent of 30 mg daily): The OROS conversion table equates immediate-release methylphenidate 10 mg three times daily (30 mg total) to 36 mg once daily. Extended-release strengths are 18, 27, 36 and 54 mg, so a one to one conversion to 30 mg is not available. The 18 mg dose corresponds to 5 mg three times daily and the 54 mg dose to 15 mg three times daily.
  11. The correct answer is B (42 g, calculated from 3 g per application, applied twice daily for seven days): Both arms need 2 x 3 x 0.5 g = 3 g per application. Twice daily gives 6 g per day, and seven days gives 42 g. Counting one arm, counting units as grams, or defaulting to a standard tube size each give a wrong total.
  12. The correct answer is B (An unopened, sealed bottle of metformin that expired two months ago): Wholesaler programs accept sealed, intact products inside the return window. Opened bottles and water-damaged stock do not qualify. Medication returned by a patient cannot be restocked or returned for credit because the pharmacy cannot guarantee how it was stored, and it must instead be destroyed through the pharmacy's waste program.
  13. The correct answer is C (It can cause miscarriage and birth defects, so effective contraception is needed): Misoprostol is a prostaglandin analogue that stimulates uterine contractions and is contraindicated in pregnancy, so women able to conceive need effective contraception. Its usual adverse effect is diarrhea and abdominal cramping, not constipation. It does not weaken diclofenac or raise blood pressure.
  14. The correct answer is D (Drug, quantity, date, and signatures of the pharmacist and the witness, filed in the narcotic records): Destruction of returned narcotics should leave a record naming the drug and quantity, the date, and both the person who destroyed it and the witness. Dropping the witness signature or the quantity makes the record unverifiable. Weight alone cannot show how many tablets were destroyed.
  15. The correct answer is C (Treat the result as biased by non-random allocation and seek adjusted or randomized evidence first): Patients were not assigned at random, and the drug group had a better baseline prognosis plus more intensive follow-up, so the lower readmission may reflect who received the drug. Larger samples do not fix bias. The finding is uninterpretable until adjusted analysis or randomized data are available, not proof of no effect.
  16. The correct answer is A (Sweating may remain, but tremor and palpitations may fade, so more frequent testing is wise): Nonselective beta-blockers can mask adrenergic warning signs such as tremor and palpitations, while sweating is cholinergic and usually persists. She should rely on glucose testing more than on symptoms. Claiming that all symptoms vanish, that they increase, or that lows become less likely would mislead her about her risk.
  17. The correct answer is D (Amoxicillin clavulanate combined with a macrolide or doxycycline for broader coverage of resistant pathogens): Comorbid COPD and diabetes widen the likely pathogens to include Haemophilus influenzae and beta-lactamase producers, so a beta-lactamase inhibitor combination plus atypical coverage is advised. Monotherapy with amoxicillin or doxycycline is intended for healthy adults. Aspiration is not the usual cause here.
  18. The correct answer is D (Contact her prescriber promptly about changing to labetalol or nifedipine, which suit pregnancy): ACE inhibitors and ARBs are fetotoxic and should be stopped as soon as pregnancy is recognized, because exposure can harm fetal kidney development. Losartan shares the same risk, and a lower ramipril dose does not remove it. Labetalol, nifedipine and methyldopa are the usual antihypertensive choices in pregnancy.
  19. The correct answer is C (Metoclopramide, because its central dopamine blockade can worsen his Parkinson motor symptoms and reduce levodopa effect): Metoclopramide crosses the blood-brain barrier and antagonizes dopamine D2 receptors, which can worsen parkinsonism and oppose levodopa. Ondansetron does not act on dopamine receptors, and domperidone has little central penetration, so both are generally preferred in Parkinson disease. Antihistamines do not impair levodopa absorption.
  20. The correct answer is A (Yes, because for a rare outcome the odds and the risk are nearly equal): When an outcome is rare, the odds of the event are nearly equal to its risk, so the odds ratio approximates the relative risk. With a 0.5% incidence this holds well. The approximation fails for common outcomes, where the odds ratio overstates the relative risk, and the two measures are not identical in general.
  21. The correct answer is B (Match the drug name and DIN on the stock bottle to the prescription): The most reliable defence against similar drug names is to compare the full name and DIN on the stock container with the prescription. Memory of tablet appearance is unreliable because manufacturers change products. The first letters of hydroxyzine and hydralazine are identical, and moving bins helps selection but does not replace the label comparison at verification.
  22. The correct answer is D (Write 1 mg without the trailing zero, since 1.0 can be read as 10): A trailing zero after a decimal point, such as 1.0 mg, can be missed when the decimal is faint or the page is copied, so the dose may be read as 10 mg, a tenfold overdose. ISMP Canada advises writing whole number doses without a trailing zero. Extra zeros, underlining or verbal repetition do not remove the risk from the written order.
  23. The correct answer is A (recognize additive AV nodal blockade and urgently ask the prescriber to stop one drug): A beta-blocker plus a non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker can cause marked bradycardia and heart block through additive sinus node and AV nodal depression. A rate of 44 beats/min with dizziness calls for prompt prescriber review, not reassurance, food advice or more rate-slowing drugs.
  24. The correct answer is A (ask the prescriber about an angiotensin receptor blocker, since ramipril is the likely cause): A dry, persistent cough that begins weeks after starting an ACE inhibitor is a recognized class effect caused by bradykinin accumulation. It rarely responds to cough suppressants and resolves after the drug is changed, usually to an angiotensin receptor blocker. Stopping an antihypertensive without a plan is unsafe, and an expectorant does not treat a dry cough.
  25. The correct answer is B (464 tablets: four unopened bottles and 64 tablets remaining in the partly used bottle): Eight bottles hold 800 tablets. The three full bottles dispensed account for 300 and the 36 tablets taken from the fourth bottle leave 64 in it, so 800 minus 300 minus 36 equals 464. That total is four sealed bottles (400) plus 64 tablets.
  26. The correct answer is C (Report it to Health Canada, since hospitals must report serious reactions within 30 days of documentation): Under Vanessa's Law, hospitals must report serious adverse drug reactions to Health Canada within 30 days of first documenting them, whether or not the reaction is already listed in the monograph. Monograph labelling and committee schedules do not remove or delay that obligation, and the duty rests with the hospital rather than the manufacturer.
  27. The correct answer is D (A second person verifies the product against the order without seeing prior results): An independent check means the second person works from the original order without being influenced by the first person's results, so an error is not simply confirmed by agreement. Reviewing completed work, repeating the steps as the same person, or listening to numbers read aloud all allow the first error to pass unnoticed.
  28. The correct answer is B (In a part of the pharmacy the public cannot reach, so customers cannot self select): Schedule II products need no prescription but must sit in an area with no public access, so the pharmacist can assess the need and advise before the sale. Open shelves within sight of a pharmacist describe Schedule III. Any retail outlet applies to unscheduled products, and a prescription is the requirement for Schedule I.
  29. The correct answer is B (Record assessment, rationale, plan and follow up, and notify the primary care provider if appropriate): Prescribing under minor ailment authority is a professional service requiring a documented assessment, the rationale for treatment, the plan, and arranged follow up. Notifying the primary care provider supports continuity of care. Sales records or a label alone do not meet regulatory standards.
  30. The correct answer is D (Ask what traditional medicine she plans to use, check interactions, and respect her decision): Cultural humility means inviting her to share what matters and respecting her right to include traditional healing. Asking what she plans to use allows interactions to be checked, which keeps her safe, while respecting her decision supports trust damaged by past dismissive care. Dismissing the practice, requiring physician permission or ending the visit repeats the harm.
  31. The correct answer is B (Recommend gradual dose reduction with her prescriber, plus cognitive behavioural therapy and sleep hygiene): Benzodiazepines in older adults increase falls, fractures and cognitive impairment, and Choosing Wisely Canada advises against long-term use for insomnia or anxiety. A slow taper with behavioural support is the recommended approach. Long-acting agents accumulate and are more harmful, and abrupt stopping risks withdrawal seizures.
  32. The correct answer is C (Continue and titrate slowly, because benefit is judged after two to three months of therapy): Preventive migraine drugs need titration to an effective dose and a trial of roughly two to three months before benefit can be judged. Ten milligrams is a starting dose only, so a lack of response now says little. Doubling the dose abruptly would worsen anticholinergic effects and sedation without speeding benefit.
Case CHB13

MK is a 4 year old girl brought to a community pharmacy by her mother with acute otitis media. She weighs 16 kg, is 102 cm tall and has no drug allergies. She has not taken an antibiotic in the past three months. Her physician prescribed amoxicillin 90 mg/kg/day divided into two doses for ten days. The pharmacy stocks amoxicillin 400 mg/5 mL suspension and acetaminophen 160 mg/5 mL suspension. Her mother also wants to treat the fever with acetaminophen at 15 mg/kg per dose.

  1. The correct answer is C (9 mL per dose, because each dose of 720 mg is contained in that volume): The daily dose is 90 mg/kg x 16 kg = 1440 mg, divided into two doses of 720 mg. At 400 mg per 5 mL, 720 mg is 720 / 400 x 5 = 9 mL. Giving the whole daily total as one measure doubles the dose, and 4.5 mL or 5 mL matches neither the weight-based calculation nor twice daily dosing.
  2. The correct answer is D (7.5 mL, which delivers 240 mg and matches the 15 mg/kg dose for her weight): A dose of 15 mg/kg x 16 kg is 240 mg, and 240 mg at 160 mg per 5 mL is 7.5 mL. A 5 mL measure gives only 10 mg/kg, 10 mL gives 20 mg/kg, and 3.75 mL gives half the intended dose.
  3. The correct answer is C (Limit doses to five in 24 hours, since six doses would exceed 75 mg/kg per day): Pediatric acetaminophen is limited to 75 mg/kg/day, which for 16 kg is 1200 mg, or five doses of 240 mg. Six doses total 1440 mg, which is 90 mg/kg/day and raises the risk of liver injury. The adult maximum does not apply to a child, and household spoons are not accurate for measuring a dose.
  4. The correct answer is A (No, because an NNT depends on follow-up time and the one-year value is stronger): An NNT is only meaningful alongside its follow-up time. Preventing one event per 40 patients in one year is a much stronger effect than the same yield accumulated over five years. NNTs from different durations cannot be compared directly, and nothing in the data suggests a dose difference.
  5. The correct answer is A (The Mann-Whitney U test, because the data are skewed and the groups are independent): Length of stay is strongly skewed with extreme values and the groups are independent, so a non-parametric test of ranks, the Mann-Whitney U test, is appropriate. A t-test assumes approximately normal data. The Wilcoxon signed-rank test is for paired data, and a chi-square test needs categorical data.
  6. The correct answer is B (Give the second dose 2 to 6 months after the first dose): NACI recommends two doses of recombinant zoster vaccine given 2 to 6 months apart in immunocompetent adults, with a shorter interval of 1 to 2 months possible for those who are or will be immunosuppressed. A single dose does not complete the series. A 7 to 14 day interval is too short and a 12 month interval is too long.
  7. The correct answer is A (a pressurized metered dose inhaler with a valved holding chamber, which needs minimal inspiratory force): Dry powder inhalers depend on a brisk inspiratory flow to break up the powder, so a patient with weak effort will deposit little drug whatever the strength or brand. A pressurized inhaler with a holding chamber delivers a slow-moving aerosol that can be inhaled with tidal breaths. Theophylline is a poor replacement because of its narrow therapeutic range and interactions, and inhaled therapy remains appropriate.
  8. The correct answer is C (Retrieve the wrong medication, supply the correct one, and arrange contact with PD and incident documentation): The wrong medication must be retrieved so it cannot be taken, and PD may have gone without his supply or be holding hers, so he must be contacted. Documenting the event allows the team to examine why the bag was handed over. Waiting for a complaint leaves another patient exposed.
  9. The correct answer is B (Short sentences in common words, written at about a grade 6 reading level): Plain language materials use short sentences, familiar words and a reading level near grade 6 so most adults can follow them. Technical terms, dense text and small decorative type raise the reading burden and are hard for patients with limited health literacy.
  10. The correct answer is D (Listen without interrupting, acknowledge his frustration, and invite him to explain what happened): Angry people calm more quickly when they feel heard. Listening without interrupting and naming the frustration lowers tension and gives the pharmacist the facts needed to fix the problem. Explaining policy, issuing warnings or offering a discount early can feel dismissive or escalate the situation before the concern is understood.
  11. The correct answer is C (Ask privately how he wants to be addressed, then use that name aloud): Asking privately how a patient wishes to be addressed respects identity and avoids disclosing sensitive information to others. The pharmacist can still use the legal name where records or billing require it, while using the chosen name in conversation. Announcing names or questioning identity publicly breaches privacy and causes harm.
  12. The correct answer is D (Dabigatran capsules, because moisture quickly degrades them once removed from the original packaging): Dabigatran capsules are sensitive to moisture and the monograph requires them to stay in the original bottle or blister until taken. The other three drugs are stable in compliance packaging and are routinely packed together. Excluding the sensitive product protects potency and is documented on the profile.
  13. The correct answer is C (Suggest allergy assessment before labelling her, as rashes during mononucleosis are often not allergic): A non-urticarial rash during infectious mononucleosis after an aminopenicillin is common and usually reflects the viral illness rather than true allergy. Labelling her now would steer her away from first line antibiotics for life. Formal assessment lets the label be assigned or avoided correctly, and an auto-injector is not indicated.
  14. The correct answer is B (Contact police promptly with the specific threat details, since imminent serious harm overrides confidentiality duties): The courts and professional codes recognize an exception to confidentiality when there is a clear, serious and imminent risk of death or serious bodily harm to an identifiable person. Disclosure should go to those who can prevent the harm, and it should be limited to what is needed.
  15. The correct answer is C (About 80 morphine milligram equivalents per day, which exceeds the 50 threshold for added caution): Oxycodone totals 40 mg daily, which is 60 morphine milligram equivalents at a factor of 1.5. Tramadol totals 100 mg daily, which is 20 at a factor of 0.2, giving 80 in total. That exceeds the 50 level at which caution and reassessment are advised, but it remains below 90.
  16. The correct answer is B (Tell the eye surgeon about tamsulosin, since it can cause floppy iris syndrome during cataract surgery): Tamsulosin and other alpha blockers are associated with intraoperative floppy iris syndrome, and the surgeon can plan technique if informed. Stopping the drug beforehand does not reliably prevent the problem because the effect persists for a long time. The surgeon should be informed rather than changing therapy.
  17. The correct answer is D (A gain of 1 kg in one day or 2 kg within one week): Rapid weight gain reflects fluid accumulation days before symptoms appear. A gain of about 1 kg in a day or 2 kg in a week is the usual trigger for contacting the team so the diuretic can be adjusted. Waiting for visible swelling or a month of gain delays treatment.
  18. The correct answer is A (Repeat in 7 to 10 days to kill newly hatched nymphs, and comb out nits): Permethrin kills live lice but is only partly effective against eggs, so nymphs that hatch afterwards must be treated with a second application 7 to 10 days later, before they can mature and lay eggs. Daily repeat use adds irritation without benefit, and waiting months allows reinfestation.
  19. The correct answer is C (Smoke may induce CYP1A2 and lower clozapine levels, and THC can worsen psychotic symptoms): Inhaled smoke from burning plant material induces CYP1A2, the main enzyme clearing clozapine, so levels can fall and psychosis may relapse. THC itself can also worsen psychotic symptoms. Cannabis does not cause agranulocytosis, and blood monitoring for clozapine relates to the drug itself.
  20. The correct answer is D (Her overnight basal rate, with a possible reduction guided by her diabetes team): Lows occurring about nine hours after the final bolus fall outside the action time of rapid-acting insulin, so the continuous basal delivery is the likely driver. A basal rate test and reduction guided by her team addresses the cause. Changing bolus settings or adding food treats the symptom or targets the wrong insulin.
  21. The correct answer is C (The pen holds 1500 units, since U-500 means 500 units in each mL): The number after the U states units per mL, so U-500 contains 500 units per mL and 3 mL holds 1500 units. It is five times as concentrated as U-100, which is why the dosing device and the counselling must be specific to U-500.
  22. The correct answer is C (Weigh 120 mg of drug, dilute to 1,200 mg, and take 600 mg of mixture): The least weighable quantity is 6 mg divided by 0.05, which is 120 mg, so 60 mg is below what the balance can weigh accurately. Mixing 120 mg of drug into a total of 1,200 mg makes a 10% mixture, and 600 mg of that mixture holds exactly 60 mg of drug. Using 300 mg of the mixture would supply only 30 mg, and 200 mg of a 600 mg mixture supplies only 40 mg.
  23. The correct answer is D (Hold simvastatin during the five day course and for three days afterward): Ritonavir is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor that sharply raises simvastatin exposure and the risk of rhabdomyolysis, so simvastatin is held during treatment and for a few days after the inhibition fades. Spacing the doses does not help because the inhibition lasts all day. Amlodipine can usually be continued with monitoring.
  24. The correct answer is A (A very large magnitude of effect with a clear dose-response gradient across exposure levels): GRADE allows observational evidence to be upgraded for a large effect, a dose-response gradient, or when plausible residual confounding would reduce the observed effect. Sample size, adjustment and journal prestige do not raise certainty because unmeasured confounding can still remain.
  25. The correct answer is A (Inject 0.5 mg of glucagon subcutaneously or intramuscularly, then call emergency services): A child under 25 kg receives the lower dose of 0.5 mg given by injection, and emergency services must be called. The dose is chosen by weight, not by how severe the episode is. Oral glucose or gel is unsafe when a patient cannot swallow, and glucagon is not effective by mouth.
  26. The correct answer is B (720 mg twice daily, because two 500 mg units correspond to two 360 mg units): The conversion is 500 mg of mycophenolate mofetil to 360 mg of mycophenolic acid, so 1000 mg equals 720 mg per dose. Giving the same milligram amount would overdose, and the other values come from halving or multiplying incorrectly.
  27. The correct answer is A (Cap the dose at the adult maximum of 4 g daily in divided doses): Weight based pediatric doses are not allowed to exceed the usual adult maximum, which is the ceiling for large children and adolescents. Capping at 4 g gives about 1 g every six hours. Halving the dose has no basis, and rounding up would go further past the limit.
  28. The correct answer is D (Ask which concern matters most to her, then explore cost and swelling solutions together): Adherence problems have identifiable causes, here cost and an adverse effect, and addressing them requires understanding her priorities first. Raising doses would worsen swelling and cost, and telling her to comply or labelling her non-adherent ignores the barriers.
  29. The correct answer is B (Give 8.4 mL per dose twice daily, which delivers 675 mg per dose): The daily dose is 90 x 15 = 1350 mg, so each of two doses is 675 mg. At 400 mg per 5 mL, 675 mg equals 8.4 mL. The other volumes come from using 60 mg/kg/day, giving the whole daily dose at once, or dividing by four.
  30. The correct answer is A (AUC to MIC ratio of 400 to 600, which reduces nephrotoxicity compared with trough targeting): The 2020 consensus guideline recommends AUC guided dosing with an AUC to MIC target of 400 to 600 for serious MRSA infection, as trough targets of 15 to 20 mg/L increase kidney injury without clear benefit. Peak levels are not used. Very low troughs may be subtherapeutic for bacteremia.
  31. The correct answer is A (They are strongly anticholinergic and raise the risk of confusion, urinary retention and falls): Diphenhydramine blocks muscarinic receptors in the brain and bladder, which worsens cognition, causes urinary retention and constipation, and adds to fall risk. This anticholinergic burden, not tolerance, is why geriatric criteria list first-generation antihistamines. Renal accumulation and blood pressure interactions are not the main concerns with this drug.
  32. The correct answer is D (Continue it and tell her prescriber, since a single lapse does not end treatment): Acamprosate supports abstinence by restoring glutamate and GABA balance and is not harmful if alcohol is consumed, so patients continue it through a lapse and tell the prescriber. Skipping doses or doubling them has no benefit, and a double dose adds diarrhea. Treatment is usually kept going for several months to a year.
  33. The correct answer is D (Keep the tablets in the original glass bottle, tightly closed, away from heat and moisture): Nitroglycerin tablets lose potency when exposed to light, heat, air and moisture, and the drug can be absorbed by some plastics. The tightly closed glass bottle protects potency, so the tablets should stay in it. Refrigeration is not required and can add moisture when the bottle is opened.
  34. The correct answer is A (Recommend same day medical assessment for suspected impetigo superinfection and hold any steroid changes): Weeping, honey coloured crusts, fissures and fever in a child point to secondary bacterial infection, which needs examination and often antibacterial treatment. A more potent steroid can worsen infection, and an antifungal or wet wraps do not treat it. Steroid decisions follow the assessment.
  35. The correct answer is D (Ask the prescriber for 80 mg once daily, because her clearance is below 30 mL/min): Cockcroft-Gault gives (140 - 74) x 82 x 1.04 / 190, which is about 29.6 mL/min, below the 30 mL/min cutoff. Below that cutoff enoxaparin accumulates, and the treatment dose is reduced to 1 mg/kg once daily, about 80 mg. Halving each dose while keeping the twelve hour interval, or waiting for 15 mL/min, does not match this adjustment.
  36. The correct answer is B (Amiodarone with sofosbuvir can cause serious bradycardia, so alternatives or cardiac monitoring are needed): Sofosbuvir given with amiodarone has caused severe symptomatic bradycardia, including cases needing a pacemaker, probably through an effect on cardiac conduction. The prescriber should consider another antiarrhythmic or arrange cardiac monitoring at the start. Rosuvastatin levels actually rise with velpatasvir, which is a dose caution rather than a reason to double it, and ramipril has no meaningful interaction.
  37. The correct answer is A (About 2.5 mmol/L, found by dividing 100 by the 40 unit daily total): The correction factor equals 100 divided by the total daily dose, so 100 divided by 40 gives 2.5 mmol/L per unit. The 500 rule is used for the carbohydrate ratio, giving grams per unit rather than glucose lowering. Inverting the division or using 200 gives values that do not match the rule.
  38. The correct answer is A (Identifying the title of pharmacy student and arranging for the supervising pharmacist to answer the question): Protected titles belong to registered pharmacists, so a student must identify accurately to avoid misleading patients. Patient counselling that needs a pharmacist's judgement must be done by the supervising pharmacist, and after-the-fact review does not make a student's direct answer acceptable.
  39. The correct answer is D (Doxycycline 100 mg twice daily, which avoids beta-lactams and covers the usual community pathogens): With a documented anaphylactic reaction, penicillins including amoxicillin clavulanate and cloxacillin should be avoided because they share the beta-lactam core. Doxycycline is an accepted alternative for a healthy adult with mild pneumonia. Nitrofurantoin reaches only urinary concentrations and has no respiratory role.
  40. The correct answer is A (Patients decide whether care felt respectful, and providers work to address bias and power imbalances): Cultural safety is defined by the patient's experience of feeling respected and free from discrimination, and it requires providers to reflect on their own bias and on the power imbalance in the relationship. Learning customs or completing training builds awareness but does not guarantee safety. Identical treatment ignores individual and historical context.
  41. The correct answer is C (Drug Shortages Canada, the federal website where manufacturers post mandatory shortage and discontinuation reports): Drug Shortages Canada is the federal site where shortage and discontinuation reports from manufacturers are posted. Canada Vigilance collects adverse reaction reports, the Drug Product Database lists authorized products rather than their supply, and provincial formularies are not the national source of shortage information.
  42. The correct answer is D (aspirin plus clopidogrel for 21 days, followed by a single antiplatelet agent long term): Short-course dual antiplatelet therapy started within 24 hours of a minor stroke or high-risk TIA reduces early recurrence, and it is limited to about 21 days because longer use adds bleeding without more benefit. Warfarin is not indicated without atrial fibrillation or another cardiac source.
  43. The correct answer is D (Advise acetaminophen, since NSAIDs cause sodium and water retention and can worsen heart failure): NSAIDs, including COX-2 selective agents, retain sodium and water, blunt the effect of diuretics and ACE inhibitors, and raise the risk of heart failure hospitalization. Acetaminophen at regular doses is the preferred analgesic. Short courses and lower doses reduce but do not remove the risk.
  44. The correct answer is A (Two effective methods together, starting one month before and ending one month after treatment): Isotretinoin is a potent teratogen, so the Canadian program asks for two effective forms of contraception, at least one of them primary, from at least one month before treatment until one month after the last dose. A single method or a shorter window leaves the embryo exposed.
  45. The correct answer is D (an SGLT2 inhibitor such as dapagliflozin for kidney and heart protection, despite modest glucose lowering): In diabetic kidney disease with albuminuria, SGLT2 inhibitors slow kidney decline and reduce cardiovascular events even when eGFR is reduced, with modest glycemic effect at this eGFR. Glyburide is risky in kidney disease, and pioglitazone and saxagliptin raise heart failure concerns.
  46. The correct answer is B (Disclose the cost difference and the facility interest, then offer the commercial product to MS): AB has a financial interest in the facility, and MS has said that cost matters to her. Disclosing the interest and the price difference, then offering the equivalent commercial product, puts the patient's interest first. Dispensing the compounded product or steering MS without full information serves the facility rather than MS.
  47. The correct answer is A (Call 911 and give first aid, because conscientious objection does not apply to emergency care): Conscientious objection does not apply to emergency care, and a patient who is bleeding heavily, dizzy, and pale needs immediate help. Calling emergency services and giving first aid addresses the danger first. Redirecting her to another pharmacy or the prescriber, or stepping away, would delay urgent care.
  48. The correct answer is A (60 umol/L, which falls within the usual therapeutic range for total phenytoin): Albumin of 20 g/L is 2.0 g/dL, so the adjusted level is 30 / (0.2 x 2.0 + 0.1) = 30 / 0.5 = 60 umol/L. Low albumin leaves more phenytoin unbound, so the total level underestimates the effect and the dose should not be raised on the basis of the raw value. Entering g/L instead of g/dL gives a meaningless result.
  49. Technique should be corrected with a validated upper arm device before readings are repeated. Starting a drug or abandoning home monitoring would act on unreliable data.
  50. The correct answer is B (Recommend reducing acetylsalicylic acid to 81 mg daily, since higher doses weaken ticagrelor's benefit): Maintenance acetylsalicylic acid doses above 150 mg daily reduce the clinical benefit of ticagrelor, so a low dose such as 81 mg daily is used with it. Ticagrelor is meant to be taken with acetylsalicylic acid, not instead of it. Clopidogrel is dosed at 75 mg daily for maintenance and is not a better partner for a higher acetylsalicylic acid dose.
  51. Dose adjustment is usually assessed after two to four weeks at a tolerated dose. Adding a benzodiazepine for months creates dependence risk without treating depression.
  52. The correct answer is A (Take 2 mL by mouth twice daily; dispense 120 mL for the 30 day course): The 20 mg dose of a 10 mg/mL solution is 2 mL. Two doses daily for 30 days is 2 x 2 x 30 = 120 mL. Dispensing 60 mL would cover only 15 days, and the other volumes come from a tenfold decimal error in either direction.
  53. The correct answer is B (About 12 mg, which is 10% of the total daily dose given): A breakthrough dose is usually 10 to 15% of the total daily opioid dose, so 12 to 18 mg for 120 mg per day. Twelve milligrams is the only choice in that range. Doses of 24 mg or more exceed the usual breakthrough amount and raise the risk of sedation and respiratory depression.
  54. The labelled titration with valproate begins at 25 mg every other day. Faster titration is a recognized risk factor for Stevens-Johnson syndrome.
  55. Searching primary literature and submitting a report to Health Canada's Canada Vigilance program both add to the evidence. Absence from a tertiary source does not rule out a drug effect.

Informal discussion, covering up or leaving action to the owner delays protection of patients and does not meet the reporting duty.
  58. Discontinuation should be gradual to avoid withdrawal symptoms. Early or abrupt stopping raises the chance of relapse and discontinuation syndrome.
  59. Slow decline in levels does not make a full-dose restart safe. Self-directed half doses are not a recognized plan.
  60.   61. Nothing should sit between the HEPA filter and an open vial or needle, because that unobstructed clean airflow protects the critical site.   88. A 200 MME threshold is far too high, and a dose-independent rule or a 20 MME ceiling does not match the guidance.
  89. The correct answer is C (The intravenous dose is usually lower, about 100 mg, because injection delivers the whole dose): Intravenous administration has a bioavailability of 100 percent, so a drug that is 50 percent bioavailable orally needs roughly half the oral dose given intravenously.   90. Poor metabolizers produce much less of it, giving weaker platelet inhibition and a higher risk of stent thrombosis, so an alternative such as ticagrelor or prasugrel is often chosen. Accumulation and renal mechanisms do not apply.
  91. Tea, juice or food binds the drug and cuts absorption. Lying down raises the risk of esophageal injury.

The horizontal line is the confidence interval, and a short line means a more precise estimate. Square size and line length say nothing directly about the size of the effect or study quality.
