A short gap of 30 minutes is not sufficient. Because the interaction can raise TSH, a recheck in the following weeks confirms that the dose remains adequate.
  125. The correct answer is B (The absolute benefit is larger in the high risk women despite identical relative risk): The same relative risk applies a constant proportional reduction, so absolute benefit scales with baseline risk: 0.25 percentage points at 1% versus 2.5 percentage points at 10%. High risk women therefore gain more per person treated. Relative risks from different populations can still be compared once baseline risk is considered.
  126. The correct answer is B (Stop the isotretinoin now and contact her prescriber urgently for pregnancy confirmation and counselling): Isotretinoin causes major malformations at any point in pregnancy, so every further dose adds risk and the drug must be stopped immediately. Her prescriber must confirm the pregnancy and arrange counselling. Finishing the week, halving the dose or adding contraception now does not remove fetal exposure.
  127. The correct answer is C (94%, because 423 of 450 patients without influenza had a negative test): Specificity is the proportion of patients without influenza who test negative. With 27 false positives among 450, 423 tested negative, which is 94%. The 90% figure is sensitivity, 6% is the false positive rate, and 63% is the positive predictive value.
  128. The correct answer is B (Bacterial vaginosis, which causes thin grey discharge, fishy odour and elevated pH): Bacterial vaginosis produces a thin homogeneous grey discharge, an amine odour that worsens after intercourse and a vaginal pH above 4.5. Candidiasis causes thick white discharge with itching and a normal pH. Trichomoniasis typically brings frothy discharge, itching and urinary symptoms, which she does not have.
  129. The correct answer is D (Carbamazepine may lower implant hormone levels, so add condoms or consider switching to an IUD): Carbamazepine induces CYP3A4 and speeds clearance of etonogestrel, so implant failure is more likely, and subcutaneous delivery does not bypass hepatic metabolism. Replacing the implant more often is not an established fix. A copper IUD or levonorgestrel IUS is unaffected, and condoms are needed in the meantime.
  130. The correct answer is A (Explain the point of care result is a screen only, and refer for laboratory testing): Diabetes Canada does not support diagnosis from point of care A1c devices, so an abnormal screen needs confirmation with a laboratory A1c or glucose test. Her result sits in the range suggesting prediabetes, together with risk factors, which warrants follow up and lifestyle counselling. Treatment is not started on a screening value.
  131. The correct answer is B (Store everything securely out of reach until it can be returned to a participating pharmacy): Distance does not change the preferred route. Medication and sharps should be held securely, away from children and pets, until they can be taken to a take-back site, which is safer than flushing or burning. Pouring drugs into soil and burning tablets both release drug and combustion products into the environment.
  132. The correct answer is D (25 patients, from the inverse of the 4 percentage point absolute risk reduction): The absolute risk reduction is 12% minus 8%, or 4 percentage points (0.04), and the number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.04, which equals 25. Using the relative risk reduction or a single group's event rate does not measure the absolute benefit and gives misleading numbers.
  133. The correct answer is D (Dried cannabis has no DIN and is accessed through licensed sellers, not pharmacist dispensing): Dried cannabis has no DIN and is not a drug product that pharmacists dispense. Access for medical purposes is through licensed sellers with a health care practitioner's document, or through provincially authorized retailers. NAPRA drug schedules and verbal narcotic prescription rules do not apply to cannabis.
  134. The correct answer is A (Arrange throat swab or rapid antigen testing before any antibiotic is started): A high McIsaac score raises the probability of group A streptococcal pharyngitis but does not confirm it, so guidelines recommend testing before antibiotics to avoid unnecessary use. Skipping testing risks missed treatment and rheumatic complications. Analgesia can be offered while awaiting results.
  135. The correct answer is D (Level C, because it involves a hazardous drug and needs specialized containment, training and procedures): Methotrexate is on the NIOSH list of hazardous drugs, and NAPRA places non-sterile hazardous compounding in the highest complexity level, which requires containment, trained staff and specific procedures. Capsule form and low dose do not lower the classification to the lowest level. Experience with oral liquids does not qualify a pharmacy for hazardous work.
  136. The correct answer is B (Ask the prescriber to add an inhaled corticosteroid, as LABA monotherapy raises serious asthma risk): A long-acting beta agonist used without an inhaled corticosteroid is associated with increased risk of severe exacerbations and is not recommended in asthma. His two emergency visits show the current plan is failing. Raising the LABA dose or scheduling salbutamol adds more bronchodilator without treating inflammation.
  137. The correct answer is A (Admission odds fell significantly, but the hazard ratio interval includes 1, so benefit is unproven): The odds ratio interval of 0.45 to 0.80 excludes 1, so the reduction in admission is statistically significant. The hazard ratio interval of 0.58 to 1.02 includes 1, so an effect on time to first exacerbation is not demonstrated. A ratio below 1 alone does not establish benefit, and both measures are valid in trials.
  138. The correct answer is B (Report the suspicion promptly to the child welfare agency, without waiting for proof of abuse): The duty to report is triggered by reasonable grounds to suspect a child is at risk, not by certainty. The duty belongs personally to the professional, parental permission is not required, and telling another professional does not discharge it.
  139. The correct answer is C (Advise against naproxen, since it can worsen heart failure and kidney function; try scheduled acetaminophen): NSAIDs cause sodium and water retention and reduce renal perfusion, and with an ACE inhibitor and a diuretic they raise the risk of decompensated heart failure and kidney injury. Short acting NSAIDs carry the same risk, and a proton pump inhibitor only protects the stomach. Scheduled acetaminophen, up to the daily limit for an older adult, is the safer step before other alternatives.
  140. The correct answer is C (Carbamide peroxide ear drops for several days to soften wax, then gentle irrigation if needed): Cerumenolytic drops such as carbamide peroxide soften impacted wax, and gentle irrigation can then remove it when the eardrum is intact. Ear candling is ineffective and can burn the skin or block the canal. Cotton swabs push wax deeper and strong peroxide or alcohol irritates the canal.
  141. The correct answer is A (That the substance is intended for medical assistance in dying for the named patient): The Criminal Code exempts a pharmacist who dispenses a substance on a practitioner's prescription once the pharmacist has been informed that it is intended for medical assistance in dying. Diagnosis details and written approvals from a college are not required for dispensing. The pharmacist is not asked to re-verify eligibility, which belongs to the assessing practitioners.
  142. The correct answer is A (21 tablets, made up of 12, then 6, then 3 across the steps): With 5 mg tablets the doses need 4, 2 and 1 tablets daily. Over three days each step uses 12, 6 and 3 tablets, which totals 21. A written calendar showing tablets per day would suit a patient with limited numeracy.
  143. The correct answer is B (A prescribing cascade; recommend reassessing oxybutynin, which opposes donepezil and may worsen cognition): Cholinesterase inhibitors can cause urinary urgency, and treating that effect with an anticholinergic starts a prescribing cascade. Oxybutynin blocks the acetylcholine action donepezil is meant to support and worsens confusion and dry mouth. The better response is to reassess the bladder drug, not escalate or add anticholinergics.
  144. The correct answer is D (Her husband needs simultaneous treatment, and both should abstain until therapy is complete): Trichomoniasis is a sexually transmitted infection, and men often carry it without symptoms, so partners are treated at the same time to prevent reinfection. Intercourse is held until both have completed treatment and any symptoms have resolved. Antifungals do not act on Trichomonas, and waiting for symptoms keeps the cycle of reinfection going.
  145. The correct answer is B (Pharmaceutical grade from a Health Canada licensed supplier with a certificate of analysis for the lot): NAPRA standards require active ingredients of pharmaceutical grade from a reputable, licensed source, supported by a certificate of analysis that verifies identity and purity for the lot. Chemical, food and veterinary grades are not manufactured or tested to the standard needed for human use. A low price or a verbal assurance does not replace documented quality.
  146. The correct answer is B (Roll the pen gently until evenly cloudy, then inject about 30 minutes before eating): Cloudy premixed human insulin must be resuspended by gently rolling or inverting the pen, since vigorous shaking creates bubbles and dosing errors. The regular insulin component needs about 30 minutes to start acting, so the injection is timed before the meal. Injecting after eating would mismatch the insulin peak with the meal glucose rise.
  147. The correct answer is A (take 15 g of fast-acting carbohydrate such as juice, then recheck in 15 minutes): For a conscious patient with mild to moderate hypoglycemia, 15 g of fast-acting carbohydrate is given and glucose is rechecked in 15 minutes, repeating if still below 4.0 mmol/L. Chocolate is absorbed too slowly, and delaying the recheck risks missing ongoing hypoglycemia.
  148. The correct answer is A (Search the eight digit number in Health Canada's Drug Product Database and confirm product details): The eight digit number is a Drug Identification Number, and the Drug Product Database lists each one with its ingredients, strength, form and status. Bilingual labelling, a Canadian address and invoice matching are all useful checks, but none of them shows that Health Canada authorized the product.
  149. The correct answer is C (About 6 mg daily, using 0.75 mg dexamethasone for each 5 mg prednisone): Forty milligrams of prednisone is eight units of 5 mg, and eight times 0.75 mg gives 6 mg of dexamethasone. Dexamethasone is far more potent than prednisone, so matching milligram doses or giving a near equal weight dramatically overdoses the patient. Underestimating the potency would instead undertreat the condition.
  150. The correct answer is C (The unexpected severe skin reaction, because serious unexpected reactions to new drugs are most valuable): Health Canada asks health professionals to prioritize serious reactions, unexpected reactions and any reaction to recently marketed products, because these create safety signals. Common, labelled reactions add little. Pharmacists in all settings may report voluntarily, and managing the patient does not replace reporting.
Case CHB14

MV is a 78 year old woman with heart failure and atrial fibrillation. She takes digoxin 0.25 mg daily, furosemide 40 mg twice daily, ramipril 5 mg daily and amiodarone 200 mg daily, which was added two weeks ago. She has nausea and sees yellow halos. Serum digoxin is 2.6 nmol/L (target 0.6 to 1.2), potassium is 3.1 mmol/L, and creatinine is 130 umol/L.

  1. The correct answer is A (amiodarone inhibits P-glycoprotein and reduces digoxin clearance, which increases serum digoxin concentrations): Amiodarone inhibits P-glycoprotein and lowers renal and nonrenal digoxin clearance, often doubling levels. Her reduced renal function adds to accumulation. The other mechanisms are not established interactions with digoxin.
  2. The correct answer is C (hold digoxin, obtain an ECG, and correct the low potassium under medical supervision): Her symptoms and level indicate digoxin toxicity, and low potassium increases the risk of arrhythmia, so digoxin is held, an ECG is checked and potassium is corrected. Intravenous calcium is avoided in digoxin toxicity because it may worsen arrhythmias.
  3. The correct answer is D (restart at roughly half the previous dose and measure a level after at least a week): The interaction with amiodarone calls for a digoxin dose reduction of roughly 50%. Digoxin has a long half-life, so a level should be taken at least a week after a change, and earlier sampling would not reflect steady state.
  4. The correct answer is B (Piperacillin-tazobactam plus vancomycin, which covers Pseudomonas and MRSA while cultures are pending): Late onset ventilator pneumonia with a history of MRSA and recent antibiotic exposure calls for broad coverage of resistant gram negatives including Pseudomonas and of MRSA. Piperacillin-tazobactam with vancomycin gives the best coverage of the options offered; given recent IV antibiotic exposure, guidelines (IDSA/ATS 2016) also favour adding a second antipseudomonal agent from another class until cultures return. Ceftriaxone lacks antipseudomonal activity, and the other pairings neither cover MRSA nor suit a critically ill ventilated patient.
  5. The correct answer is C (Respect his choice, assess his risk, and involve the prescriber before Ramadan): A culturally sensitive approach respects his religious practice while assessing hypoglycemia risk, since sulfonylureas are a concern during fasting. The pharmacist then involves the prescriber to adjust timing and doses. Forbidding fasting, unchanged dosing or stopping treatment each ignore either his values or his safety.
  6. The correct answer is B (Oxybutynin can precipitate urinary retention with prostate obstruction, so check residual volume and monitor closely): Antimuscarinics reduce detrusor contraction and can cause acute retention in men with bladder outlet obstruction. They may still be used with an alpha blocker if the post void residual is low and the patient is monitored. Oxybutynin does not alter PSA, shrink the prostate or meaningfully interact with tamsulosin.
  7. The correct answer is C (Explain it is not a valid prescription here and help her reach a Canadian prescriber): The Food and Drug Regulations define a practitioner as a person entitled under the laws of a Canadian province to treat patients, so a prescription from a US physician is not a valid prescription in Canada. A printed license number, a faxed confirmation or a transfer from a US pharmacy does not change the prescriber's status. The pharmacist should help her reach a Canadian prescriber.
  8. The correct answer is B (One 200 mg capsule at bedtime for 12 to 14 days of each cycle): Cyclic micronized progesterone is given at 200 mg at bedtime for 12 to 14 days each month, which causes adequate secretory change and shedding. Three days or a weekly dose is too little to protect the endometrium. Giving 400 mg every day for the whole cycle exceeds the dose needed and increases sedation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Her average exceeds the 130/80 mmHg home threshold, so prescriber assessment is warranted): The 2025 Hypertension Canada guideline defines hypertension as 130/80 mmHg or higher, and it uses the same threshold for home averages as for office and ambulatory readings. Her average is above that value, so she needs clinical assessment rather than reassurance. Ambulatory monitoring is an alternative method, not a prerequisite for diagnosis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Fidelity, which means keeping commitments and remaining faithful to obligations owed to the patient): Fidelity is the duty to keep promises and honour commitments to patients. Justice concerns fair distribution of resources and veracity concerns truthful disclosure, neither of which is the issue in a promised follow-up call. Autonomy is about the patient's decisions, not the pharmacist's own commitment.
  11. The correct answer is B (Adapting and renewing are pharmacist prescribing acts that a technician cannot perform on delegation): Adapting and renewing prescriptions depend on assessing the patient and exercising clinical judgment, which are part of the pharmacist's prescribing authority and cannot be delegated to a regulated technician. Verbal permission or a later countersignature does not transfer that authority. Renewal still requires an assessment that a technician is not authorized to make.
  12. The correct answer is D (90%, the proportion of culture positive patients whom the test correctly identified): Sensitivity is the proportion of culture positive patients detected by the test: 72 of 80, or 90%. The 85% figure is the specificity (102 of 120), the 80% figure is the positive predictive value (72 of 90), and 45% is the overall rate of positive tests (72 true plus 18 false positives, 90 of 200).
  13. The correct answer is D (Oseltamivir 30 mg once daily, adjusted for the moderately reduced renal function): Oseltamivir is renally eliminated, and when creatinine clearance falls below about 60 mL/min the prophylaxis dose is reduced to 30 mg once daily. Outbreak prophylaxis continues for at least 10 to 14 days and until public health declares the outbreak over, not for five days. Twice daily 75 mg is a treatment dose and is not suited to prophylaxis.
  14. The correct answer is B (Record the minimum, maximum and current temperatures at least twice daily using a calibrated thermometer): Minimum and maximum readings show whether the temperature left the 2 to 8 degree range between checks, which a current reading alone cannot show. Checks at least twice daily with a calibrated thermometer allow excursions to be found early. Freezing damages many vaccines as surely as heat does, so both limits must be tracked.
  15. The correct answer is A (A mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist such as spironolactone, with potassium and creatinine checked within one week): Mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists form the fourth pillar and lower mortality when added to an ACE inhibitor, beta-blocker and SGLT2 inhibitor. Potassium and creatinine need an early recheck because hyperkalemia is the main risk. Digoxin and loop diuretics relieve symptoms or admissions but have not been shown to lower mortality, and amlodipine has no role in reducing heart failure mortality.
  16. The correct answer is D (Contact the prescriber to authorize an early portion, and document the change before dispensing it): When a prescriber specifies the quantity and interval for each portion of a narcotic, the pharmacist dispenses accordingly. An earlier portion changes the prescriber's directions, so the pharmacist needs the prescriber's approval and a record of it. A patient signature, travel plans or the total authorized quantity do not allow the pharmacist to override the stated interval.
  17. The correct answer is B (Repeat the dose now, since vomiting occurred within three hours of the first dose): Vomiting within three hours of an emergency contraceptive dose means absorption may have been incomplete, so the full dose is repeated, and an antiemetic can be offered. Waiting for a second episode or for the next period delays treatment while efficacy falls with time. Absorption is not complete within thirty minutes in a way that makes a repeat unnecessary.
  18. The correct answer is A (About 15 g, so one 15 g tube is enough for the full course): Six handprints need three fingertip units, which is 1.5 g per application. Twice daily gives 3 g per day, and five days gives 15 g, so one 15 g tube suffices. The other amounts come from assuming wrong coverage per fingertip unit.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Hold warfarin, give vitamin K 2.5 mg orally, and recheck the INR in 24 hours): For an INR above 10 without bleeding, guidelines advise holding warfarin and giving low dose oral vitamin K with a repeat INR the next day. Intravenous vitamin K and prothrombin complex concentrate are reserved for major bleeding because they risk overcorrection and thrombosis. Waiting a week leaves a very high bleeding risk, and the antibiotic interaction should be addressed at follow up.
  2. The correct answer is B (It is hypothesis-generating only, because testing many subgroups makes a chance finding quite likely): With 20 post hoc subgroups, about one nominally significant result is expected by chance at the 5% level. Subgroup findings from a neutral trial should be treated as hypotheses to test in a new trial. A subgroup claim is more credible when prespecified, supported by a significant interaction test and biologically plausible.
  3. The correct answer is C (Advise assessment for sexually transmitted infections, because these symptoms do not fit candidiasis): Frothy yellow green discharge, dysuria and a new partner point to trichomoniasis or another sexually transmitted infection that needs testing, prescription therapy, partner treatment and screening. Antifungal products have no activity against protozoa and would delay proper care. Probiotics do not treat an established infection.
  4. The correct answer is A (Complete blood count, liver enzymes and creatinine at baseline and regularly, with alcohol use limited): Methotrexate can suppress the bone marrow, injure the liver and depend on renal clearance, so blood counts, liver enzymes and creatinine are checked at baseline and regularly. Alcohol adds to liver risk and should be limited. Lipid, thyroid and uric acid tests do not follow its main toxicities.
  5. The correct answer is B (park the gum against the cheek, and avoid coffee for fifteen minutes before and during use): Nicotine gum is absorbed through the oral mucosa using a chew and park method, with the peppery taste signalling release. Fast chewing causes swallowed nicotine, hiccups and jaw pain, and acidic drinks such as coffee reduce buccal absorption. Swallowed nicotine is largely metabolized by the liver, and replacing a piece every five minutes would exceed the labelled frequency and daily maximum and increase nicotine side effects.
  6. The correct answer is C (A sudden, severe headache that she describes as the worst she has ever had): A thunderclap headache described as the worst of her life can signal a subarachnoid hemorrhage and needs urgent assessment. The other descriptions fit migraine, tension type headache, and sinus congestion with a cold, which can be managed with self-care. Red flags are new or abrupt severity, not the presence of pain itself.
  7. The correct answer is B (Call 911, give naloxone, and repeat the dose in 2 to 3 minutes if needed): Overdose response means calling emergency services, giving naloxone, and repeating the dose every 2 to 3 minutes if there is no response while supporting breathing. Delaying the call or withholding naloxone risks brain injury from low oxygen. Withdrawal is uncomfortable but far less dangerous than continued respiratory depression.
  8. The correct answer is D (Message only patients who agreed to promotions and ask other patients for new consent first): Personal information may be used only for the purposes a patient agreed to, so a number given for refill reminders cannot be reused for promotions without consent. An opt-out line after the fact does not replace consent, and an age cutoff does not address the consent problem.
  9. The correct answer is B (Regular weight checks, plus fasting glucose and lipids at 12 weeks and then yearly): Olanzapine and other second-generation antipsychotics cause early weight gain and dysglycemia, so weight is tracked closely at early visits with glucose and lipids at 12 weeks, then yearly. Testing only at baseline or only after symptoms appear misses silent metabolic change. Liver tests and ECGs are not the core metabolic monitoring.
  10. The correct answer is A (This suggests white coat hypertension, so out-of-office readings guide the decision and monitoring continues): A raised clinic reading with normal pharmacy and home averages is the pattern of white coat hypertension, and out-of-office values are the better guide to her true pressure. She should still be monitored because this pattern can progress to sustained hypertension. Masked hypertension is the opposite pattern, and ignoring the office reading entirely would be inappropriate.
  11. The correct answer is B (Health Canada reviewed the submission and accepted the drug's safety, efficacy and quality): A Notice of Compliance is issued after Health Canada completes its review of a drug submission and finds the evidence of safety, efficacy and quality acceptable. The eight digit number is the Drug Identification Number, which is a separate identifier. Formulary listing is a provincial reimbursement decision, and the Special Access Program applies to drugs that are not authorized for sale.
  12. The correct answer is D (Benefit builds over several weeks of daily dosing, unlike stimulants that act within hours): Atomoxetine is a selective norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor with no immediate stimulant effect, so benefit builds over about 4 to 6 weeks of steady daily dosing. Expecting an effect within hours or one week leads to unwarranted discontinuation. Skipping weekend doses prevents steady levels.
  13. The correct answer is D (About 47 mg/kg/day, within the stated range, so the prescription can be filled as written): Each 5 mL contains 250 mg, so three doses give 750 mg per day. Dividing 750 mg by 16 kg gives about 47 mg/kg/day, inside the 40 to 50 mg/kg/day range. The other values come from mistakes such as doubling or halving the daily total, so no call to the prescriber is needed.
  14. The correct answer is B (Considerable variation exists between trial results beyond chance, so the pooled estimate needs cautious interpretation): I-squared estimates the proportion of variability across trials that is due to real differences rather than chance. A value near 78% signals substantial heterogeneity in populations, doses or methods. The pooled average may then hide meaningful differences, and the sources of variation should be explored.
  15. The correct answer is A (Change to a suitable liquid at the same dose, document it, and notify the prescriber): In most provinces a pharmacist may adapt a prescription by changing the dosage form, dose, regimen or duration when it is in the patient's interest, provided the change is documented and the prescriber is notified. Changing to a different drug is a different authority, and altering the dose without a clinical basis is not adaptation.
  16. The correct answer is C (Hold methimazole and arrange same-day assessment with a complete blood count and differential): Fever with sore throat in the first months of an antithyroid drug is agranulocytosis until proven otherwise, which needs the drug held and an urgent blood count. Waiting a week or lowering the dose delays recognition of a dangerous condition. Propylthiouracil can cause the same problem, so switching drugs does not remove the concern.
  17. The correct answer is D (Call the poison control centre now for advice and do not induce vomiting): Poison control can estimate risk from the child's weight and the possible number of tablets and direct whether emergency care is needed. Ipecac and induced vomiting are no longer recommended because of aspiration risk. Charcoal and milk should not replace expert advice.
  18. The correct answer is A (Advise against estrogen-containing methods, suggest progestin-only options, and arrange smoking and blood pressure follow-up): Smoking at age 35 or older and elevated blood pressure both raise the cardiovascular risk of estrogen-containing contraception, and the patch delivers estrogen continuously. The vaginal ring also contains estrogen. Progestin-only methods are safer, and smoking cessation and blood pressure follow-up address her underlying risk.
  19. The correct answer is A (Book a qualified sign language interpreter or video relay, and add written notes): A qualified interpreter or video relay service provides accurate and confidential communication in her own language, and written notes reinforce the key points. Lip reading is unreliable for medical vocabulary, and a staff member with a few signs cannot guarantee accuracy or confidentiality. Treating a handout as declined counselling places the barrier on the patient rather than removing it.
  20. The correct answer is C (It balances known and unknown prognostic factors between groups on average, so differences reflect treatment): Random allocation distributes both measured and unmeasured prognostic factors by chance, so on average the groups differ only in the treatment received. It does not make groups identical in any single trial, and it has no direct effect on follow-up completion or on blinding.
  21. The correct answer is A (October 10, which is 28 days after the date of first opening): September has 30 days, so 18 days after September 12 is September 30, and 10 more days reaches October 10. Counting weeks or a calendar month is not the same as 28 days. Opened eye drops are limited because the preservative can no longer protect against contamination after repeated use.
  22. The correct answer is C (Withhold the dose and contact the prescriber, since tolerance may have fallen after five missed days): After several missed doses, opioid tolerance can fall enough that the full methadone dose causes dangerous respiratory depression, even if he looks alert and has used other opioids intermittently. Methadone has a long half-life, so toxicity can develop hours later. The prescriber must reassess and reorder a safe restart dose, which the pharmacist should not set alone.
  23. The correct answer is A (contact the prescriber to clarify whether metformin was omitted on purpose and document the response): A drug that was held in hospital and not resumed, with no reason given, is an unintentional discrepancy until the prescriber says otherwise. Kidney function has recovered, so metformin may be appropriate, but the decision belongs to the prescriber. Clarifying and documenting prevents an unplanned loss of diabetes treatment or an unsafe restart.
  24. The correct answer is B (Twenty-five days, since eight actuations daily from 200 actuations gives that supply): Two puffs four times daily is eight actuations per day, and 200 divided by 8 gives 25 days. Counting four actuations a day gives 50 days, and ten per day gives 20, neither of which matches the order. The two week course is shorter than the life of the inhaler, so one inhaler covers the regular phase.
  25. The correct answer is A (100 mg daily, because clearance falls by half while the dosing interval is unchanged): Average steady state concentration equals dose rate divided by clearance, so halving clearance means the dose must also be halved to hold the concentration constant: 200 mg x 0.5 = 100 mg. A change in clearance does alter the average concentration, and the reduction needed is proportional, not larger or smaller.
  26. The correct answer is C (Swallow each capsule with a full glass of water and stay upright for 30 minutes): Doxycycline can lodge in the esophagus and cause ulceration, so patients should take it with plenty of water and remain upright for about 30 minutes. Dairy products bind doxycycline and lower absorption without protecting the esophagus. Lying flat increases the risk, and opening the capsule changes the product and does not remove it.
  27. The correct answer is D (Take the last dose Monday evening, omit Tuesday and Wednesday doses, and give no bridging): With normal renal function, a high bleeding risk procedure needs the direct oral anticoagulant omitted for two full days, so the final dose is Monday evening. Heparin bridging adds bleeding without lowering thromboembolism in atrial fibrillation patients on a direct oral anticoagulant. Full doses restart 48 to 72 hours after high risk surgery once hemostasis is secure.
  28. The correct answer is D (Take methotrexate on one chosen day weekly and folic acid reduces mouth sores and nausea): Methotrexate for rheumatoid arthritis is taken once weekly, and daily dosing errors have caused fatal toxicity. Folic acid lowers mouth sores, nausea and some liver enzyme elevation without reducing efficacy. It does not boost anti-inflammatory effect or absorption.
  29. The correct answer is A (Naltrexone blocks opioid analgesia, and bupropion with tramadol raises seizure risk, so avoid it): Naltrexone is an opioid antagonist, so it blocks tramadol's analgesia and can precipitate withdrawal in patients using opioids regularly. Bupropion and tramadol both lower the seizure threshold, and tramadol also interacts with sertraline. The combination is therefore unsuitable, and no dose adjustment makes it safe.
  30. The correct answer is A (Eight tablets, which complete the remaining four days of the sixty day total supply): At two tablets a day the full course is 120 tablets, and each fourteen day portion is 28 tablets. Four full portions use 112 tablets, leaving 8 tablets for the last four days. The other quantities miscount either the leftover days or the tablets per day.
  31. The correct answer is B (The former pharmacy must record the transfer and cancel the remaining refills at its location): When a non-narcotic prescription is transferred, the originating pharmacy documents the transfer and inactivates the prescription so that the same refills cannot be dispensed in two places. The receiving pharmacist records the original prescriber, dates and refills remaining. A new prescription is not required.
  32. The correct answer is A (Contact the prescriber to change to oxycodone immediate-release liquid, about 10 mg every 6 hours): Controlled-release tablets must not be crushed because this causes dose dumping of a potent opioid in an elderly patient. Converting to an immediate-release liquid at the same total daily dose of 40 mg, divided into 10 mg every 6 hours, keeps pain controlled. Stopping abruptly risks withdrawal and uncontrolled pain.
  33. The correct answer is C (Sitagliptin 50 mg once daily, since his eGFR requires a reduced dose of the drug): Sitagliptin is cleared by the kidneys and the dose is 50 mg daily when the eGFR is from 30 to below 45 mL/min/1.73 m2. The 25 mg dose is reserved for an eGFR below 30. Linagliptin does not need renal adjustment, but sitagliptin does, and his metformin dose is not a reason to avoid it.
  34. The correct answer is B (Choose a different antiseizure drug, because this allele predicts a high risk of Stevens-Johnson syndrome): HLA-B*15:02 is strongly associated with carbamazepine induced Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis, especially in people of Han Chinese and Southeast Asian ancestry. Carbamazepine should be avoided in carriers and another agent chosen. Slow titration or steroid cover does not remove the risk, and the allele is not linked to liver injury.
  35. The correct answer is B (Carbamazepine, started low and titrated, with monitoring of sodium and blood counts): Carbamazepine is the first-line drug for classic trigeminal neuralgia because it blocks sodium channels and reduces paroxysmal pain. It can cause hyponatremia and blood count changes, so these are monitored. Opioids and NSAIDs work poorly for this pain, and tricyclics are better suited to continuous neuropathic pain.
  36. The correct answer is A (Withhold the product, reprepare it correctly, and record the error as a near miss): Any label that does not match the prescription is an error that must not leave the pharmacy, so the product is withheld and prepared again with the verified strength and directions. Recording it as a near miss lets the pharmacy find the cause. Releasing with verbal correction or relying on a recent INR leaves the written directions wrong, and a call to the prescriber is unnecessary when the prescription itself is clear.
  37. The correct answer is C (Levonorgestrel may be less effective here, so ulipristal or a copper IUD is preferred): Levonorgestrel efficacy appears to fall in women with higher weight or BMI, so ulipristal or a copper intrauterine device is better for RT. Ulipristal is less affected by weight, and a copper device does not depend on weight at all. Treating the two oral products as interchangeable ignores this evidence.

  45. Here 1.42 exceeds 1.38, so a meaningfully worse outcome with the new drug cannot be excluded, even though the point estimate looks favourable. A parent's permission is not a legal prerequisite for a capable minor, and an individual store policy cannot override the law.

  84. Rifampin plus pyrazinamide for two months carries unacceptable liver toxicity for latent infection. Waiting for symptoms forfeits the chance to prevent progression, which is greatest in the early years after infection.
  85. The correct answer is A (Continuous potent use risks skin atrophy and tachyphylaxis, so treatment breaks help preserve response): Continuous use of a potent steroid thins the skin and leads to tachyphylaxis, a falling response to the drug. Planned breaks or a step down to a lower potency product keep benefit while limiting harm. The ointment keeps its activity, and the skin does not need rest to rebuild its own corticosteroids.
  86. The correct answer is D (NSAID users had 2.4 times the bleeding risk, and the interval excludes 1.0): A relative risk of 2.4 means the bleeding risk was about 2.4 times higher with the drug, and an interval that excludes 1.0 indicates a statistically significant association. It is a ratio of risks, not a percentage point difference or an event rate. The interval reflects uncertainty in the ratio, not a range of event percentages.
  87.   88. The correct answer is B (Multiply by body weight and by 60, then divide by 1000 to get mg/h): The ordered amount is per kilogram per minute, so multiplying by weight gives mcg per minute, multiplying by 60 gives mcg per hour, and dividing by 1000 reaches mg per hour. Dividing by 60 instead would give a rate per second, which no pump uses, and dividing by weight would shrink the dose instead of scaling it up to the patient.

  94. Many centres use adjusted weight, ideal weight plus 0.4 times the excess, as a compromise when actual weight is well above ideal. Body surface area is not part of the Cockcroft-Gault equation.

The correct answer is A (Recount, review records to find the cause and report an unexplained loss to Health Canada): A narcotic discrepancy must be investigated through recounting and record review. If the loss stays unexplained, the pharmacist has a duty to report it to Health Canada, the federal regulator. Quietly adjusting the record or waiting for a later audit hides a possible diversion, and a report that skips Health Canada does not meet the regulation.
  120. The correct answer is D (Stop allopurinol and send her urgently to the emergency department for suspected severe reaction): Fever, mucosal erosions, conjunctivitis and blistering after starting allopurinol suggest Stevens-Johnson syndrome or toxic epidermal necrolysis, which is life threatening. The culprit must be stopped immediately and urgent hospital care arranged. Dose reduction or symptomatic treatment at home delays care and risks progression.
  121. The correct answer is B (Billing fraud and professional misconduct, which should be reported to the college and payer): Billing a payer for quantities that were not dispensed is fraud and a serious breach of professional standards, whatever the size of the amount. Pharmacists have an individual duty to act, and an employee cannot set that duty aside because the owner gave the instruction. Treating it as stock management or a clerical slip understates a deliberate practice.
  122. The correct answer is C (A compounding record with lot numbers, weights, preparer and checker names, and the assigned beyond-use date): The master formulation record is the fixed recipe, while the compounding record documents what was actually done for one batch: the ingredients and lots used, quantities weighed, who prepared and checked it, and the beyond-use date. A copy of the recipe, safety data sheets and the prescription label do not show batch-specific details that allow the batch to be traced.
  123. The correct answer is A (Dispense the usual daily dose only and record the missed dose without making it up): A single missed day does not require a makeup dose. Doubling the dose, or giving two doses close together, risks sedation and respiratory depression because tolerance is set by the daily dose. A single missed dose normally does not require dosing to stop, and the pharmacist records it.
  124. The correct answer is D (Use an oral syringe marked in millilitres because it measures small volumes accurately): Household spoons vary widely in size and are not accurate for liquid medication, and a standard teaspoon is about 5 mL, not 4 mL. An oral syringe in millilitres gives the most accurate small measurement for a young child. Dosing cups are less precise at low volumes.
Case CHB15

PL is a 72 year old woman with newly diagnosed nonvalvular atrial fibrillation found at a routine visit. She has hypertension and type 2 diabetes and takes perindopril 4 mg daily and metformin 500 mg twice daily. Weight is 68 kg, serum creatinine 88 umol/L, hemoglobin 132 g/L and liver enzymes are normal. Resting heart rate is 112 beats/min, blood pressure 138/82 mmHg and ejection fraction 55%. She has no prior stroke or bleeding and only mild palpitations.

  1. The correct answer is B (Start a direct oral anticoagulant, since age 65 or older alone meets the threshold): CHADS-65 recommends an oral anticoagulant for nonvalvular atrial fibrillation in anyone aged 65 or older, or with heart failure, hypertension, diabetes or prior stroke or TIA. She qualifies on age, hypertension and diabetes. Direct oral anticoagulants are preferred over warfarin, and aspirin is not recommended for stroke prevention.
  2. The correct answer is A (5 mg twice daily, because she meets none of the criteria for dose reduction): Apixaban is reduced to 2.5 mg twice daily only when two of three criteria are met: age 80 or older, weight 60 kg or less, and creatinine 133 umol/L or higher. She meets none, with age 72, weight 68 kg and creatinine 88 umol/L. Her estimated creatinine clearance is about 55 mL/min, which does not change the dosing frequency. The loading regimen belongs to venous thromboembolism treatment.
  3. The correct answer is D (Take it as soon as she remembers the same day, then continue the usual schedule): Apixaban is taken twice daily, so a missed dose is taken as soon as it is remembered on the same day and the usual schedule resumed. Doubling doses increases bleeding risk without adding protection. Apixaban is not monitored with an INR, so waiting for a result has no meaning.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decline, because final checks are limited to pharmacists or, where permitted, regulated technicians): The final check is a regulated function that only a pharmacist, or a regulated technician where the province allows it, may perform. Experience and accuracy do not change who is authorized, and unregulated staff are not accountable to a college. A sample check or later signature does not make an unauthorized check acceptable.
  5. The correct answer is D (Prompt medical assessment, since weight loss and dysphagia are alarm features needing investigation): Unintentional weight loss and difficulty swallowing are alarm features that raise concern for esophageal or gastric malignancy or stricture, so he needs prompt medical evaluation, often with endoscopy. Starting empiric acid suppression could partly mask symptoms and delay the diagnosis. Antacid combinations and higher PPI doses do not address that concern.
  6. The correct answer is D (the nozzle toward the outer wall of each nostril, away from the septum, sniffing gently): Spraying onto the septum dries and irritates the mucosa and is the usual cause of nosebleeds with these sprays. Aiming outward toward the side wall deposits drug on the target turbinate tissue, and a gentle sniff keeps the spray in the nose rather than the throat. Forceful sniffing or tilting the head back sends more drug down the throat.
  7. The correct answer is A (Equivalence was not shown because the upper limit of 1.18 falls outside the 1.15 margin): Equivalence requires the whole confidence interval to sit inside both margins, not just the point estimate. The upper limit of 1.18 exceeds 1.15, so a clinically important difference remains possible. Failing to find a difference is not the same as demonstrating equivalence, and 90% intervals are standard for equivalence testing.
  8. The correct answer is B (Escalate to triple therapy with an inhaled corticosteroid added, as her eosinophils suggest benefit): Repeated exacerbations on dual bronchodilators with blood eosinophils of 300 cells/uL or more identify patients who benefit from adding an inhaled corticosteroid. Triple therapy lowers exacerbations in this group. Her pneumonia risk is not elevated by history, bronchodilators do not cause pneumonia, and theophylline or a macrolide is not the first step.
  9. The correct answer is C (Doses of 90 MME or more daily carry higher risks, so gradual tapering should be considered): The 2017 Canadian opioid guideline advises staying below 50 MME per day when possible and avoiding 90 MME or more, because overdose risk rises with dose without clear added benefit. A slow taper, usually about 10% of the dose every 2 to 4 weeks, is suggested instead of abrupt discontinuation, which risks withdrawal and uncontrolled pain.
  10. The correct answer is B (Morphine extended-release 30 mg every 12 hours, with immediate-release kept for breakthrough pain): Ten milligrams every 4 hours is 60 mg per day, which divides into 30 mg every 12 hours for an extended-release product. A breakthrough immediate-release dose stays available. Doubling the total or giving the whole daily amount once a day would not match the established requirement.
  11. The correct answer is D (A biological safety cabinet vented outdoors, in a room at negative pressure to surrounding areas): Hazardous drugs must be prepared in a containment primary engineering control, typically a biological safety cabinet vented to the outdoors, inside a room kept under negative pressure so contaminants flow into the room rather than into corridors. A horizontal laminar flow hood blows air toward the operator and protects only the product. Respirators and face shields are secondary protection.
  12. The correct answer is A (Oral rehydration solution with loperamide for short term symptom relief, and fluids as tolerated): Mild traveller's diarrhea without fever or blood is managed with fluids and electrolytes, and loperamide can reduce stool frequency. Antibiotics are reserved for moderate or severe illness and carry resistance and adverse effect risks. Plain water alone does not replace lost electrolytes.
  13. The correct answer is C (Weigh 12 g of the 2.5% cream and add 48 g of plain cream base): The final product needs 0.5% of 60 g, which is 0.3 g of hydrocortisone. Each gram of 2.5% cream holds 0.025 g, so 0.3 divided by 0.025 gives 12 g of stock cream. Plain base makes up the remaining 48 g, and larger amounts of stock cream would give a stronger product than ordered.
  14. The correct answer is A (Start nystatin suspension, and teach her to rinse and spit after every inhalation): Inhaled corticosteroids deposit steroid in the mouth and throat, which favours Candida overgrowth. Nystatin suspension treats mild oropharyngeal thrush, and rinsing and spitting after each dose lowers the risk of recurrence. Stopping the inhaler is not required, and terbinafine and chlorhexidine are not standard thrush treatments.
  15. The correct answer is A (Ask what he likes and dislikes about taking metformin, then reflect his answers back): Motivational interviewing explores the patient's own views, using open questions and reflection to draw out his reasons for change. Scare tactics and directive statements create resistance, and waiting for symptoms ignores the silent damage of high glucose.
  16. The correct answer is D (Provide large-print labels and counselling sheets, and offer an audio or tactile identification option): Pharmacies are expected to remove barriers to safe medication use, and large-print labels with an audio or tactile identifier let a patient with low vision manage her own drugs. Relying on a neighbour or a magnifier shifts the burden to her and risks privacy and error. A home care referral is a disproportionate response to a problem the pharmacy can solve directly.
  17. The correct answer is B (Half-life increases because clearance falls while the volume of distribution stays the same): Half-life is proportional to volume of distribution divided by clearance (0.693 x Vd / CL). When clearance falls and Vd is unchanged, half-life rises, so accumulation is slower to reach steady state and the dose usually needs reducing. Half-life is independent of the dose given.
  18. The correct answer is A (confirm full name with date of birth or address directly with the patient): Two identifiers confirmed with the patient prevent a prescription from being filed in the wrong profile, which would carry the wrong allergies and history into screening. Guessing from dispensing dates or prescribers is unreliable, and leaving the check to a later step lets the error pass through the system.
  19. The correct answer is C (About 13.1 mL per hour from the 1600 mcg/mL bag): The dose is 5 x 70 = 350 mcg per minute, or 21 000 mcg per hour. Dividing by 1600 mcg per mL gives 13.1 mL per hour. The 0.22 figure is a rate per minute, so running it on the pump would infuse the drug 60 times too slowly.
  20. The correct answer is C (Probable adverse drug reaction, with a total Naranjo score of 7): The items add to 1 + 2 + 1 + 0 + 2 + 1 = 7. Scores of 5 to 8 are probable, while 9 or more is needed for definite and usually requires a positive rechallenge. Scores of 1 to 4 are possible and 0 or less is doubtful.
  21. The correct answer is D ($48.00, the difference between brand and plan price for each of the 60 tablets): The plan pays up to the lowest-cost price of $0.40, so VF pays the difference of $1.20 - $0.40 = $0.80 per tablet. Over 60 tablets, 0.80 x 60 = $48.00. The other figures confuse the plan's share with the patient's share.
  22. The correct answer is D (2 tablets short of the expected balance of 158 tablets): Expected balance is 240 plus 100 received minus dispensed units. Six prescriptions of 28 is 168 and the extra 14 gives 182, so 340 minus 182 equals 158. The counted 156 is therefore 2 tablets short, and even a small unexplained shortage needs investigation.
  23. The correct answer is C (Acetaminophen, because NSAIDs reduce renal lithium clearance and can raise levels to toxic range): Lithium is cleared by the kidneys, and NSAIDs reduce renal prostaglandin-dependent blood flow, increasing lithium levels by 15 to 40 percent or more. Even short courses can cause toxicity. Acetaminophen does not affect lithium handling and is the preferred analgesic.
  24. The correct answer is D (Order a quantitative HCV RNA test to distinguish active infection from past resolved infection): A reactive antibody shows exposure but cannot separate current infection from cleared infection, since some infected people clear the virus spontaneously. HCV RNA testing confirms active infection and guides the next steps. Direct acting antivirals are not used in pregnancy, and genotyping or biopsy come after active infection is established.
  25. The correct answer is C (Rest, fluids and symptom relief come first, and fill it only if symptoms worsen or persist): A delayed prescription lets patients treat the likely viral illness supportively and use the antibiotic only if they worsen or fail to improve. Filling it immediately defeats the stewardship purpose. Saving it for later illnesses promotes self-treatment of viral infections and misuse.
  26. The correct answer is A (Stop her from dispensing, recheck the affected prescriptions, and report the concern as required): Patient safety comes first, so she must stop dispensing now and the prescriptions she handled must be rechecked before they reach patients. Pharmacists in most provinces also have a duty to report a colleague whose impairment puts patients at risk. Covering for her, waiting for an inspector, or delaying the review leaves mislabeled drugs in circulation.
  27. The correct answer is A (Sudden withdrawal can cause rebound insomnia, anxiety and, less often, seizures, so doses are reduced slowly): After long term use the brain adapts to the benzodiazepine, so abrupt removal can cause rebound insomnia, anxiety, tremor and occasionally seizures. A slow dose reduction lets the nervous system readjust. Lorazepam is not stored in fat, and sudden withdrawal does not create lasting tolerance to other sedatives.
  28. The correct answer is C (Explain that trouble swallowing and weight loss need prompt medical assessment before treatment): Dysphagia and unintentional weight loss are alarm features that can signal esophageal or gastric cancer, so he needs timely investigation, often endoscopy. Selling a proton pump inhibitor first can relieve symptoms and delay diagnosis. Lifestyle measures and a short trial of acid reducers are only reasonable once alarm features are excluded.
  29. The correct answer is D (125 mg twice daily, starting the day before ascent and continuing until descent begins): The usual prophylactic dose of acetazolamide is 125 mg twice daily, started the day before ascent and continued until descent or acclimatization is secure. Higher doses add side effects without better prevention. Starting after arrival or using weekly dosing does not match its pharmacokinetics.
  30. The correct answer is C (Suspect carbamazepine toxicity from clarithromycin and urgently contact the prescriber about changing therapy): Clarithromycin strongly inhibits CYP3A4, which metabolizes carbamazepine, so levels can rise within days and cause dizziness, diplopia, and ataxia. The prescriber should check a level and change the antibiotic to one without this interaction, such as azithromycin. Stopping carbamazepine abruptly risks seizures, and these symptoms are not a benign antibiotic effect.
  31. The correct answer is D (Patient, drug and strength, quantity, directions, prescriber, date, and who prepared and checked it): A usable record identifies the patient, the exact drug and strength, the quantity and directions, the authorizing prescriber, the date and the individuals who prepared and checked it, so errors can be traced. Records that omit the prescriber or the people involved cannot support an investigation. Payment details are secondary to these clinical and accountability elements.
  32. The correct answer is B (A puncture-resistant sharps container with a tight lid, returned to a collection site when full): Sharps belong in a rigid, puncture-resistant container that closes securely, and a full container should go to a pharmacy or other designated collection program. Household containers can split or leak, recapping raises needle-stick risk, and sharps in garbage or recycling injure sanitation workers.
  33. The correct answer is D (refer KL promptly to a physician or urgent care for assessment and document the referral): Fever, pleuritic chest pain and shortness of breath suggest possible pneumonia or another serious condition that needs a physical examination and possibly imaging. Symptomatic cough products would delay diagnosis. Referral plus documentation keeps the care team informed and creates a record of the advice given.
  34. The correct answer is C (Avoid haloperidol, treat the infection, and if an antipsychotic is needed use low dose quetiapine): Haloperidol is a strong D2 antagonist and can severely worsen parkinsonism. Olanzapine and chlorpromazine also block dopamine enough to worsen motor function. Treating the infection and reviewing contributing drugs should come ahead of any antipsychotic, and low dose quetiapine is the usual antipsychotic when one is truly required.
  35. The correct answer is B (An NK1 receptor antagonist such as aprepitant, since the regimen is highly emetogenic): Guidelines for highly emetogenic regimens such as cisplatin call for a 5-HT3 antagonist, dexamethasone and an NK1 antagonist, often with olanzapine. Lorazepam is an adjunct for anticipatory symptoms, not a replacement for core agents. Metoclopramide and prochlorperazine are weaker alternatives for prevention in this setting.
  36. The correct answer is D (The antibiotic can intensify gliclazide's effect and raise hypoglycemia risk when she skips meals): Sulfamethoxazole displaces sulfonylureas from protein binding and can inhibit their metabolism, which prolongs the glucose-lowering effect. Her tight fasting glucose and skipped meals make severe hypoglycemia likely. Absorption interference and cross-allergy are not meaningful with these two drugs, and the interaction raises rather than lowers sulfonylurea effect.
  37. The correct answer is A (A case-control study, which compares prior exposure between people with and without the outcome): Choosing participants by outcome status and then looking back at exposure defines a case-control design. It suits rare events because only a modest number of controls is needed, and it yields an odds ratio. A cohort design would start from exposure and need very large numbers to capture a rare event.
  38. The correct answer is B (Avoid treating him unless it is an emergency, and refer him to the nearby clinic): Professional standards advise against treating family members except in emergencies or when no one else is available, because objectivity and documentation suffer. A walk-in clinic is nearby, so referral is the better course. Having a colleague dispense or limiting prescribing to culture-confirmed cases does not fix the underlying objectivity concern.
  39. The correct answer is B (Do not crush it, since this releases the whole dose at once, and ask about alternatives): Crushing an extended-release product destroys the release mechanism and delivers the full daily dose within a short time, which can cause bradycardia or hypotension. The pharmacist should ask the prescriber about an immediate-release product in divided doses. Splitting the crushed dose or soaking the tablet does not restore controlled release.
  40. The correct answer is C (Cooperate and provide access promptly while arranging coverage so patient care continues safely): Inspectors have statutory authority to enter and review records during operating hours, and pharmacists are expected to cooperate. The manager can arrange staff coverage so care continues, but access should not be delayed or conditioned on a pharmacy form or legal review. Inspectors are themselves bound by confidentiality duties.
  41. The correct answer is B (Take no more today, drink oral rehydration fluid and seek care if it continues): She has already taken 8 mg (4 mg plus 2 mg plus 2 mg), which is the labelled self-care maximum for one day, so no further loperamide should be taken today. Oral rehydration fluid replaces losses while the illness runs its course. She should see a clinician if the diarrhea continues beyond 48 hours or if fever, blood or severe pain develops. A stimulant laxative would worsen diarrhea.
  42. The correct answer is B (Triptans are contraindicated after myocardial infarction, so an NSAID with an antiemetic is preferred): Triptans constrict blood vessels through serotonin receptor activity and are contraindicated in ischemic heart disease, including prior myocardial infarction. Beta-blockers do not reverse this effect, and supervised dosing does not remove the contraindication. Other triptans share the same risk, so an NSAID with an antiemetic is the sensible alternative for her.
  43. The correct answer is D (Do not dispense and call the prescriber, since PDE5 inhibitors with nitrates risk severe hypotension): PDE5 inhibitors potentiate nitrate induced vasodilation and can cause profound hypotension, and infrequent use does not remove the risk because an attack may occur at any time. Tadalafil needs about 48 hours clear of nitrates and sildenafil about 24 hours, so a four hour gap is inadequate. The prescriber should be contacted about an alternative.
  44. The correct answer is C (Reduce to 20 mg daily or on-demand use, then monitor for return of symptoms): Guidance for uncomplicated reflux with sustained control supports stepping down to a lower dose or on-demand use, with a plan for symptom recurrence. Stopping abruptly can cause rebound acid hypersecretion that lasts weeks, so a reduction is gentler. There is no evidence that rotating proton pump inhibitors prevents tolerance, and lifelong full dose therapy is not required without a compelling indication.
  45. The correct answer is C (Serotonin syndrome, supported by rapid onset after linezolid with clonus and hyperreflexia): Linezolid is a reversible MAO inhibitor, so adding it to an SSRI can precipitate serotonin syndrome within hours. Inducible clonus and hyperreflexia with onset over hours favour serotonin toxicity, whereas neuroleptic malignant syndrome develops over days with rigidity and slow reflexes. Quetiapine is a weak contributor, and sepsis would not cause clonus.
  46. The correct answer is D (U can be read as zero, so 10 U may become 100; write 'units'): ISMP Canada lists U as a dangerous abbreviation because a handwritten U can be read as a zero, turning 10 units into 100 units. The accepted fix is to write the word units in full. IU is also on the do-not-use list, and insulin is among the highest risk drugs, so it receives no exemption.
  47. The correct answer is B (About 12 hours after the last dose, in the morning before the next dose): Lithium targets are defined for a standardized 12 hour post-dose trough, so sampling must match that timing or the result cannot be compared with the range. A level at 0900 after a 2100 dose fits this. Peak or random levels are higher and unreliable for adjusting doses.
  48. The correct answer is C (Test non-invasively for H. pylori and treat only if the result is positive): For adults under 60 with uninvestigated dyspepsia and no alarm features, a test and treat strategy for H. pylori is recommended. Treating without confirming infection exposes the patient to antibiotics needlessly. Endoscopy is reserved for alarm features, and testing while on a PPI risks a false negative result.
  49. The correct answer is A (Suggest stopping glyburide and starting linagliptin 5 mg daily, citing hypoglycemia and renal function): An effective recommendation names a specific drug, dose and the reasons, so the prescriber can act on it immediately. Vague requests and rhetorical questions leave the work to the prescriber, and unilaterally stopping a prescribed drug oversteps the pharmacist's role.
  50. The correct answer is B (Withdraw through a 5 micron filter needle, then replace it with a fresh needle before use): Breaking a glass ampule releases fragments, so the drug should be drawn up through a 5 micron filter needle that traps particles. The filter needle is then replaced with a regular needle, since injecting through it could dislodge captured particles or interfere with dose delivery. Alcohol on the neck does not remove glass shards.
  51.   52. The correct answer is B (Give thiamine by injection before or with the glucose, and start a symptom triggered benzodiazepine): Fever, hallucinations, tachycardia and disorientation after stopping heavy drinking indicate severe alcohol withdrawal with delirium (delirium tremens usually begins 48 to 96 hours after the last drink, but can appear earlier in severe cases), which is treated with benzodiazepines titrated to symptoms. In a malnourished patient thiamine must be given before or with glucose to avoid precipitating Wernicke encephalopathy. Antipsychotics alone lower the seizure threshold, and relapse prevention drugs do not treat withdrawal.
  53.   60. The correct answer is D (10 days, which is about five half-lives of the drug): Steady state is reached after about five half-lives, so 5 x 48 hours = 240 hours, or 10 days. The rule of five days applies only to drugs with a half-life of about one day. A level at one half-life would reach only half of the steady state concentration.
  61.   62.   64. The correct answer is C (Infant exposure is low, because a relative infant dose below 10 percent is acceptable): The relative infant dose compares the infant's weight adjusted dose through milk with the mother's dose, and under 10 percent is generally considered acceptable for most drugs. At 2 percent the infant's exposure is small and breastfeeding can usually continue. Any detectable milk level does not by itself justify stopping, and the cutoff is far below fifty percent.

  67. The vehicle is then added in portions so the paste thins evenly, and the final volume is adjusted last. Adding all the liquid at once traps dry powder inside lumps that never disperse.

  70. The correct answer is A (Absolute risk reduction of 3%, with a number needed to treat of about 33): The absolute risk reduction is 12% minus 9%, which is 3 percentage points. The number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.03, or about 33 patients over two years. The 25% figure is the relative risk reduction, and the reciprocal of that value does not give the number needed to treat.
  71. Citalopram is not reliably sedating, and adding a hypnotic treats the symptom rather than the cause. His coffee intake is already restricted to the morning.
  72.   73.
