Both are given at different injection sites within 12 hours of birth to prevent perinatal transmission, and the vaccine series then continues on schedule. Delay lowers the protection considerably.
  74. The correct answer is B (Cross-sectional survey; it gives prevalence but cannot show whether exposure preceded any outcome): A single snapshot of current use answers a prevalence question quickly and cheaply. The cost is that exposure and any outcome are measured at the same time, so temporality cannot be judged. Cohort and randomized designs answer causal or incidence questions that were not asked here.
  75. The correct answer is C (Recommend an intravaginal azole such as clotrimazole for seven days and advise follow-up): Oral fluconazole is avoided in pregnancy because it has been linked to an increased risk of miscarriage and, at higher doses, congenital malformations. Seven days of an intravaginal azole such as clotrimazole is the recommended treatment, and symptoms that persist should prompt a return visit. Terbinafine is not a treatment for vaginal candidiasis, and oral nystatin does not reach the vaginal tissue.
  76. The correct answer is A (Ask the prescriber for a pMDI with a valved spacer or a soft mist inhaler): Dry powder devices depend on adequate inspiratory flow to break up the powder, and a weak breath delivers little drug to the airways. A pMDI with a valved holding chamber or a soft mist inhaler needs much less inspiratory effort. Coaching cannot raise a flow limited by weakness, and higher resistance devices need even stronger effort.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Disclose the objection and make sure KT receives timely access through referral or another pharmacist): Most provincial colleges allow a conscientious objection but require the pharmacist to inform the patient and make sure she can obtain the service in a timely way. Because KT's access is time-sensitive and the distance is large, a referral or arranged supply must work in practice. Misleading her about safety or simply sending her away does not meet that duty.
  2. The correct answer is B (Double to 6.25 mg twice daily, then keep doubling every two weeks toward target): Beta-blockers in heart failure are started low and doubled about every two weeks if the patient is stable and euvolemic, aiming for the trial target dose. Daily increases are too rapid and risk decompensation. Waiting months delays benefit, and echocardiography is not the trigger for titration.
  3. The correct answer is A (Reduce to 0.25 mg twice daily and monitor behaviours weekly before any further reduction): Behavioural symptoms that have been stable for several months are the usual point to attempt a slow antipsychotic dose reduction. A step of roughly half the dose with close monitoring allows relapse to be detected early. Stopping at once removes that safety net, and increasing the dose first exposes her to more adverse effects.
  4. The correct answer is B (Ulipristal 30 mg as one dose, since it remains effective up to 120 hours): Ulipristal acetate keeps its effectiveness through 120 hours, so AM at 90 hours still has an oral product available. Levonorgestrel is licensed for 72 hours and loses efficacy as time passes, and the two dose regimen was replaced by a single 1.5 mg dose. A copper device is the most effective method, but it is not the only one available after three days.
  5. The correct answer is D (1:00 am, giving a 5.5 hour sleep window that matches his average sleep): Sleep restriction sets the time in bed to the patient's average sleep time, here 5.5 hours and not below about 5 hours, so counting back from the fixed 6:30 am wake time gives 1:00 am. Keeping nine or seven hours does not compress sleep. Four hours is below the safe minimum window.
  6. The correct answer is C (About 31 mL/min, so many renally cleared drugs need a dose reduction or longer interval): For a woman, CrCl = (140 - 86) x 58 x 1.04 / 105 = 3257 / 105, which is about 31 mL/min. At this level many renally cleared antibiotics need a lower dose or a longer interval. Using the male factor of 1.23 would give about 37 mL/min and overestimate her clearance.
  7. The correct answer is D (Deliver the drugs to the prescriber and arrange return of unused drugs to the pharmacy): Guidance from pharmacy regulators and national pharmacy organizations advises providing the drugs directly to the practitioner who will carry out the procedure, since that person is responsible for administration. Leaving injectable drugs with the patient, family or a courier weakens control of the drugs. Unused drugs should come back to the pharmacy for proper disposal.
  8. The correct answer is A (1 mL, which provides 40 mg for each dose in this infant): A dose of 5 mg/kg for 8 kg is 40 mg. At 40 mg/mL this equals 1 mL. The other volumes come from underdosing by weight, doubling the dose, or mixing up the concentration with the 100 mg/5 mL suspension.
  9. The correct answer is A (14 mg per 24 hours for six weeks, then 7 mg for two weeks): Smokers of 10 or fewer cigarettes per day start on the middle strength of 14 mg for six weeks, followed by 7 mg for two weeks. The 21 mg strength is intended for those smoking more than 10 per day, and a 7 mg patch alone is too low to control withdrawal. The usual course is about eight weeks with a stepdown.
  10. The correct answer is D (400 mg every 8 hours, based on a dosing rate of 50 mg per hour): At steady state the dosing rate equals clearance times average concentration, so 5 L/h x 10 mg/L = 50 mg/h. Over an 8 hour interval that is 50 x 8 = 400 mg, with F equal to 1. The other doses come from dosing rates that do not match the stated clearance and target.
  11. The correct answer is B (About 77 percent, from 46 tablets taken out of 60 expected): Tablets taken are 60 minus 14, or 46. Expected doses are 2 per day over 30 days, or 60. The ratio 46 divided by 60 is about 77 percent, which falls below the 80 percent threshold. The 14 remaining tablets are the missed doses, not the adherence rate.
  12. The correct answer is D (The sensor reads interstitial fluid, which lags blood glucose, so he should treat the low): Sensors measure glucose in interstitial fluid, which trails blood glucose by several minutes, and the lag is largest when glucose changes quickly such as during exercise. When symptoms match the fingerstick, treat the low. Waiting risks further decline, and discounting a symptomatic meter reading is unsafe.
  13. The correct answer is C (Suggest switching to a progestin-only method and reassess supply before adding a galactagogue): Estrogen containing contraceptives can reduce milk volume, especially early in lactation, so removing the likely cause is the first step. Progestin-only methods do not lower supply. Domperidone is not approved for lactation in Canada and carries a cardiac rhythm warning, so it should not be added before the cause is addressed.
  14. The correct answer is D (Azithromycin 1 g as a single dose, with a test of cure about 4 weeks later): Doxycycline is avoided throughout pregnancy because of effects on fetal teeth and bones, so azithromycin is used. Pregnant patients should have a test of cure about 4 weeks after treatment because untreated chlamydia raises risks for the pregnancy and newborn. Skipping the test of cure does not meet that standard.
  15. The correct answer is A (An ACE inhibitor such as ramipril, with potassium and creatinine checked after starting): In heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, an ACE inhibitor (or ARB or ARNI) reduces mortality and also lowers pressure, so it is the preferred addition. Diltiazem worsens systolic function, and doxazosin has been linked to more heart failure events. Amlodipine is neutral for outcomes but does not improve survival.
  16. The correct answer is C (Inadequate blinding, which can inflate a subjective benefit through expectation effects on patient reporting): A subjective outcome such as pain is highly sensitive to what patients expect. When the taste exposes the assignment, patients on the active drug may rate pain lower because they believe they are treated. Concealment and intention to treat were adequate, so they do not explain the result.
  17. The correct answer is B (Continue buprenorphine, add acetaminophen and an NSAID, and ask the dentist to avoid oxycodone): Stopping buprenorphine risks relapse and makes later re-induction difficult. Its high receptor affinity limits the effect of added full agonists, so oxycodone is poorly effective and tempts dangerous dose escalation. Scheduled acetaminophen and an NSAID, with dentist support and possibly split buprenorphine dosing, treat the pain while keeping recovery stable.
  18. The correct answer is D (Explain the fetal risk, and ask the prescriber to review alternatives, with contraception meanwhile): Valproate carries a high risk of major congenital malformations and neurodevelopmental problems, so it should be avoided in people who can become pregnant unless no alternative exists. Folic acid does not remove the risk. Stopping abruptly risks relapse, and intermittent use is ineffective for bipolar disorder.
  19. The correct answer is A (Detection bias, because closer surveillance of exposed patients uncovers cases that would otherwise go unrecognized): When one group is examined more intensively, more outcomes are found in that group even if true risk is the same. Here the testing threshold differed by exposure, so more clots were detected among users. Recall and publication issues relate to reporting, not to how hard clinicians looked.
  20. The correct answer is C (Bismuth quadruple therapy with a PPI, bismuth, tetracycline and metronidazole for 14 days): After failure of a clarithromycin regimen, clarithromycin should not be reused because resistance is likely. Both the levofloxacin and rifabutin regimens depend on amoxicillin, which is contraindicated with anaphylaxis, and lengthening a failed regimen does not overcome resistance. Bismuth quadruple therapy contains no penicillin or macrolide and can work despite earlier metronidazole exposure.
  21. The correct answer is B (Arrange a dilated eye examination now, since retinopathy may already be present at diagnosis): In type 2 diabetes the onset is often unrecognized, so retinopathy can be present at diagnosis and screening starts right away, then every one to two years. The five year delay applies to type 1 diabetes after puberty. Early retinopathy is usually silent, and visual acuity testing alone does not detect it.
  22. The correct answer is B (About 4,975 mg daily, which exceeds the 4 g maximum, so he must reduce intake): Extra strength tablets give 2 x 500 x 4 = 4,000 mg, and the cold product adds 325 x 3 = 975 mg, for 4,975 mg. All sources of acetaminophen count toward the 4 g daily maximum, and no 6 g limit exists. He should lower the dose and stop the duplicate product.
  23. The correct answer is C (Take 3 tablets of 20 mg once daily; dispense 90 tablets for the course): The 60 mg dose from 20 mg tablets is 3 tablets, and 3 tablets x 30 days is 90 tablets. Two tablets would give only 40 mg, 30 tablets would cover just 10 days, and six tablets would double the daily dose.
  24. The correct answer is B (Confirm the pages were destroyed, not copied, then document the incident and assess the breach): A misdirected fax is a privacy breach even when the recipient is cooperative. The pharmacy must contain it, confirm what happened to the information, document it, and assess whether notifying the patient or the privacy commissioner is required.
  25. The correct answer is A (Decline to fill it as written, since managing diabetes falls outside a dentist's prescribing scope): A practitioner may prescribe only within the scope of the profession, and dentists treat conditions of the teeth and mouth. A diabetes drug is outside that scope, so the order is not a valid prescription. Telephone confirmation or patient consent cannot extend a dentist's scope, so the pharmacist should help the patient reach a physician or use provincial pharmacist authority.
  26. The correct answer is B (2.4 million units intramuscularly once weekly for 3 consecutive weeks, given in the gluteal muscle): Late latent or unknown duration syphilis needs three weekly doses of benzathine penicillin G, 2.4 million units each, because the organism is slowly dividing and needs prolonged exposure. A single dose is only enough for early syphilis. Oral penicillin V does not achieve adequate tissue levels and is not an equivalent alternative.
  27. The correct answer is A (Most of the crude association reflected confounding by smoking, leaving no clear independent effect): A large drop in the estimate after adjusting for a measured variable indicates that variable was confounding the crude result. The adjusted interval spans 1.0, so no independent association is shown. Effect modification would appear as different ratios within smoking strata, not as one shrinking pooled estimate.
  28. The correct answer is A (Discuss with the prescriber and dispense the commercial tablets, since no patient need justifies compounding): Compounding is meant to meet a patient need that a commercial product cannot, and NAPRA standards discourage preparing a copy of an available product. The belief that capsules are purer has no support, and manufactured tablets have tested content uniformity. Crushing tablets or weighing powder adds risk and no benefit, so the pharmacist should explain this to the prescriber.
  29. The correct answer is B (Stop spironolactone, arrange urgent reassessment and repeat potassium, and review diet and potassium products): A potassium of 6.0 mmol/L or higher on a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist calls for stopping it and rechecking promptly, even without symptoms or a creatinine rise. A dose reduction alone is suited to milder elevations. A potassium-sparing diuretic would add to the problem.
  30. The correct answer is B (Fidaxomicin 200 mg twice daily for 10 days is preferred for a first recurrence): Fidaxomicin lowers the chance of further recurrence compared with vancomycin because it spares more of the gut flora. Metronidazole is not recommended for recurrence, and a shorter vancomycin course does not address relapse. A tapered and pulsed vancomycin regimen can be used when fidaxomicin is unavailable, but it is not preferred, and a repeat standard vancomycin course is not the usual choice after an initial vancomycin course.
  31. The correct answer is D (Valacyclovir 2 g twice daily for one day, started right away at the tingling): Episodic treatment of cold sores is most effective when taken during the prodrome, and the one day valacyclovir 2 g twice daily regimen suits that purpose. Starting after vesicles form reduces the benefit, and the three day 500 mg regimen is for genital herpes. The seven day regimen is the zoster dose and is excessive here.
  32. The correct answer is D (Dispense the kit, acknowledge his past experience, and offer treatment information only if he asks): Naloxone is intended for wide availability, and an extra kit in another location is reasonable since overdoses occur in many places. Making the kit conditional on treatment discussion or documenting a refusal recreates the judgement that drives him away from care. Respectful, non-coercive contact keeps the door open for later conversations about treatment.
  33. The correct answer is C (Discuss adherence and barriers with her, and share the fill gaps with her physician): Filling a 30 day supply once in 75 days means she has had the drugs for well under half the time, so apparent resistance is most likely nonadherence. Adherence and barriers must be addressed before a fourth drug is added, otherwise the added agent is also likely to go untaken. Spironolactone is appropriate only after true resistant hypertension is confirmed.
  34. The correct answer is A (Privately ask the preferred name and pronouns, use them, and keep the legal name for claims): Using the patient's chosen name and pronouns, asked about privately, supports dignity, while the legal name is kept for purposes where it is required, such as insurance claims. Questions about surgical history or gender history that are not needed to dispense safely are intrusive. Skipping counselling removes an important safety service and is not respectful.
  35. The correct answer is C (28 tablets, because half a tablet twice daily uses one tablet each day): Half a tablet twice daily is one whole tablet per day, so a 28 day pack needs 28 tablets. Counting only one dosing time gives 14, counting a whole tablet at each time gives 56, and 42 comes from an invented one and a half tablets per day.
  36. The correct answer is B (Wait at least five weeks after the last fluoxetine dose, since its active metabolite persists): Fluoxetine and norfluoxetine have very long half-lives, so about five weeks are needed before an MAO inhibitor is started to avoid serotonin syndrome. Most other SSRIs need roughly 14 days. Overlap or short gaps risk a potentially fatal interaction.
  37. The correct answer is D (low molecular weight heparin, which does not cross the placenta and is preferred through pregnancy): Low molecular weight heparin does not cross the placenta and is the standard treatment for VTE in pregnancy. Warfarin crosses the placenta and causes embryopathy mainly in early pregnancy and bleeding later, and direct oral anticoagulants are avoided because pregnancy data are lacking.
  38. The correct answer is C (Each draw would deliver 200 units, so supply a U-500 pen or syringe): A U-100 syringe marked at 40 units holds 0.4 mL, and 0.4 mL of U-500 contains 200 units, a fivefold overdose that can cause severe, prolonged hypoglycemia. Dosing U-500 through a U-100 syringe also invites conversion errors, so a U-500 pen or a purpose-made syringe should be supplied.
  39. The correct answer is A (advise against the combination, since the total acetaminophen would exceed the daily maximum for her): Her regular acetaminophen supplies 3 g daily, and the combination caplets would add up to 4 g more, a total of about 7 g that is far above the usual 3 to 4 g daily ceiling and dangerous for the liver. Spacing the doses does not change the total daily amount. A cough product without acetaminophen would be a safer product.
  40. The correct answer is D (Take 1 tablet by mouth every other day, written out in full words): QOD is on the ISMP Canada list of dangerous abbreviations because it can be misread as qd (daily) or qid (four times daily), leading to a daily or fourfold dose. Writing every other day in plain words on the label removes the confusion. Twice weekly and every second week are different schedules that do not match the confirmed order.
  41. The correct answer is B (Ask her to describe in her own words how she will take it): Teach-back asks the patient to restate the plan in her own words, which tests how well the pharmacist explained it rather than how well the patient listened. A nod or an absence of questions does not show understanding. Repeating the same explanation adds no verification.
  42. The correct answer is B (22.4 g of cocoa butter, after allowing for 1.6 g of base the drug displaces): Total drug is 12 x 200 mg, or 2.4 g. With a displacement factor of 1.5, 1.5 g of drug displaces 1 g of cocoa butter, so 2.4 g displaces 1.6 g of base. Twelve molds hold 24 g of base, and 24 g minus 1.6 g leaves 22.4 g to melt.
  43. The correct answer is A (Yes, valacyclovir 1 g three times daily for seven days should start now): Antiviral therapy for herpes zoster works best when started within 72 hours of rash onset, and he is at about 48 hours. Valacyclovir 1 g three times daily for seven days is the standard zoster dose. The shorter, lower dose regimens are meant for genital herpes, and waiting for crusting loses the benefit.
  44. The correct answer is D (Separate the antacid from levothyroxine by at least four hours, such as after supper): Calcium binds levothyroxine in the gut and reduces its absorption, which can raise her TSH over time. A gap of at least four hours is recommended, so an antacid later in the day or after supper works. Shorter gaps such as thirty minutes or one hour are not enough to prevent the binding.
  45. The correct answer is C (Amoxicillin 1 g three times daily, with treatment usually continued for five days if clinically stable): High dose amoxicillin is first line for otherwise healthy adults with outpatient pneumonia because it covers Streptococcus pneumoniae reliably, and about five days is adequate when the patient improves. Fluoroquinolones are reserved for comorbidity or allergy. Cephalexin has poor pneumococcal activity and no atypical coverage.
  46. The correct answer is D (Stop, respect the withdrawal of consent, and document the refusal and any counselling given): A patient may withdraw consent at any point before treatment, and proceeding afterward would be a battery. The payment and the prepared dose are not factors, so the pharmacist stops and records what happened.
  47. The correct answer is D (Suggest azithromycin instead of clarithromycin, and ask him to avoid grapefruit juice while on buspirone): Buspirone is extensively metabolized by CYP3A4, so clarithromycin and grapefruit juice can sharply raise its levels and cause dizziness, nausea and sedation. Azithromycin has minimal CYP3A4 inhibition. Alprazolam is also a CYP3A4 substrate, and increasing buspirone would worsen the interaction.
  48. The correct answer is A (Atorvastatin 80 mg once daily, with a repeat lipid panel in about eight weeks): High-intensity statin therapy lowers LDL by about 50% and includes atorvastatin 40 to 80 mg and rosuvastatin 20 to 40 mg daily. Simvastatin 80 mg is no longer used because of a higher myopathy risk, while pravastatin 40 mg and fluvastatin 80 mg are moderate intensity. Lipids are rechecked 4 to 12 weeks after a change.
  49. The correct answer is B (His target is a systolic pressure below 130 mmHg, so intensification should be recommended): Hypertension Canada recommends a treatment target of a systolic pressure below 130 mmHg for adults with diabetes, and a systolic pressure of 137 mmHg is above it. The general 140/90 mmHg target and an age-based relaxation do not apply here, and a systolic target below 120 mmHg is not the recommended target for him.
  50. The correct answer is D (Ask the patient to state how she will take the tablet each week): Final verification closes the counselling loop by having the patient show or state the plan, so errors can be corrected before she leaves. Asking whether it seemed clear invites a polite yes. A monograph or an invitation to call does not confirm understanding at the time of dispensing.
  51. The correct answer is D (Confirm a negative pregnancy test, then inject and advise condoms for seven days): The injection interval can be stretched slightly beyond 12 weeks, but at 16 weeks protection may have lapsed, so pregnancy should be excluded first. After a late injection, backup contraception is used for seven days. Waiting for a period leaves her unprotected.
  52. The correct answer is A (Console the technician, review the system contributors, and fix the software and workflow weaknesses): Just culture separates human error, at-risk behaviour and reckless behaviour. Human error is managed by consoling the person and fixing the system conditions that made the error likely. Punishing based on the outcome, or discouraging reports, drives events underground and does not prevent recurrence.
  53. The correct answer is A (A current tertiary source such as a pharmacology textbook or compendium of drug monographs): Background questions about established drugs are answered fastest and most reliably from tertiary sources, which summarize accepted evidence. Primary studies and systematic reviews take longer and suit novel or controversial questions. Manufacturer departments help with product specific data but are not the usual starting point.
  54. The correct answer is D (Offer oseltamivir prophylaxis to all unit residents, including vaccinated ones, for at least 14 days): In an institutional outbreak, vaccine protection in frail older adults is incomplete, so antiviral prophylaxis is offered to all residents on the affected unit, vaccinated or not. It runs for at least 14 days and continues until about a week after the last case. Five days at treatment doses is too short and too high.
  55. The correct answer is B (Per 1,000 patients treated, about 40 strokes are avoided and 25 bleeds occur, favouring treatment): An NNT of 25 means about 40 strokes avoided per 1,000 treated, while an NNH of 40 means about 25 serious bleeds per 1,000. A larger NNH signals rarer harm, not more harm. Because the patient weights a disabling stroke more heavily, the balance favours treatment, and NNT and NNH can be compared when the events are weighed by importance.
  56. The correct answer is D (4 tablets daily, which supplies about 31 mEq of bicarbonate each day): A target of 30 mEq divided by 7.7 mEq per tablet is 3.9, so 4 tablets give 30.8 mEq, the closest whole-tablet dose. Three tablets would fall about 25% short of the target, and six would overshoot it by half.
  57. The correct answer is B (Map the sequence of events and identify where the process deviated, asking why at each point): A root cause analysis builds a timeline of what happened, then repeatedly asks why to move from the visible error to underlying system causes such as labelling, storage, workload or communication. Corrective actions are chosen after the causes are understood, and the goal is not to assign individual blame. Closing the review early would leave the same hazards in place.
  58. The correct answer is A (sound-alike brand names combined with a verbal order that was not read back): Celexa and Celebrex sound similar, and a spoken order without read-back and verification of the indication gives no chance to catch the mix-up. Confirming the drug name by spelling it and stating the indication prevents this error. The other factors do not explain a name confusion.
  59. The correct answer is B (Use reliable contraception, because spironolactone can feminize a male fetus if pregnancy occurs): Spironolactone is an antiandrogen that can interfere with male genital development, so effective contraception is required while taking it for hirsutism. It raises rather than lowers potassium, so supplements are not appropriate. Improvement in hair growth takes months, not two weeks.
  60. The correct answer is C (reduce warfarin by about 25 to 30% and check the INR weekly for several weeks): Amiodarone inhibits warfarin metabolism and the interaction builds over weeks, so INR rises. A proactive dose reduction with close monitoring is standard. Amiodarone does not induce warfarin metabolism, and aspirin alone is not adequate stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation.
  61. The correct answer is C (Take 15 g of fast carbohydrate, such as juice, then recheck in 15 minutes): Mild to moderate hypoglycemia in a conscious patient is treated with 15 g of fast-acting carbohydrate, with a recheck after 15 minutes. Chocolate and milk contain fat and protein that slow absorption, so they raise glucose too slowly. Doubling the dose risks a rebound high, and delaying the recheck leaves a low unconfirmed.
  62. The correct answer is D (A new prescription is needed, since narcotic prescriptions cannot be refilled under federal regulations): The Narcotic Control Regulations do not allow a narcotic prescription to be refilled, so a repeat notation is not valid. A new prescription, or a verbal order from the prescriber for a new prescription where permitted, is needed. Timing and documentation do not change that rule.
  63. The correct answer is D (extended treatment is offered because unprovoked proximal DVT carries a high recurrence risk off therapy): An unprovoked proximal DVT recurs in roughly one in ten patients in the first year after anticoagulation stops, so patients with low bleeding risk are usually offered extended therapy. Aspirin lowers recurrence far less than an anticoagulant, and a D-dimer does not decide whether treatment can stop.
  64. The correct answer is B (A sterile 0.22 micron membrane filter, which retains bacteria and meets the requirement for sterilizing filtration): A membrane with a nominal pore size of 0.22 micron or smaller retains bacteria and is the accepted standard for sterilizing filtration of heat-labile solutions. A 0.45 micron or 1.2 micron filter only clarifies the solution or removes large particles and does not sterilize. A 5 micron filter needle only catches glass or rubber fragments.
  65. The correct answer is B (Advise against ginkgo, because it may impair platelet function and add to her bleeding risk): Ginkgo has antiplatelet effects and has been linked to bleeding, so combining it with an anticoagulant adds risk, while evidence for memory benefit is weak. Separating doses does not change a pharmacodynamic interaction, and unsupervised dose reduction of apixaban would raise stroke risk.
  66. The correct answer is C (34, calculated as 1 divided by 0.03 and rounded up to a whole patient): The absolute risk reduction is 8% minus 5%, or 3 percentage points. The number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.03, which is 33.3, rounded up to 34 so the benefit is not overstated. Using the ARR itself or either group's event rate does not produce an NNT.
  67. The correct answer is C (No more than 2 g daily in divided doses, with alcohol avoided): Acetaminophen remains the preferred analgesic in cirrhosis, but the daily total is limited to about 2 g because glutathione stores are reduced and alcohol adds risk. NSAIDs are a poor substitute since they can cause kidney injury and variceal or gastrointestinal bleeding in cirrhosis. The usual 4 g ceiling is not appropriate here, and near normal blood tests do not remove the need for a lower limit.
  68. The correct answer is A (cause coronary artery vasoconstriction and are contraindicated in patients with ischemic heart disease): Triptans act on serotonin receptors that also constrict coronary arteries, so they are contraindicated after a myocardial infarction and in other ischemic heart disease. The alert is therefore a true contraindication rather than a minor warning. The other effects listed are not characteristic of triptans.
  69. The correct answer is B (Decline to share details without her consent and offer to relay his concern to her): Personal health information, including drug names and fill dates, may not be released to a family member without consent or lawful authority. Offering to relay the husband's concern lets JM decide what to share. A spouse does not have presumed access, and showing identification only proves who he is, not that he is entitled to the profile.
  70. The correct answer is C (Before starting work, so the employer can plan coverage that keeps patient access timely): Colleges expect pharmacists to disclose a conscientious objection early, such as to an employer, so that coverage can be arranged without delaying patients. Waiting for a patient request or for a manager to ask creates avoidable disputes and access problems. Disclosure before starting work is the professional approach.
  71. The correct answer is A (share the review with the nurse practitioner, because both providers are directly involved in her care): Providers who are directly involved in a patient's care form the circle of care, and information needed for that care may be shared under implied consent unless the patient has restricted it. A new signed form is not required in this setting. Sharing should still be limited to what the nurse practitioner needs.
  72. The correct answer is C (Suggest an antibiotic without CYP3A4 inhibition, such as doxycycline, and inform the transplant team): Clarithromycin strongly inhibits CYP3A4 and can raise tacrolimus to toxic levels within days. An antibiotic without this interaction avoids the problem, and the transplant team can confirm the choice. Pre-emptive halving or late monitoring leaves levels unpredictable, and macrolides raise rather than lower tacrolimus.
  73. The correct answer is A (A statin is advised for adults with diabetes aged 40 or older, whatever the LDL): Diabetes Canada recommends a statin for most adults with diabetes aged 40 or older to lower cardiovascular risk, whatever the starting LDL. Good glycemic and blood pressure control do not replace statin therapy. The LDL and age thresholds in the other statements are not the criteria used in the guideline.
  74. The correct answer is B (Test at least four weeks after antibiotics, holding the PPI for two weeks first): A breath test can read falsely negative when a PPI is still suppressing bacterial load or when antibiotics were taken within the previous four weeks. Holding the PPI for two weeks and waiting four weeks after the antibiotics gives a reliable result. Confirmation matters for gastric ulcers because persistent infection raises the risk of recurrence and complications.
  75. The correct answer is D (Wear gloves, use a dedicated tray and spatula, clean them afterward, and never crush tablets): Oral hazardous drugs can shed dust and contaminate counting surfaces, so staff should wear gloves, count on a tray and spatula set aside for hazardous drugs, and clean them afterward. Tablets must not be crushed or split by the pharmacy because this releases drug particles. A shared tray or automated pouch machine transfers residue to other patients' medications.
  76. The correct answer is B (Cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia, including stimulus control and sleep restriction, before any sleeping pill): Canadian and international guidelines place CBT-I, with stimulus control and sleep restriction, ahead of drug therapy for chronic insomnia because its benefits last after treatment ends. Sedative-hypnotics and sedating antihistamines add risks and lose effect over time. Drug therapy is reserved for short courses when CBT-I fails or is unavailable.
  77. The correct answer is A (Most well-informed patients would choose the intervention, and clinicians can apply it to most patients): A strong recommendation means the desirable effects clearly outweigh the undesirable ones for nearly all informed patients, so most would want the intervention. It is not an obligation for every individual, and patient circumstances still matter. Strength of recommendation is separate from the design of the supporting studies.
  78. The correct answer is B (It protects the public by setting standards, registering pharmacists and handling public complaints): Provincial colleges are self-regulating bodies whose mandate is protection of the public, which they pursue by registering practitioners, setting standards of practice and handling complaints and discipline. Advocacy and fee negotiation belong to associations, and a college does not award compensation.
  79. The correct answer is B (Confirm the patient's identity with two identifiers, such as full name and date of birth): Release of a prescription requires confirming that the person is the right patient, usually with two identifiers such as full name and birth date or address. Familiarity can be mistaken, a signature alone does not verify identity, and asking someone to answer to a name read aloud also exposes private information.
  80. The correct answer is A (Fifteen days per bottle, so the next bottle is due on day sixteen): Each day uses 17 g x 2 = 34 g of powder. A 510 g bottle therefore lasts 510 / 34 = 15 days. Thirty days would apply only to a once daily order, and the other intervals do not match the daily powder use.
  81. The correct answer is A (Inhibits CYP3A4, raising simvastatin levels and increasing the risk of myopathy and rhabdomyolysis): Clarithromycin is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and simvastatin depends heavily on CYP3A4 for clearance, so exposure rises several fold and muscle toxicity becomes likely. The statin should be held during the antibiotic course or the antibiotic changed. Induction and protein binding displacement are not the mechanism here.
  82. The correct answer is C (Wait at least six hours after inhaling and longer if any effects persist): Health Canada advises waiting at least six hours after inhaling cannabis before driving, and longer if any effects remain, because impairment can persist after the high fades. Two hours is too short, and feeling alert does not rule out impairment. Vaporizing does not make driving safe sooner.
  83. The correct answer is C (His wife, since a spouse generally ranks ahead of an adult child in statutory lists): Provincial laws set a ranked list of substitute decision makers, and a spouse or partner usually ranks above children. Clinicians propose treatment but do not act as the substitute, and arrival time has no bearing on the hierarchy.
  84. The correct answer is D (Lorazepam, because it is conjugated by glucuronidation and has no active metabolites): Lorazepam and oxazepam are eliminated by glucuronidation, which is relatively preserved in cirrhosis, and they have no active metabolites to accumulate. Diazepam and chlordiazepoxide rely on hepatic oxidation and produce long-acting active metabolites that can cause excess sedation or precipitate encephalopathy. Midazolam also accumulates when hepatic clearance falls.
  85. The correct answer is C (Insulin glargine, because dosing errors with insulin can cause severe hypoglycemia and lasting brain injury): Insulin is on the ISMP Canada list of high-alert medications because errors can cause severe harm even with small dosing differences. Docusate, calcium and acetaminophen carry risk like any medication, but they do not meet the high-alert definition. High-alert status calls for added safeguards such as independent double checks, standardized concentrations and storage controls.
  86. The correct answer is A (About 76 mL/min, so nitrofurantoin is suitable for a short course): For a woman, CrCl is (140 minus 58) x 62 x 1.04 divided by 70, which gives about 76 mL/min. That is above the 60 mL/min cutoff in the Canadian product monograph for nitrofurantoin, so a standard short course is appropriate. The male constant would overestimate the result, and a decimal slip would underestimate it.
  87. The correct answer is A (Explain that a physician or nurse practitioner assesses eligibility and offer provincial access information): Eligibility is decided through independent assessments by a physician or nurse practitioner, not by the pharmacist. The pharmacist helps by explaining this and pointing the patient to the provincial access process. A court order is not needed, and waiting for deterioration is not a legal requirement for requesting an assessment.
  88. The correct answer is D (Assessing the appropriateness of a new prescription and counselling the patient on it): Clinical assessment of therapy and patient counselling on new drugs are pharmacist responsibilities that require professional judgement and cannot be delegated. Data entry, counting and filling, and taking refill requests are technical or support tasks that technicians and trained assistants can perform under the applicable provincial rules.
  89. The correct answer is A (2 mg after each loose stool, to a maximum of 8 mg daily): Nonprescription loperamide is labelled for an initial 4 mg followed by 2 mg after each loose stool, with a self-treatment maximum of 8 mg per day. Self-treatment should not continue beyond about two days, so a longer course needs assessment. Higher doses raise the risk of cardiac toxicity and ileus.
  90. The correct answer is C (Combining an NSAID with an ACE inhibitor and diuretic increases the risk of acute kidney injury): NSAIDs constrict the afferent arteriole by reducing prostaglandins, while the ACE inhibitor dilates the efferent arteriole and the diuretic lowers volume, so glomerular filtration can collapse. This combination is often called the triple whammy. NSAIDs tend to raise blood pressure rather than lower it, and they do not induce enzymes that clear ramipril.
  91. The correct answer is A (Start low, such as 25 mcg daily, and titrate every six to eight weeks): Older patients and those with coronary disease can develop angina or arrhythmia when thyroid hormone raises myocardial oxygen demand, so treatment starts low and increases slowly. Full replacement from day one risks precipitating ischemia. Withholding treatment leaves overt disease untreated, which worsens cardiac and metabolic risk.
  92. The correct answer is C (Quarantine the boxes and ask the manufacturer for stability data before deciding on use): The exposure time is unknown, so the pharmacist cannot show the stock stayed within any labelled allowance. The manufacturer may hold data on room temperature excursions that supports or rules out use. Moving the boxes to the refrigerator does not undo earlier degradation, and the refill should be sourced from properly stored stock meanwhile.
  93. The correct answer is C (Decline, because verifying and counselling are pharmacist acts needing a practising registration, not just competence): Verification and counselling are part of the practice of pharmacy, so they require a practising registration whatever the duration or supervision. A manager's letter or a second pharmacist on site does not change MB's registration status, and reporting the shift afterward would simply document unauthorized practice.
  94. The correct answer is A (Take 1 tablet by mouth in the morning): Patient-friendly labels use everyday words such as 'by mouth' and 'in the morning' and avoid abbreviations and Latin terms. The other wordings keep jargon such as qd, AM, oral route and per os that many patients cannot interpret.
  95. The correct answer is A (Autonomy, because a capable patient may accept or refuse treatment after informed discussion): Autonomy protects the right of a capable person to accept or refuse treatment after understanding the risks. MR understands her options, so her family's wishes do not override her choice. Beneficence and non-maleficence guide the pharmacist to inform and advise, but they do not justify overriding a capable refusal.
  96. A taper over two weeks and a low dose short course lack support, and omitting steroids misses a proven benefit.
  97. The correct answer is A (Use of the product carries a reasonable probability of serious adverse health consequences or death): Type I is the highest risk class: a reasonable probability of serious harm or death, so removal is most urgent. Type II involves temporary or reversible effects with remote risk of serious harm, and Type III involves products unlikely to cause harm. The class does not limit which level of the supply chain must act.
  98. The correct answer is C (Draw one legible line through it, then write the correct dose, initials and date): Health records must remain a reliable legal account of what was recorded and when. A single legible line, with initials and date, shows the original entry and who corrected it. Covering, erasing or destroying entries conceals the original and can be treated as record tampering.
  99. The correct answer is A (Reduce to 30 mg subcutaneously once daily because clearance is below 30 mL/min): Enoxaparin is cleared by the kidney, and with a creatinine clearance below 30 mL/min the prophylactic dose is reduced to 30 mg once daily to avoid accumulation and bleeding. Surgical type does not remove the need for renal adjustment. Stretching the interval to 48 hours or raising the dose would both miss the labelled adjustment.
  100. The correct answer is C (Contact the walk-in prescriber and family physician to clarify therapy before dispensing the oxycodone): Two opioids from different prescribers combined with a benzodiazepine in an older adult with sedation and a fall creates a serious risk of respiratory depression and injury. The pharmacist should communicate with both prescribers to rationalize therapy before another opioid is added. Dispensing alone ignores the duplication, and stopping a drug unilaterally risks withdrawal.
  101. Ondansetron and metoclopramide are second-line alternatives, and scopolamine is not preferred because of anticholinergic effects and limited pregnancy evidence.
  102. The correct answer is A (Antidepressant discontinuation syndrome, managed by restarting paroxetine and then tapering the dose slowly): Short half-life SSRIs such as paroxetine commonly cause dizziness, sensory disturbances, nausea and irritability within days of abrupt cessation. Restarting the drug relieves symptoms, and a slower taper prevents recurrence. Serotonin syndrome arises from excess serotonergic activity, not withdrawal.
  103. The correct answer is C (32 days, after subtracting the four priming actuations from the total available): Priming uses four of the 200 actuations, leaving 196 for the patient. At two puffs three times daily the patient uses six actuations per day, and 196 divided by 6 is 32.7, so the inhaler supports 32 full days. Ignoring the priming sprays gives 33 days, and the other values come from misreading the daily puff count.
  104. His LDL and non-HDL both exceed these cut points. A risk above 20% or an LDL of 5.0 mmol/L would qualify him independently but are not required, and calcium scoring is optional.
  105. The correct answer is B (Increase the sample size, because detecting the same difference with greater certainty requires more participants): With the significance level and expected effect held constant, power rises only when the study obtains more information, which usually means more participants or more events. Smaller samples and fewer events lower power, and a test chosen for a secondary outcome cannot change the power of the primary comparison.
  106.   107. The correct answer is D (Revise it to reflect current practice, train staff on the changes, and set a review date): A procedure is only useful if it reflects what staff actually do, so it needs revision after changes in suppliers or software, staff training on the revision, and a scheduled review. Keeping an outdated version or relying on verbal teaching leaves practice inconsistent and impossible to audit. Supplier documents do not replace the pharmacy's own procedure, which must meet its regulatory obligations.
  108. His fasting and other readings are in range, so basal insulin is working. The supper dose acts overnight hours earlier, and adding NPH would add hypoglycemia risk without addressing the cause.
  109.   110. A high colony count alone does not define infection without symptoms. Serial cultures only generate more positive results that tempt unnecessary treatment.
  111.   112. The upper arm and buttock are slower or less dependable sites, and a subcutaneous injection into the abdomen gives slower absorption than the intramuscular route.
  113.   114. Tracing the vials, retrieving any already dispensed, and reporting the event allow patient harm to be prevented and the process to be corrected.   115. The correct answer is D (In the Data section, together with measured values and the medication history gathered at the visit): DAP stands for Data, Assessment and Plan. Data holds what was reported by the patient and what was measured or observed. Assessment holds the pharmacist's interpretation, such as a possible metformin adverse effect, and Plan holds the actions and follow up.
  116. The correct answer is A (20%, calculated as the absolute difference of 1.0% divided by the control rate): Relative risk reduction is the absolute difference in event rates divided by the control event rate: 1.0 divided by 5.0 equals 20%. Simple subtraction gives the absolute risk reduction, and 4% is only the treated group's event rate. Dividing the treated rate by the control rate gives a relative risk of 0.8, which is not the reduction.
  117. Acetaminophen is the safer choice for fever in this setting. The other effects named are not the established concern.
  118. The correct answer is B (15 to 25 degrees Celsius, as defined for controlled room temperature in Canadian monographs): Canadian monographs define controlled room temperature as 15 to 25 degrees Celsius. The narrower 20 to 25 degree range comes from United States standards, and the 2 to 8 degree range is for refrigerated products. Stock exposed to temperatures outside the labelled range needs assessment before it is used.
  119. LK should keep verifying, record the concern and escalate through the pharmacy and the college if needed. Partial compliance still breaches the standard, and walking away leaves patients unprotected.
  120.   121. The correct answer is C (Effect modification, because the effect truly differs by diabetes status and is reported by stratum): When the size of a treatment effect varies across levels of another factor, that factor is an effect modifier. The appropriate response is to present stratum-specific results, not to adjust the difference away. Confounding distorts an association, whereas effect modification is a real feature of it.
  122. The correct answer is B (acceptable for a young child, since tidal breathing through the chamber delivers the dose): A valved holding chamber lets a child who cannot hold a single slow breath still receive the drug, because the aerosol stays suspended while she breathes normally for several breaths. Each puff should be sprayed and inhaled separately, so firing two puffs together into the chamber wastes drug. A fast deep breath worsens deposition in the throat, and a dry powder inhaler needs a forceful inhalation that most young children cannot generate.
  123. The correct answer is D (20 days, since the patient uses 5 mL of solution each day): At 1 mg/mL, a 2.5 mg dose is 2.5 mL, and two doses daily total 5 mL. A 100 mL bottle divided by 5 mL per day gives 20 days. Using only one dose per day gives 40 days, which ignores the twice daily schedule.
  124. Doubling 2.0 gives 4.0 mcg/L, close to his baseline of 4.2, so no new concern is raised. A fall that fails to reach about half, or any later rise, would need attention.
  125. The correct answer is A (Use a paired t-test, because each patient acts as their own baseline control): The same 30 patients were measured twice, so the observations are paired and the analysis must account for that. A paired t-test analyzes the within patient differences and, with normal data, is the right test. An unpaired test ignores the pairing and loses power, and chi-square and ANOVA address other designs.
Case CHB16

A randomized trial of 3,000 adults with heart failure compared a new add-on drug with placebo over 18 months. The primary outcome was hospitalization for heart failure: 12.0% in the drug group (180 of 1,500) and 15.0% in the placebo group (225 of 1,500). The relative risk was 0.80 (95% CI 0.67 to 0.96), p = 0.017. The prespecified significance level was 0.05.

  1. The correct answer is C (Data are compatible with a true risk reduction of roughly 4% to 33%, not zero): A relative risk of 0.67 to 0.96 corresponds to a risk reduction of about 4% to 33%. Because 1.0 lies outside the interval, no effect is not compatible with the data at the 5% level. The interval describes uncertainty around the population estimate, not the spread of individual responses.
  2. The correct answer is A (About 34, from an absolute risk reduction of 3 percentage points, rounded up): The absolute risk reduction is 15.0% minus 12.0%, or 3 percentage points. The number needed to treat is its reciprocal, 1 divided by 0.03, which is 33.3 and is rounded up to 34. Using relative measures or a single group rate does not give the number of patients treated per event prevented.
  3. The correct answer is C (If the drug did nothing, results at least this extreme would occur 1.7% of the time): A p value is the probability of data at least as extreme as those observed, assuming the null hypothesis is true. It is not the probability that the null hypothesis is true, and it says nothing about the size or importance of the benefit, which must be judged from the effect estimate and interval.
  4. The correct answer is C (Replace glyburide with gliclazide modified release, which carries less hypoglycemia risk in older adults): Glyburide has active metabolites that accumulate as kidney function falls, so lows are frequent and prolonged in frail older adults. Gliclazide modified release has no active metabolites and causes fewer severe lows than glyburide. Raising the dose or combining two sulfonylureas increases risk further, and timing the tablet after meals does not remove the problem.
  5. The correct answer is D (Thirty to sixty minutes before departure, repeating every four to six hours if needed): Antihistamines work best as prophylaxis, so the dose is taken 30 to 60 minutes before travel and may be repeated every 4 to 6 hours. Taking it after symptoms start gives poor control. A single evening dose has worn off long before the crossing.
  6. The correct answer is B (Pause the infusion, give an antihistamine, then restart at 10 mg/min or slower): Flushing and itching of the upper body during a rapid infusion is vancomycin infusion reaction, caused by histamine release, not true allergy. It is managed by pausing, giving an antihistamine, and restarting at a slower rate, with 1.5 g needing at least 150 minutes at 10 mg/min. Epinephrine and labelling an allergy are not warranted without hypotension or airway signs.
  7. The correct answer is C (Registrants should promptly tell the college about charges, and the college decides on restrictions): Most colleges require registrants to report criminal charges and findings promptly so the college, not the registrant, can judge whether public protection requires conditions. Waiting for a verdict removes the college's ability to respond. An offence involving impairment can bear on fitness to practise even when no medication was involved.
  8. The correct answer is A (Avoid sexual contact until 7 days after treatment and until his partner is treated): After single-dose therapy, patients should abstain for 7 days and until all partners have been treated, otherwise reinfection and ongoing transmission occur. Condoms reduce but do not remove the risk, and symptom resolution does not show a partner is cured. A 6 month wait is longer than needed.
  9. The correct answer is D (Continue the current dose and repeat the level once steady state is reached, about five half-lives): With a 24 hour half-life, steady state takes roughly five days, so the level on day three is still rising and does not reflect the final concentration. Doubling the dose now risks toxicity once accumulation finishes. Repeating the level at steady state allows a sound decision.
  10. The correct answer is C (Refer for medical assessment, since a first postmenopausal episode needs diagnosis and fluconazole raises warfarin effect): A first episode of vaginal symptoms after menopause should be assessed by a clinician because other diagnoses are possible. Fluconazole inhibits CYP2C9 and can markedly raise INR in patients taking warfarin. Changing warfarin doses without monitoring risks bleeding or clotting.
  11. The correct answer is C (Discard the batch, remake it by melting the base slowly with minimal heat, and document): Overheating cocoa butter produces unstable crystal forms that melt near room temperature and give a grainy product. Chilling or remelting the batch does not reliably restore the stable form, so the suppositories no longer meet quality standards. The base should be melted gently, just until it is mostly liquid, and the remake documented.
  12. The correct answer is B (Contact the prescriber, because cefadroxil shares an identical side chain with amoxicillin): Cefadroxil has the same R1 side chain as amoxicillin, which makes cross reactivity a real concern after a prior anaphylactic reaction. A different antibiotic with a dissimilar side chain or no beta-lactam should be requested. Observation or rash monitoring does not make a high risk exposure acceptable.
  13. The correct answer is D (It acts mainly locally, has low systemic absorption, and is effective for vaginal symptoms): Low dose vaginal estrogen treats genitourinary symptoms by acting at the tissue, with minimal systemic absorption. Because blood levels stay low, a progestogen is generally not needed. It does not treat hot flashes, and its benefit continues in older women.
  14. The correct answer is B (Use tall-man lettering on tags and alerts, and store the two products apart): Look-alike names are a system problem, and the most reliable fixes change the environment: tall-man lettering emphasizes the differing letters, and separate storage reduces mix-ups at the point of picking. Reminders and extra vigilance depend on memory and do not hold up under workload.
  15. The correct answer is B (lenses should be removed before instilling the drops and reinserted after at least ten minutes): Seasonal, bilateral itching with watery discharge and no pain fits allergic conjunctivitis, which an antihistamine eye drop treats. The preservative in many such drops can build up in soft lenses, so the lenses come out first and go back in after about ten minutes. Antibiotics have no role without signs of bacterial infection.
  16. The correct answer is C (Three refills, since the original fill does not count and one refill has been used): The original dispensing is not a refill. Two fills means one refill has been used, so 4 - 1 = 3 refills remain. A transfer does not reset or add to the authorization the prescriber wrote.
  17. The correct answer is A (Obtain the amber bottles specified, because the stability data apply only to the container tested): A beyond-use date is valid only for the formulation, container and storage conditions covered by the stability data. Changing the bottle material and colour removes that support, and halving the date or adding foil is a guess rather than evidence. An urgent need does not change this, so the pharmacist should source the specified container or use another validated formulation.
  18. The correct answer is C (The oncologist submits a Special Access Program request to Health Canada for this life-threatening condition): The Special Access Program lets a practitioner request an unauthorized drug for a patient with a serious or life-threatening condition when conventional therapies have failed or are unsuitable. The practitioner, not the patient or the pharmacy, makes the request to Health Canada. Importing, compounding and provincial plans do not create legal authority to sell an unapproved drug.
  19. The correct answer is B (Doxycycline 100 mg twice daily, which covers MRSA without a sulfonamide component): With documented MRSA and purulence, the regimen needs MRSA activity. His sulfonamide reaction removes trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, leaving doxycycline as a suitable oral agent. Beta-lactams such as cephalexin and amoxicillin do not cover MRSA.
  20. The correct answer is B (Choose the drug with proven fracture reduction, since bone density is only a surrogate outcome): Fractures are the outcome that matters to FT, and bone mineral density is a surrogate that does not always predict fracture reduction. The drug with trial evidence of fewer fractures is the better supported selection. Larger surrogate gains alone do not show a clinical benefit.
  21. The correct answer is D (Withhold the drug and contact the prescriber about the documented penicillin allergy before any dispensing): The order has several problems: cc is a dangerous abbreviation that should be mL, and the label should read three times daily for ten days. The documented penicillin allergy with a rash is the priority, because amoxicillin is a penicillin and could cause a more serious reaction. Cephalexin shares a similar side chain and should not be substituted without the prescriber, and counselling does not replace avoiding a known allergen.
  22. The correct answer is A (Both pass into milk in very small amounts and are preferred analgesics during breastfeeding): Acetaminophen and ibuprofen both transfer into milk at very low levels, with a low relative infant dose, so they are preferred analgesics in lactation. Pumping and discarding is unnecessary for either drug. Ibuprofen has not been linked to bleeding in breastfed infants at usual doses.
  23. The correct answer is A (180 tablets, which equals three more fills of 60 tablets each on this prescription): The prescription authorizes 6 fills in total: the original plus 5 refills. Three have been used (original plus 2 refills), leaving 3 fills of 60 tablets, which is 180 tablets. The larger numbers forget the fills already used.
  24. The correct answer is D (Refer him to his physician for confirmation with home or ambulatory blood pressure monitoring): An elevated average office pressure should be confirmed with out-of-office measurement before a diagnosis is made, as Hypertension Canada recommends. A reading of 148/92 is above the 140/90 office threshold, so waiting six months is too long. Diuretics are prescription drugs and a diagnosis is not made at a screening event.
  25. The correct answer is A (w/w means grams per 100 g of product; w/v means grams per 100 mL): Weight per weight expresses grams of drug in 100 g of the finished product, which suits creams and ointments that are weighed. Weight per volume expresses grams in 100 mL, which suits solutions and lotions that are measured by volume. A 10% w/w and a 10% w/v product therefore differ in how the amount is measured, and the amounts in two containers are only equal if the weights or volumes match.
  26. The correct answer is D (At least two years from the date of each entry, as the federal minimum): The Narcotic Control Regulations require narcotic records to be kept for at least two years. Provincial colleges often set longer periods for prescription records, so the federal period is a minimum. An annual count is not a retention rule, and ten years is not the federal requirement.
  27. The correct answer is D (Codeine is contraindicated in children under 12 years, so contact the prescriber): Health Canada contraindicates codeine in children under 12 because some children convert it very rapidly to morphine, which can cause fatal respiratory depression, and this cannot be predicted. Dose adjustment or observation does not remove that risk. The pharmacist should ask for a non-codeine analgesic such as ibuprofen or acetaminophen.
  28. The correct answer is C (Check canister contents at every refill, with a second person confirming the barcode match): Automation moves the main risk to the canister loading step, where the wrong drug in a canister causes many wrong fills. Verifying contents and barcode at each refill, with an independent person, controls that risk. The pharmacist still performs the final check of filled vials, and canister loading must be limited to trained staff using the barcode system.
  29. The correct answer is B (Temporarily hold the ACE inhibitor, spironolactone, dapagliflozin and furosemide, and contact her care team today): During dehydrating illness, drugs that lower blood pressure, volume or renal perfusion or raise potassium are paused as sick day management, then restarted once she eats and drinks normally. She needs same day assessment because of dizziness. Doubling the diuretic would worsen volume depletion and kidney injury.
  30. The correct answer is A (8 turns per year, with about 46 days of inventory on hand): Turnover equals cost of goods sold divided by average inventory, so 1,200,000 / 150,000 = 8 turns. Days on hand equals 365 / 8, about 46 days. The other pairings use an incorrect turnover or an inconsistent days calculation.
  31. The correct answer is A (The pharmacist assesses suitability, including his hypertension, and gives advice before the sale is completed): Schedule II products require a pharmacist to be involved in the sale, assessing whether the product suits the patient and giving advice, which matters here because decongestants can raise blood pressure. A prescription is not needed, and self-selection is not permitted for this schedule.
  32. The correct answer is D (Ask the prescriber to use azithromycin, which barely inhibits CYP3A4, and continue atorvastatin): Clarithromycin raises atorvastatin exposure several fold, increasing the risk of myopathy and rhabdomyolysis at an 80 mg dose. Azithromycin does not meaningfully inhibit CYP3A4, so it treats the infection without changing statin levels. Spacing the doses does not help because the inhibition lasts for days, and coenzyme Q10 has no proven protective effect.
  33. The correct answer is D (the AUC ratio to the brand has a 90% interval within 80 to 125%): Bioequivalence is judged from blood concentration data: the 90% confidence interval of the ratio of AUC (and Cmax) between generic and brand must fall within 80 to 125%. This does not mean the generic holds 80% of the dose. Generics may differ in fillers, shape and colour, and clinical symptom trials are not required.
  34. The correct answer is A (No, continuing anticoagulation depends on his CHADS-65 risk profile and not on his current rhythm): Stroke risk after ablation is governed by patient risk factors such as age, hypertension, diabetes and heart failure, and recurrences of atrial fibrillation can be silent. He has hypertension and is over 65, so anticoagulation continues. Aspirin does not provide adequate protection, and there is no fixed twelve month stopping point.
  35.
