He has none, so no antithrombotic therapy is recommended, and aspirin is not suggested for stroke prevention. Paroxysmal pattern and symptoms do not change that decision, and his risk factors are reviewed each year.
  36. The correct answer is B (Interview AG or a caregiver, then verify with at least one other source): A best possible medication history uses a structured patient or caregiver interview plus at least one other source, such as dispensing records or a medication list. Single sources are frequently incomplete, particularly for over the counter products and recent changes. Drowsiness limits the reliability of a single recall by the patient.
  37. The correct answer is A (Place the new stock behind the older stock so earlier expiry dates are used first): First expiry, first out means products with the earliest expiry dates are positioned to be used first, so the older bottle stays at the front. Mixing lots in one container loses traceability for recalls, and rotating by invoice date can leave a product with an earlier expiry behind a later one.
  38. The correct answer is C (0.8 mL, calculated from 20 mg divided by a concentration of 25 mg/mL): Volume equals dose divided by concentration, so 20 mg / 25 mg/mL = 0.8 mL. Inverting the fraction gives 1.25 mL, and the other volumes would deliver half the dose (0.4 mL), about 1.6 times the dose (1.25 mL) or 2.5 times the dose (2 mL).
  39. The correct answer is B (call 911 or go to an emergency department for observation, since symptoms can return later): Anaphylaxis can recur hours after apparent recovery as a biphasic reaction, and the effect of epinephrine wears off within minutes. Every use of an auto-injector therefore requires emergency evaluation and a period of observation. Home monitoring and a routine appointment leave her without access to treatment if airway symptoms return.
  40. The correct answer is D (Increase to 125 mcg daily and recheck the TSH in about four weeks): Thyroxine requirements rise early in pregnancy, and a 25 to 30 percent increase on 100 mcg gives 125 to 130 mcg, so 125 mcg fits. TSH is rechecked about every four weeks until stable. A 50 percent rise overshoots, and reducing the dose is wrong because the fetus depends on maternal hormone through the first trimester.
  41. The correct answer is C (Case-control designs sample by outcome, so incidence and relative risk cannot be calculated directly): In a case-control study participants are selected by outcome status, so the investigators fix the ratio of cases to controls and cannot estimate incidence or risk. The odds ratio is the valid measure of association in this design. Sample size, lack of randomization, and outcome type do not decide it.
  42. The correct answer is A (Alert the prescriber and arrange an INR check within 3 to 5 days of starting): Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole inhibits warfarin metabolism and can raise the INR substantially within days, increasing bleeding risk. Early INR monitoring, with a dose adjustment if needed, is the standard response. Holding warfarin leaves him unprotected, and increasing the dose would worsen the interaction.
  43. The correct answer is B (Carbamazepine induces liver enzymes and can lower pill effectiveness, so an IUD is preferred): Carbamazepine is a strong CYP3A4 inducer that lowers ethinyl estradiol and progestin levels, so combined pills, patches, rings, and some progestin-only pills can fail. Copper or levonorgestrel IUDs and depot medroxyprogesterone are not affected in this way. A temporary barrier method or a larger pill dose does not reliably fix the problem.
  44. The correct answer is A (15 mg once daily with the evening meal, since his clearance is about 45 mL/min): Cockcroft-Gault gives (140 minus 79) x 72 x 1.23 / 120, which is about 45 mL/min. Rivaroxaban 20 mg is reduced to 15 mg once daily when clearance is 15 to 50 mL/min, and both of these doses are taken with the evening meal for absorption. Age alone does not halve the dose in atrial fibrillation.
  45. The correct answer is A (Take each dose 30 minutes before meals, and spread protein intake across the day): Large neutral amino acids from dietary protein compete with levodopa for absorption and for transport across the blood-brain barrier. Taking levodopa about 30 minutes before meals, on an empty stomach, improves delivery, and spreading protein through the day softens the effect. Doubling doses or moving all tablets to bedtime would leave daytime symptoms uncontrolled.
  46. The correct answer is B (Report the gaps honestly to the auditor and refuse to recreate or backdate any records): Fabricating or backdating records to satisfy an audit is itself fraud and misconduct, and it would convert a documentation gap into a serious offence. Honest disclosure lets the auditor decide how to treat the six claims, and the pharmacist can investigate how the records were lost. Withdrawing claims or delaying disclosure conceals the problem.
  47. The correct answer is A (It exceeds the 130/80 mmHg threshold, so confirmation and treatment review are needed): The 2025 Hypertension Canada guideline uses 130/80 mmHg as the diagnostic threshold in adults, including those with diabetes, and 134/82 mmHg is above it on a properly measured office average. The general 140/90 mmHg target does not apply to him. Waiting a year leaves raised pressure untreated in a patient already at high vascular risk.
  48. The correct answer is D (Morning prednisone raises glucose mainly in the afternoon and evening, so test before dinner): Intermediate-acting prednisone given in the morning peaks in effect about four to eight hours later, so glucose climbs after lunch and into the evening while the fasting value may stay near normal. Monitoring only fasting glucose misses the pattern. She should test before dinner or two hours after lunch and discuss treatment changes with her prescriber.
  49. The correct answer is A (Six tablets, because the stated 1200 mg daily maximum caps the total): The range of two to three tablets every six hours could total up to 12 tablets (2400 mg), but the prescriber added a maximum of 1200 mg in 24 hours, which equals six 200 mg tablets. The explicit daily maximum governs, and it should be printed clearly on the label. Counting four doses of two or three tablets ignores that cap.
  50. The correct answer is A (Proceed with vaccination, since egg allergy is not a contraindication to influenza vaccine): NACI states that egg allergy, including past anaphylaxis to egg, is not a contraindication to influenza vaccines, and no special precautions beyond usual post-vaccination observation are needed. Allergist referral and extended hospital observation are unnecessary. Whole-virus products are not safer and are not used for this reason.
  51. The correct answer is D (Every participant takes identical looking tablets on the same schedule, so blinding is maintained): When two active treatments differ in form or schedule, each arm receives the active drug for its own regimen plus a placebo matching the other regimen. Everyone then takes the same number of tablets at the same times, so neither participants nor staff can tell the groups apart.
  52. The correct answer is A (Treat with cephalexin 500 mg four times daily for 7 days, then repeat a culture): Asymptomatic bacteriuria is treated in pregnancy because it raises the risk of pyelonephritis and preterm birth. Cephalexin is a safe, effective agent, and a follow-up culture confirms cure. Trimethoprim is a folate antagonist and is avoided in the first trimester, and fluoroquinolones are avoided in pregnancy.
  53. The correct answer is A (See her neurologist now to plan a switch from valproate and start folic acid): Valproate carries a high risk of major malformations and neurodevelopmental effects, so women who might become pregnant should be moved to a safer drug before conception, with folic acid started early. Stopping abruptly risks seizures harmful to mother and fetus, and continuing unchanged or waiting for a positive test exposes the embryo during organ formation.
  54. The correct answer is D (Check strength by label and imprint, reassure her, and advise an INR within two weeks): Warfarin is a critical dose drug, so even with an interchangeable product a prudent INR check soon after a manufacturer change is reasonable. Confirming the strength by imprint settles the family's worry. Stopping doses risks clot formation, and the colour of a tablet is not a reason to change the number of tablets taken.
  55. The correct answer is D (Amoxicillin is reasonable, since symptoms worsened after initial improvement, suggesting bacterial sinusitis): Worsening after initial improvement (double worsening) with fever and pain points to secondary bacterial sinusitis, for which amoxicillin is first-line. Purulent discharge alone does not separate viral from bacterial disease, but this pattern does. Fluoroquinolones are not first-line and waiting 21 days is far beyond usual practice.
  56. The correct answer is A (About 3.9, calculated as (30 x 180) divided by (70 x 20)): Cases had 30 exposed and 70 unexposed, controls had 20 exposed and 180 unexposed. The odds ratio is the cross-product 5400 divided by 1400, about 3.9. Dividing proportions gives a risk-style ratio that a case-control design cannot validly estimate, and inverting the products reverses the direction of association.
  57. The correct answer is A (Advise against using it because it is unauthorized, and offer to report the source): A product without a Drug Identification Number has not been authorized by Health Canada, so its quality, strength and source cannot be verified, which matters greatly for a narrow therapeutic index drug such as warfarin. Matching tablet markings or a manufacturer certificate does not replace regulatory review. Reporting the source helps Health Canada act on unauthorized products.
  58. The correct answer is C (HLA-B*58:01 testing can be considered, since carriers face higher risk of severe skin reactions to allopurinol): The HLA-B*58:01 allele is strongly associated with allopurinol induced Stevens-Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis and DRESS, with higher prevalence in Han Chinese, Thai and Korean populations. Screening is reasonable in these groups. HLA-B*15:02 relates to carbamazepine, and G6PD testing is a rasburicase or dapsone concern.
  59. The correct answer is A (Contact her prescriber promptly about switching to labetalol or nifedipine, which suit pregnancy): ACE inhibitors are fetotoxic and should be replaced as soon as pregnancy is recognized, ideally with labetalol, nifedipine or methyldopa. Leaving blood pressure untreated carries maternal and fetal risk, so abrupt cessation without a substitute is poor care. Halving the dose does not make fetal exposure safe.
  60. The correct answer is D (Drops per minute equal mL per hour here, so run 40 drops per minute): With 60 drops per mL, the rate in drops per minute is mL per hour x 60 drops divided by 60 minutes, so the two numbers are identical. An order of 40 mL per hour is 40 drops per minute. The factor of 60 cancels only for microdrip sets, whereas a 15 or 20 drop set requires a calculation.
  61. The correct answer is C (Explain each step first, ask permission, and let her pause or stop the check): Trauma-informed care emphasizes safety, trust, autonomy and control. Explaining steps and asking permission before touch gives her predictability and agency. Rushing removes control, asking for details of the trauma is unnecessary and may retraumatize, and refusing the service penalizes her for her reaction.
  62. The correct answer is A (200 capsules, which equals two capsules daily for 100 days, and the prescriber is notified): The daily use is two capsules, so 100 days requires 2 x 100 = 200 capsules. The original 60 capsules were a 30 day supply, so quadrupling it gives 240, which is a different number of days. The pharmacist documents the adapted quantity and notifies the prescriber.
  63. The correct answer is D (Treat with 15 g carbohydrate, avoid driving for 40 minutes, and recheck before starting): Diabetes Canada advises that a driver with a glucose below 4.0 mmol/L treats the low and does not drive until about 40 minutes later, after rechecking. Feeling well does not confirm that cognition has recovered. Skipping a tablet or driving after only 15 minutes does not allow enough time for brain function to return to normal.
  64. The correct answer is D (Confounding by indication likely explains it, so the drug is not shown to cause exacerbations): Prescribers chose the drug for sicker patients, so disease severity is linked to both receiving the drug and having exacerbations. That is confounding by indication, which can make a helpful drug look harmful. Careful adjustment or a randomized comparison would be needed to judge its effect.
  65. The correct answer is B (Hyperkalemia, as trimethoprim blocks distal sodium channels and adds to ACE inhibitor and spironolactone effects): Trimethoprim acts like amiloride on the distal nephron, reducing potassium secretion. Added to an ACE inhibitor and an aldosterone antagonist it can cause serious hyperkalemia, especially in older adults. Potassium should be rechecked within a few days or a different antibiotic considered.
  66. The correct answer is D (Polyethylene glycol 3350, since it is not absorbed and does not accumulate in kidney impairment): Magnesium and phosphate containing laxatives and enemas are renally cleared and can cause dangerous hypermagnesemia or hyperphosphatemia in CKD. Aluminum accumulates in kidney failure and is not a suitable laxative. Polyethylene glycol is a non absorbed osmotic agent without electrolyte load and is the safer product.
  67. The correct answer is A (Give 9 mL (180 mg) every 6 to 8 hours as needed with food): At 10 mg/kg, her dose is 180 mg, which is 9 mL of a 100 mg/5 mL suspension. Doses are spaced 6 to 8 hours apart so the daily total stays under 40 mg/kg. Doubling or halving gives a toxic or ineffective dose, and every 4 hours would exceed the daily limit.
  68. The correct answer is B (Write down the order and confirm the caller is a practitioner authorized to prescribe narcotics): Narcotics may be prescribed verbally, but the pharmacist must reduce the order to writing and be satisfied that the caller is a practitioner authorized to prescribe the drug. A verbal order is not limited to emergencies, and the regulations do not require a witness or a follow-up written prescription.
  69. The correct answer is B (Use a qualified telephone interpreter and apply teach-back to check understanding of dose and bleeding signs): Qualified interpreter services combined with teach-back give the most reliable confirmation that the patient understands a high-risk drug such as an anticoagulant. Nodding or having no questions does not show comprehension. Written English material and untrained ad hoc interpreters risk errors and may breach privacy.
  70. The correct answer is D (Switch to an oral agent and plan a total duration of about five days if stable): He meets criteria for IV to oral switch: afebrile, hemodynamically stable, tolerating oral intake and well oxygenated. Five days of total therapy is adequate for most patients who reach clinical stability. Radiographic resolution lags weeks behind clinical recovery and should not guide duration.
  71. The correct answer is D (14 tablets, which covers the 14 days between running out and the sync date): FA has 30 - 24 = 6 tablets left, enough for 6 days. The sync date is 20 days away, so he needs 20 - 6 = 14 more tablets. Dispensing 20 would ignore his remaining supply, and 6 is the leftover, not the shortfall.
  72. The correct answer is A (Start the pill today and use condoms or abstain for the first seven days): A combined pill can be started on any day if pregnancy is reasonably excluded, which is called quick start. Starting after day 5 of the cycle means ovulation may not yet be suppressed, so backup contraception is needed for the first seven days. Waiting for a period is unnecessary delay.
  73. The correct answer is D (Antipsychotics raise stroke and death risk in dementia, so use the lowest dose briefly): Antipsychotics carry a boxed warning for increased mortality and stroke in older adults with dementia, including atypical agents such as risperidone, so non-drug strategies come first and any drug is used at the lowest dose for the shortest time with regular reassessment. Risks are not limited to older agents, and addiction is not the concern.
  74. The correct answer is D (Shoe covers, hair and facial covers, mask, hand hygiene, then gown, followed by sterile gloves last): Garbing moves from the dirtiest items to the cleanest: shoe and hair covers and a mask first, then hand hygiene, then the gown, and sterile gloves at the end so that they are not contaminated while dressing. Washing hands after gloving or dressing in the buffer area defeats the purpose of controlling contamination at the entry point.
  75. The correct answer is C (Wait until moderate withdrawal appears, because early dosing precipitates withdrawal, then use a low dose): Buprenorphine binds opioid receptors tightly but is only a partial agonist, so giving it while full agonists still occupy receptors can displace them and precipitate withdrawal. Standard induction waits for moderate withdrawal and begins with a low dose. A fixed 72 hour wait is not required, and a larger initial dose raises the risk.
